NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Oveporexton is a selective oral orexin receptor 2 (OX2R) agonist proposed for the treatment
(b) (4)
of narcolepsy type 1 (NT1; narcolepsy with cataplexy) in patients
. Of note,
NT1 is an orphan indication. Oveporexton would be available as 0.5 mg, 1 mg, and 2 mg oral
tablets. The proposed dosing regimen is 1 mg or 2 mg twice daily, with the first dose taken
upon awakening and the second dose taken 3 to 5 hours later. Oveporexton can be taken
with or without food. Oveporexton is a new molecular entity (NME) that has not been
marketed anywhere in the world and would be first in its pharmacological class approved for
the proposed indication. The proposed proprietary name for oveporexton in this application
is Orzeyful.
The NDA includes results from Studies TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002, two randomized,
double-blind, placebo-controlled efficacy and safety studies in participants 16 years of age or
older with NT1, and from the ongoing long-term extension (LTE) Study TAK-861-2003. The
application also includes data from phase 1 pharmacokinetic (PK) studies, phase 2 dosefinding studies in NT1 and NT2, and updated safety information from the 90-day safety
update in the LTE.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The Applicant provided substantial evidence of effectiveness for oveporexton as a treatment
for NT1. The application included data from two adequate and well-controlled efficacy and
safety studies, TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002, both randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled trials conducted in adults with NT1. Both studies yielded highly statistically
significant results on the primary efficacy endpoint—change from Baseline to Week 12 in
mean sleep latency on the Maintenance of Wakefulness Test (MWT)—as well as on all
secondary endpoints included in the pre-specified multiple testing procedure. The magnitude
of effect was large across both studies, and the results were robust to sensitivity analyses
addressing missing data and modeling assumptions.
The Applicant sought an indication for NT1 as a whole, rather than a symptom-specific
indication such as excessive daytime sleepiness (EDS) or cataplexy in narcolepsy. This
approach required demonstration of a meaningful treatment effect across all five core
components of the NT1 symptom pentad: EDS, cataplexy, hypnagogic/hypnopompic
hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and disturbed/disrupted nighttime sleep (DNS). The review
team assessed the evidence from both studies and recommends approval for NT1.
Across both pivotal studies, oveporexton demonstrated robust, statistically significant, and
clinically meaningful improvements on objective and subjective measures of EDS, including
the MWT and the Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS). A statistically significant and clinically
meaningful reduction in weekly cataplexy rate was observed in both studies, with a greater
than 60% reduction relative to placebo in Study TAK-861-3001 and a 75% reduction in Study
24

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
TAK-861-3002. Statistically significant reductions in the frequency and distress of hypnagogic
and hypnopompic hallucinations and sleep paralysis were demonstrated in both the overall
study populations and in symptomatic subgroups, with consistent and large effect sizes across
studies. With respect to DNS—the most complex domain given the wakefulness-promoting
mechanism of action of oveporexton—the evidence, including significant improvements in
polysomnography-measured REM latency (replicated across both studies with highly
consistent effect sizes of approximately 70 to 72 minutes versus placebo), subjective sleep
quality, and sleep-related quality of life measures, supports a meaningful treatment benefit
on this pentad domain. The numerical increase in objectively measured nocturnal awakenings
observed in both studies is consistent with the drug's pharmacology and does not, in the
judgment of the review team, detract from the overall conclusion of a beneficial effect on
nighttime sleep.
A concern is that the studies were mainly conducted outside of the United States; the North
American subgroup consisted of 33 subjects from the United States and Canada. This
subgroup did not reach nominal significance for the low-dose arm for the primary outcome
(excessive daytime sleepiness), though nominal significance was achieved for the high-dose
arm; overall, results were consistent with those observed in the rest of the world and, given
that objective data can be measured for NT1, the pathophysiology of the illness is likely to be
consistent across global populations, as are the drug’s effects. Nonetheless, the limited
representation of U.S. patients warrants consideration. Representation by race and ethnicity
also remains a concern. For example, Black Americans were minimally represented across the
entire development program, with only seven participants enrolled. While narcolepsy is
known to affect all ethnic groups, relatively little is understood regarding phenotypic
expression and prevalence of the disorder across the diverse ethnic populations of the United
States. The generalizability of these findings to underrepresented groups therefore cannot be
fully established. Finally, the age subgroup of patients older than 65 years was not adequately
investigated, as nearly all participants enrolled in the studies were between 18 and 65 years
of age. The safety and efficacy of the drug in older adults therefore remains an open question
and a residual area of uncertainty.
Notwithstanding the aforementioned limitations, taken together, the efficacy data from
Studies TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002 constitute substantial evidence of effectiveness for
oveporexton as a treatment for NT1. The results are highly consistent across two
independent, adequate and well-controlled trials, with comparable or numerically larger
effect sizes in Study TAK-861-3002 relative to Study TAK-861-3001, and provide compelling
evidence of a treatment effect across all five components of the NT1 symptom pentad. The
review team considers this evidence supportive of the Applicant's approach to the NT1
indication.
(b) (4)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

Benefit-Risk Assessment
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
The Applicant, Takeda Pharmaceuticals, has submitted NDA 220860 for the approval of TAK-861, also known as oveporexton, with the
proposed trade name of Orzeyful, for the treatment of narcolepsy type 1 (NT1; narcolepsy with cataplexy, an orphan indication) in patients
(b) (4)
. Oveporexton is a new molecular entity (NME) and a selective oral orexin receptor 2 (OX2R) agonist that would be
the first in its pharmacological class approved for the treatment of narcolepsy.
The Applicant submitted data from two phase 3 randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 12-week efficacy and safety studies, TAK861-3001 (N=168) and TAK-861-3002 (N=105), conducted at multiple global sites. Against a placebo arm, Study 3001 tested two dosages
(1 mg twice daily and 2 mg twice daily) and Study 3002 tested 2 mg twice daily in patients with NT1 in patients 16 to 65 years of age. On the
primary efficacy endpoint of change from Baseline at Week 12 in mean sleep latency on the Maintenance of Wakefulness Test (MWT) on
polysomnography (PSG), both study results were strongly statistically significant for the difference between drug versus placebo. These
results were generally consistent across subgroups of region, race, and sex. (In particular, the regional consistency on an objective endpoint
signifies likely mechanistic and pathophysiological consistency for the drug’s effects on NT1, so that the overall study results should apply to
the U.S. population.) Also, on the prespecified secondary and additional secondary endpoints, results were statistically or nominally
significant. The review team analyzed all of these endpoints to determine their viability in representing clinically meaningful results for the
pentad of symptoms known to contribute to NT1. We determined that the evidence supports the conclusion that oveporexton has
demonstrated effectiveness and clinically meaningful benefit for the treatment of the whole syndrome of NT1. Additional support was
provided by the adequate and well-controlled phase 2 Study TAK-861-2001, whose positive efficacy results were consistent with the phase 3
findings across both studies.
The safety profile of oveporexton was consistent across the two placebo-controlled studies as well as the entirety of the safety study
database, including a long-term open-label extension (LTE) study. The most common adverse events (AEs) include insomnia (56%),
pollakiuria (57%), micturition urgency (16%), and salivary hypersecretion (7%). These events are consistent with the expected on-target
pharmacological effects of orexin receptor agonism, occurred predominantly within the first 2 days of treatment, and were generally mildto-moderate and self-limited. Only one death occurred in the development program, during the LTE study, a myocardial infarction in a
subject with pre-existing cardiovascular risks that were the likely primary contributing factors. Serious AEs were infrequent (1%) and were
not clearly related to the study drug’s effects. No clear trends with vital signs (including blood pressure and weight) or liver function or renal
function tests (sometimes seen in other drugs in this class) or other safety factors were noted; no major psychiatric or suicidal
ideation/behavior signals were observed. Some cases of elevated CPK on laboratory testing were observed, as well as mild mean increases in
LDL cholesterol versus placebo in the 12-week studies and in the LTE study. The major detected AEs and safety issues will be addressed by
27

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
labeling, and the drug will remain under ongoing routine postmarket pharmacovigilance. Our Controlled Substances Staff Team did not
detect any major abuse potential signals in the studies and recommends Schedule IV for oveporexton.
Overall, the benefits outweigh the risks for oveporexton for the proposed dosages and treatment paradigm for patients with NT1. The
strongly positive efficacy results across the entire NT1 symptom pentad, the manageable and well-tolerated safety profile, and the novel
mechanism of action with decreased abuse potential risks relative to some existing drugs for this condition provide an important new
therapeutic option for this serious condition. Our review team recommends approval for oveporexton, as the first drug to be available in the
United States for the treatment of the entire syndrome of NT1.

Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
•
•

Analysis of
Condition

•

•

Narcolepsy type 1 (NT1) is a rare, chronic, and serious disease. The
prevailing hypothesis is that NT1 is caused by a loss of orexinproducing neurons resulting in orexin deficiency.
Its prevalence is estimated at approximately 25 to 50 per 100,000
.
individuals, with no significant
predominance, though there is a
strong association with the human leukocyte antigen (HLA)-DQB1*06:02
allele, which is present in approximately 90 to 95% of patients with
cataplexy.
It is characterized by a classic pentad of symptoms that include excessive
daytime sleepiness (EDS), cataplexy, sleep paralysis, hypnagogic and/or
hypnopompic hallucinations, and disrupted nighttime sleep (DNS), with the
latter three representing intrusions of rapid eye movement (REM) sleep
phenomena into wakefulness or sleep-wake transitions.
The International Classification of Sleep Disorders–3 (ICSD-3) diagnostic
criteria for NT1 require 1) symptoms of EDS for ≥3 months and 2) threshold
findings on the multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) or a sleep onset REM
periods (SOREMP) on nocturnal polysomnogram associated with the
presence of cataplexy or/and 3) low (i.e., below threshold) cerebrospinal
fluid (CSF) hypocretin-1 concentration.
28

Reference ID: 5847402

Conclusions and Reasons
NT1 is a serious and chronic condition
characterized by EDS, cataplexy, and
other symptoms that have a significant
impact on patients' quality of life and
ability to function in multiple spheres.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
•
•

•

Current
Treatment
Options

•

•

Symptoms typically start in adolescence, with a small second peak of onset
at around age 35 years. In 10 to 15% of patients, narcolepsy starts before the
age of 10 years.
The core symptoms of NT1 have a significant impact on patients’ quality of
life, increasing the risk of injury, accidents, and falls, and impacting in daily
functioning.
FDA approved treatments for excessive daytime sleepiness include
amphetamines, methylphenidates, modafinil, armodafinil, and
solriamfetol. These treatments carry risks of abuse and cardiovascular
adverse events. Stevens-Johnson Syndrome, a life-threatening
dermatologic condition, has been associated with modafinil and
armodafinil.
Sodium oxybate is approved for the treatment of both EDS and cataplexy.
Risks associated with sodium oxybate include respiratory depression,
central nervous system depression, seizure, and abuse (Schedule III drug).
Sodium oxybate is only available through a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation
Strategy (REMS) program
Pitolisant is approved for the treatment of both EDS and cataplexy. Risk

The safety database for oveporexton included 770 unique participants
exposed to oveporexton across the clinical development program. At the time
of the 90-day safety update, 358 participants had been exposed for at least 6
months and 148 for at least 1 year, meeting ICH E1 recommendations for both
durations. The safety review focused on the pivotal trial pool (Studies TAK861-3001 and TAK-861-3002), which included only the to-be-marketed doses
and a placebo comparator.
No deaths occurred in the placebo-controlled studies. One death (cardiac
arrest) occurred in the long-term extension study in a participant with
multiple pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors; a causal relationship with
oveporexton cannot be definitively established or excluded, though the
background cardiovascular risk factors may have been the primary
contributors.
Serious adverse events were uncommon in the development program. In the
pivotal trial pool, the SAE incidence was 1.0% in the oveporexton group versus
0% in placebo, with observed events having clear alternative etiologies

compared to placebo were insomnia (56% vs. 1%), pollakiuria (57% vs. 5%),

predominantly within the first two days of treatment. Their frequency
decreased during long-term exposure in the extension study, suggesting
potential tolerance over time.

most cases.

during the LTE study. No cases met Hy's Law criteria for drug-induced liver

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
Narcolepsy is a chronic sleep disorder characterized by excessive daytime sleepiness (EDS)
that interferes with functioning and is typically accompanied by at least some associated
symptoms including cataplexy, sleep paralysis, hypnogogic or hypnopompic hallucinations,
and disturbed/disrupted nighttime sleep (DNS). Cataplexy, sudden loss of voluntary muscle
tone with a preserved state of consciousness, can be triggered by strong positive or negative
emotions, occurs in most patients with narcolepsy, and is frequently debilitating. Classic or
typical narcolepsy, now termed narcolepsy type 1 (NT1), is characterized by the presence of
cataplexy, and orexin deficiency, i.e., loss of hypocretin (orexin)-producing neurons in the
hypothalamus, leading to dysregulation of sleep-wake cycles. By contrast, narcolepsy
without either cataplexy or orexin deficiency—now termed narcolepsy type 2 (NT2)—
remains poorly understood and represents a distinct clinical entity with likely different
pathophysiology.
This application focuses on the treatment of NT1. NT1 is a rare, lifelong disease without an
identified cure. The classic pentad of NT1 symptoms includes EDS, cataplexy, sleep paralysis,
hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations, and DNS, with the latter three representing
intrusions of rapid eye movement (REM) sleep phenomena into wakefulness or sleep-wake
transitions.
EDS is usually the initial and often the most disabling symptom, experienced as repeated daily
episodes of an irrepressible need to sleep or lapses into sleep. "Sleep attacks" can occur in
unusual situations such as eating or walking, often on a background of overall sleepiness. EDS
can manifest as lapses in vigilance or inattentiveness. Young children may also manifest
sleepiness as excessively long night sleep or resumption of previously discontinued naps
(AASM 2014). According to the 2014 report from a September 24, 2013, FDA Patient-Focused
Drug Development Voice of the Patient meeting to hear perspectives from people living with
narcolepsy about their disease, treatment, and impact on their daily life, patients “stressed
that the effects of EDS go far beyond feeling tired or falling asleep during the day. Because of
EDS, they constantly battle 'brain fog' and other cognitive impairments, automatic behaviors
[performing actions such as walking, eating, or driving, in a semiconscious way without
awareness], and chronic sleeping deprivation.” (The Voice of the Patient, Patient-Focused
Drug Development Public Meeting: September 24, 2013, Center for Drug Evaluation and
Research, FDA 2014)
Cataplexy, defined as >1 episode of generally brief (a matter of seconds to <2 minutes)
sudden loss of muscle tone with retained consciousness, is usually bilaterally symmetrical and
can be precipitated by strong emotions (usually positive). Cataplexy usually occurs within 1
year of onset but can rarely be the initial or a long-delayed symptom. The presentation and
frequency are variable (from subtle partial attacks to complete attacks, from <1/month to
>20/day). Presentation may include manifestations such as neck weakness with head drop,
facial weakness with dysarthria, peripheral weakness with collapse, and phenomena such as
37
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
muscle twitching or small jerks. According to the 2014 Voice of the Patient report, "for those
who experience cataplexy, hallucinations or sleep paralysis, the uncontrollable and often
unpredictable loss of control can be terrifying." One web participant described cataplexy "as
feeling like a puppet where all the puppet strings have been released, without warning"
(Voice of Patient Report, Center for Drug Evaluation and Research, FDA 2014).
Associated features may include disrupted nocturnal sleep, hypnagogic or hypnopompic
hallucinations (vivid, often frightening dreamlike experiences when falling asleep or
awakening, respectively), and sleep paralysis (temporary inability to move voluntary muscles
when falling asleep or awakening).
Additional associated features include automatic behaviors (performing routine tasks without
conscious awareness during microsleep episodes), cognitive difficulties including problems
with attention and memory. Common comorbidities include obesity, depression, anxiety
disorders, obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), and cardiovascular disease, with some studies
suggesting increased rates of type 2 diabetes.
According to the International Classification of Sleep Disorders, Third Edition (ICSD-3), the
diagnostic criteria for NT1 require daily periods of irrepressible need to sleep or daytime
lapses into sleep occurring for at least 3 months, plus the presence of either cataplexy and a
mean sleep latency of ≤8 minutes with two or more sleep-onset REM periods (SOREMPs) on
multiple sleep latency testing (MSLT), or cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) orexin/hypocretin-1
concentration ≤110 pg/mL or less than one-third of mean values in normal participants. The
Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders, Fifth Edition (DSM-5) criteria are
similar, requiring a period of at least 3 months with daily episodes of an irrepressible need to
sleep, along with the presence of cataplexy occurring at least a few times per month, plus
either orexin/hypocretin deficiency (as measured by CSF levels ≤110 pg/mL or <1/3 of normal
control values) or positive MSLT findings showing a mean sleep latency ≤8 minutes and ≥2
SOREMPs (with one SOREMP potentially occurring during the preceding nocturnal
polysomnography).
NT1 usually starts in adolescence, and a small second peak of onset occurs at around age 35
years. In 10 to 15% of patients, narcolepsy starts before the age of 10 years. However, NT1 in
children was only rarely systematically studied before the current century. NT1 can have an
acute course, in which symptoms develop within a few days or weeks after a triggering event,
such as vaccination, stress or head trauma; a chronic course, in which the onset of symptoms
is difficult to determine; or a progressive course, in which the onset of different symptoms is
separated by years or even decades. In a series of 1,099 patients with narcolepsy, cataplexy
most often developed at the same time as EDS in 49% of the cohort and developed after EDS
in another 43%. Cataplexy preceded EDS in only 8% of the patients. The interval between
onset of EDS and onset of cataplexy is usually <2 to 3 years but can exceed 40 to 50 years.
Narcolepsy with cataplexy typically does not remit (remission of this presentation has been
reported only once, in a patient who received immunotherapy soon after disease onset). In
addition, narcoleptic symptoms often improve over time; the severity of EDS and cataplexy
typically decreases with age, possibly as a result of coping mechanisms (reviewed in Bassetti
CLA, et al., 2019).
38
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The prevalence of NT1 is estimated at approximately 25 to 50 per 100,000 individuals, with
.
no significant
predominance, though there is a strong association with the human
leukocyte antigen (HLA)-DQB1*06:02 allele, which is present in approximately 90 to 95% of
patients with cataplexy (Bassetti CLA, et al., 2019). However, because of the uncertainties
concerning the narcoleptic diagnoses (particularly NT2, as previously mentioned), the exact
incidence of NT1 remains unknown. A recent study, based on a large representative sample of
the U.S. general population, reports a current prevalence of NT1 at 12.6/100,000 individuals,
with prevalence similar between men and women, and not significant difference between age
groups, although it was slightly higher in individuals younger than 35 years old (Ohayon MM
et al., 2023).
The impact on quality of life (QOL) is substantial and multifaceted, affecting academic and
occupational performance, social relationships, driving safety, and overall psychological wellbeing, with many patients experiencing stigmatization, reduced employment opportunities,
and increased risk of accidents due to sudden sleep attacks or cataplexy episodes. According
to the 2022 FDA Patient-Led Listening Session summary report, a patient whose NT1
symptoms began at age 12 (preceding his diagnosis by 13 years) stated that he "fears walking
down stairs due to his cataplexy. He has broken ribs, his leg, and incurred concussions from
falling." The mother of a child with NT1 reported that "if the school feels he is at risk of injury,
they will want him to have a homebound placement, which means he would be excluded
from the social interaction school provides" (Project Sleep 2022).
In summary, NT1 is a serious and chronic condition characterized by EDS, cataplexy, and other
symptoms that have a significant impact on patients' quality of life and ability to function in
multiple spheres.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
To date, there is no approved treatment specifically indicated for NT1. Available treatments
focus on managing individual symptoms associated with narcolepsy broadly, such as excessive
daytime sleepiness or cataplexy, without distinction between NT1 and NT2. Importantly, none
of the currently available therapies address the underlying pathophysiology of NT1, namely
the loss of hypocretin (orexin)-producing neurons in the hypothalamus and the resulting
orexin deficiency. Instead, existing treatments which provide only symptomatic relief of one
or two symptoms.
Drug treatments approved to treat both EDS and cataplexy associated with narcolepsy
include: sodium oxybate (Xyrem) and its extended-release formulation (Lumryz), mixed salts
of oxybate (Xywav), and pitolisant (Wakix). Drug treatments approved to treat EDS only
include: modafinil (Provigil), armodafinil (Nuvigil), and solriamfetol (Sunosi). Older drugs that
have an approved indication for the treatment of patients with narcolepsy broadly, without
distinction between NT1 or NT2, include: amphetamine, dextroamphetamine, and
methylphenidate (Ritalin, Methylin). Treatment effect data are not labeled for these
stimulant drugs, but they are expected to be effective only for EDS and not for cataplexy.

39
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Antidepressants, such as selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, serotonin norepinephrine
reuptake inhibitors, and tricyclic antidepressants, are used off-label for the treatment of
cataplexy. Non-pharmacologic interventions that are used to manage narcolepsy symptoms
include brief regularly scheduled naps, consistent sleep schedule, avoidance of caffeine,
alcohol, and heavy meals before bedtime, relaxation before bedtime, regular exercise, and
taking safety precautions while driving or operating heavy machinery.
The currently available options for treating EDS or cataplexy in narcolepsy have several
significant limitations. Stimulants such as amphetamines and methylphenidate have wakepromoting effects, but patients often develop tolerance to these effects over time.
Amphetamines and methylphenidate have high abuse potential and are Schedule II controlled
substances. Sodium oxybate is the sodium salt of gamma-hydroxybutyric acid (GHB), which,
because of its effects, has been misused to commit drug-facilitated sexual assault. It is a
Schedule III controlled substance because of its abuse potential, but illicit use is subject to
Schedule I penalties. It is available in the United States only through a risk evaluation and
mitigation strategy (REMS) program. Modafinil and armodafinil have lower abuse potential
and are Schedule IV controlled substances. However, for many patients they do not
adequately promote wakefulness throughout the day. In addition, modafinil and armodafinil
are substrates, inducers, and inhibitors of CYP450 isoenzymes, raising the possibility of
interactions with medications that patients may be prescribed for other illnesses. The labeling
for modafinil and armodafinil both include warnings for Stevens-Johnson Syndrome,
angioedema, anaphylactoid reactions, and multi-organ hypersensitivity reactions. Aside from
pitolisant (a histamine-3 [H3] receptor antagonist/inverse agonist), drugs approved to treat
narcolepsy are classified as Schedule II, III, or IV due to their abuse potential. Pitolisant, while
not a controlled substance, carries warnings and precautions for increases in QT interval.
The significant impact of NT1 on patients’ lives, combined with the limitations of available
treatments, underscores the critical need for additional therapeutic options. Most
importantly, there is currently no drug that addresses the associated underlying
pathophysiology of NT1 by restoring orexin function or replacing the lost orexin/hypocretinproducing neurons in the hypothalamus. A therapy that directly targets this fundamental
deficit represents a new target in the treatment landscape for NT1.

40
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. and International Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
Oveporexton was approved in China on July 22, 2026, and Japan on August 3, 2026, as the
first drug in its class. It but has not yet been approved or marketed anywhere else in the
world.

Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
On July 16, 2021, the Applicant, Takeda Pharmaceuticals USA, Inc., submitted a new
investigational new drug (IND) application and fast track designation request for oveporexton
for the treatment of narcolepsy. IND 154232 was allowed to proceed on August 25, 2021. On
August 25, 2021, oveporexton was also granted fast track designation for the treatment of
narcolepsy based on its proposed mechanism of action which may restore OX2R signaling at
the postsynaptic receptors addressing the loss of orexin peptide found in narcolepsy.
On January 20, 2022, the Applicant submitted a request for a special protocol assessment
(SPA) for a 24-month carcinogenicity study in rats. On March 3, 2022, the executive
Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee (eCAC) reached agreement on the SPA and
recommended doses of 0, 1, 20, and 100 mg/kg/day for male and female rats. On May 3,

carcinogenicity study in TG-RASH2 mice. On June 16, 2022, the eCAC reached agreement on
the SPA and recommended doses of 0, 3, 30, and 300 mg/kg/day in female mice. For male
mice, the eCAC recommended doses of 20, 60, and 600 mg/kg/day.

efficacy data from the phase 1 study submitted in the request suggested an improvement
over available therapies, but comparisons were difficult due to limited interpretability from

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Oveporexton is an orally available, brain-penetrant OX2R agonist developed for the treatment
of NT1. Orexins are neuropeptides that play a role in maintaining wakefulness and sleep
(Abad and Guilleminault, 2017). A precursor peptide is cleaved to form two neuropeptide
neurotransmitters, orexin-A and orexin-B, that bind to two different subtypes of G-protein
coupled receptors, orexin type-1 receptor (OX1R) and OX2R. Both subtypes of orexin
receptors are reported to contribute to the regulation of arousal; OX2R plays a role in the
maintenance of wakefulness, whereas both OX1R and OX2R are involved in the inhibition of
REM sleep. An association between the development of NT1 and loss of orexin-producing
neurons in the brain has been reported in published literature (Tsujino and Sakurai, 2013).
In vivo pharmacology data demonstrated significant wake-promoting effects through OX2R
activity and improvements in cataplexy-like symptoms in a NT1 mouse model. Although
oveporexton showed off-target binding to cannabinoid receptor type 1 (CB-1) and
progesterone receptor B (PR-B) receptors, with functional antagonism at the CB-1 receptor
(functional activity was not assessed for the PR-B receptor), these effects are not expected to
result in clinically significant off-target effects in humans at the maximum recommended
human dosage (MRHD) of 4 mg/day (2 mg twice daily). Results from GLP-compliant safety
pharmacology studies indicated that oveporexton can affect the CNS (increased body
temperature at 20 and 100 mg/kg, decreased body temperature at 1000 mg/kg, and
hyperactivity at ≥20 mg/kg in rats), the cardiovascular system (increased blood pressure and
left ventricular pressure at ≥3 mg/kg in monkeys), and the respiratory system (non-dosedependent increased respiratory rate at 20 and 100 mg/kg and minute volume at 100 mg/kg
in rats). However, these effects are not expected to constitute a significant clinical risk, given
the large exposure margins (>100-fold) compared to clinical exposures at the MRHD.
The pharmacokinetic profile of oveporexton was studied in vitro and in vivo in mice, rats,
rabbits, and monkeys. Oveporexton was rapidly absorbed after a single oral dose in rats and
monkeys; absorption was non-dose proportional in rats but approximately dose proportional
in monkeys. The major circulating component in all animal species was unchanged
[14C]oveporexton, which accounted for 95.6% (mice), 92.5% (rats), and 75.5% (monkeys) of
the total radioactivity. [14C]oveporexton was widely distributed into tissues of pigmented and
albino rats with the greatest exposure observed in the adrenal cortex, adrenal gland, and
liver, with no evidence of binding to melanin-containing tissues. Oveporexton can be
transferred into the fetus via the placenta during gestation and is also secreted into milk in
lactating rats. Oveporexton is highly bound to plasma proteins in vitro in rat, monkey, and
human hepatocytes. The unbound fraction of oveporexton in plasma is similar in rats,
monkeys and humans (4.5%, 2.8%, and 3.8%, respectively). Oveporexton is metabolized via
multiple metabolic pathways in animals, including oxidation, glucuronidation, sulfation and
glutathione-related conjugation, resulting in both fluorinated and defluorinated metabolites.
There are no human-specific metabolites. As a result of oveporexton metabolism and
45
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
generation of defluorinated metabolites, free fluoride was detected in plasma of rats and
monkeys (higher in rats than in monkeys). In human hepatocytes, CYP enzymes contributed
100% to oveporexton metabolism with CYP3A4 as the predominant contributor (97.3%) and
CYP2C9 and 2D6 contributed much less (2.6 and 0.1%, respectively). The results from
excretion studies indicate that [14C]oveporexton-related substances are mainly excreted into
the feces via bile with very little excretion in urine after single-dose oral administration to
mice, rats, and monkeys.
Single-dose and repeat-dose general toxicity studies were performed in Sprague-Dawley rats
(up to 26-weeks duration) and in cynomolgus monkeys (up to 39-weeks duration). In rats, the
primary target organs of toxicity included incisors, kidneys, and liver. Drug-related effects on
the incisors were characterized by whitening and fractures of the teeth with histopathology
of degeneration of ameloblasts. This finding was indicative of tooth fluorosis caused by
increased fluoride concentrations which disrupts enamel formation. The tooth fluorosis is
considered a species-specific metabolism-related change for rodents. Oveporexton is a
fluoride-containing molecule and upon metabolism free fluorine is released and was detected
in plasma of both rats and monkeys, as measured in the 2-week and 4-week repeat-dose
toxicity studies. However, no fluoride-related toxicity was observed in any of the repeat-dose
toxicity studies in monkeys up to 39-weeks in duration. Additionally, no de-fluorinated
metabolites were detected in human plasma (Report TKD-BCS-01618-R1); therefore, the
tooth changes observed in rats are not considered clinically relevant to humans. Drug-related
findings were also observed in the kidneys of rats which included dilatation of kidney
proximal and distal tubules in females at doses ≥750 mg/kg/day which was associated with
increased blood urea nitrogen (BUN), creatinine, and urine volume. Drug-related findings in
the liver of rats included hypertrophy of centrilobular hepatocytes in both males and females
at doses ≥100 mg/kg, which correlated with increased liver weights. Decreases in body weight
that correlated with decreases in food consumption were also observed in longer duration
repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats. The effects on the kidneys, liver, and body weight were all
reversible upon drug cessation, while the effects observed in the incisors showed partial
reversibility upon drug cessation. The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) in the 26week general toxicity study in rats was 100 mg/kg/day for both males and females, which was
associated with a mean Cmax of 7,860 and 11,800 ng/mL, and a mean AUC24h of 115,000 and
168,000 h*ng/mL in males and females, respectively taken from Week 26 values. Safety
margins at the NOAEL compared to the MRHD are approximately 311- and 455-fold for males
and females, respectively based on AUC.
In monkeys, no adverse toxicity findings were observed in any parameter across all repeatdose toxicity studies at doses up to the highest tested dose of 300 mg/kg/day and up to 39weeks in duration. Non-adverse drug-related findings were limited to vomiting, transient
decreases in food consumption, increases in triglycerides, and microscopic findings of
minimal hepatocyte hypertrophy; and all findings were reversible upon drug cessation. The
NOAEL in the 39-week general toxicity study in monkeys was 300 mg/kg/day for both males
and females, which was associated with a mean Cmax of 14,500 and 15,500 ng/mL and a mean
AUC24h of 211,000 and 169,000 h*ng/mL in males and females, respectively on Day 273 of the

46
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
study. Safety margins at the NOAEL in monkeys compared to the MRHD are approximately
571- and 457-fold for males and females, respectively based on AUC.
Measurement of hepatic drug-metabolizing enzymes was conducted in both the rat and
monkey 4-week repeat-dose toxicity studies. In both rats and monkeys, oveporexton was
associated with an induction of several hepatic drug-metabolizing enzymes. Therefore, the
findings of hepatocyte hypertrophy in the livers of both rats and monkeys was considered to
be caused by hepatic drug-metabolizing enzyme induction, an adaptive and non-adverse
change.
A full battery of GLP developmental and reproductive toxicity studies were conducted with
oveporexton. In the fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in rats, decreased
number of corpora lutea associated with decreased numbers of implantations and live
embryos were observed at 750 mg/kg (the highest dose tested) compared to control. There
were no effects on male fertility at doses up to 750 mg/kg. The NOAEL for fertility and early
embryonic development was 100 mg/kg in females and 750 mg/kg in males (approximately
455- and 747-fold the MRHD, respectively, based on AUC). In embryo-fetal development (EFD)
studies in pregnant rats and rabbits, maternal toxicity (decreases in body weight and body
weight gain) was noted in both species at the highest doses tested (750 mg/kg/day and
100 mg/kg/day, in rats and rabbits respectively). In rats, decreased fetal weights and
decreased ossification of metatarsal bones were observed at 750 mg/kg/day compared to
control, secondary to the low maternal body weight. There were no drug-related effects on
the incidence of congenital malformations or embryo-fetal lethality in either rats or rabbits.
The NOAELs for maternal toxicity were 100 mg/kg in rats and 20 mg/kg in rabbits,
approximately 346- and 38-fold the MRHD, respectively, based on AUC; and the NOAELs for
effects on EFD were 750 mg/kg and 100 mg/kg in rats and rabbits, respectively,
approximately 1166- and 153-fold the MRHD, respectively, based on AUC. In the pre- and
postnatal development (PPND) study in rats, there were no drug-related effects on maternal
endpoints or reproductive function, or on offspring development (survival, growth, behavior,
and reproductive function). The NOAEL for effects on PPND was the highest tested dose of
10 mg/kg/day, approximately 118-fold the MRHD based on AUC. Oveporexton did not show
adverse effects in juvenile animal toxicity study (JAS) in rats dosed from the age of 28 days
through sexual maturity. The NOAEL for juvenile toxicity was the highest tested dose of
50 mg/kg in the 6-week JAS rat study. Based on the totality of nonclinical reproductive and
developmental toxicity data, oveporexton has a low risk of reproductive and developmental
toxicity in humans.
Oveporexton was found to be negative in an Ames assay and in an in vivo micronucleus assay;
however, it was found to be positive for induction of micronuclei in an in vitro micronucleus
assay following 24-hours of continuous treatment. Overall oveporexton was determined to be
non-genotoxic. Oveporexton was also found to be non-carcinogenic in a GLP 2-year rat and a
GLP 6-month transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study.
Note: Safety exposure margins are based on human population pharmacokinetic AUC24,ss at
the MRHD of 4 mg/day (2 mg BID) of 369.6 h*ng/mL in patients from clinical Study TAK-8613001.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs

Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
The primary pharmacology of oveporexton, was assessed in both in vitro and in vivo animal
studies and NT1 models.
In Vitro Studies
In vitro, the selectivity of oveporexton was established in CHO-K1 cells stably expressing
recombinant human OX1R or OX2R. Oveporexton showed agonist activity on human OX2R
and OX1R in vitro with an EC50 of 0.0025 μmol/L and 7.5 μmol/L, respectively, indicating that
it is a potent OX2R agonist with a 3000-fold selectivity for OX2R over OX1R. Receptor binding
assays to determine binding affinities (Ki values) at OX1R and OX2R were not conducted.
In Vivo Studies
The effect of oveporexton on wakefulness in animals was studied in wild type (WT) mice,
orexin 2 receptor knockout (OX2R KO) mice, and in an NT1 mouse model (OX/ataxin-3
transgenic (TG) mice). Because mice are nocturnal animals with a polyphasic sleep/wake
structure, studies were also conducted in cynomolgus monkeys which are diurnal and have a
monophasic sleep/wake structure more analogous to humans.
Oveporexton at 10 mg/kg produced a statistically significant increase in wakefulness time
during the sleep phase in both normal C56BL/6J mice (Study No. Neuroscience2024-KM-001)
and in non-transgenic WT littermates of OX2R KO mice; however, the same dose did not
affect wakefulness time in OX2R KO mice (Study No. Neuroscience2020-KM-005),
demonstrating that oveporexton produces an arousal effect in mice through activation of the
OX2R. The effect of oveporexton on wakefulness during the sleep phase was also
demonstrated in OX/ataxin-3 transgenic mice (a NT1 mouse model in which the transgene
selectively eliminates orexin producing neurons in the hypothalamus) and their nontransgenic WT littermates.

increased the duration of wakefulness episodes (Study No. Neuroscience2020-KM-002 and

increase in wakefulness times during the sleep phase (Study No. Neuroscience2020-KM-006)
when the physiological orexin level is thought to be low (Narita et al., 2023). These results
suggest that oveporexton shows arousal effects not only in nocturnal mice but also in diurnal
non-human primates with normal orexin levels during their sleep phase.

Overall, the primary pharmacology studies demonstrated that oveporexton acts as a OX2R
agonist, with much greater selectively for OX2R versus OXR1, and is capable of producing
wakefulness and ameliorating narcolepsy-like characteristics in relevant animal models.

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Secondary Pharmacology
Secondary pharmacology studies included in vitro off-target assessments against a broad
array of receptors, ion channels, and enzymes (102 targets in total, including opioid
receptors, 5-HT and dopamine transporters and receptors, NMDA, GABA, nicotinic
acetylcholine and cannabinoid receptors; Study No. Neuroscience2020-KM-001).
Oveporexton at 10 μmol/L (5205 ng/mL) did not show significant activity (>50% inhibition or
stimulation of radioligand binding) at any of the tested targets, except for cannabinoid
receptor type 1 (CB1) and progesterone receptor B (PR-B), which showed 58% and 62%
inhibition, respectively. The maximum recommended human dosage (MRHD) of oveporexton
of 4 mg/day (2 mg twice daily) is associated with a total Cmax,ss of 26.4 ng/mL and an unbound
Cmax,ss,unbound of 0.98 ng/mL (based on an unbound fraction of 0.037 in human plasma). The
Applicant proposed that the concentration at which oveporexton may affect CB1 and PR-B
receptors is approximately >5000-fold than the highest unbound Cmax and approximately 200fold the total Cmax,ss reached at the MRHD, and therefore significant CB1- and/or PR-B-related
clinical effects are not expected in humans.
A second in vitro assessment was conducted to evaluate the potential off-target functional
activity of oveporexton (Study No. US073-0004661-O_TAK-861) at various receptors, ion
channels, and enzymes (47 targets in total but not including PR-B receptors). Oveporexton did
not show significant activity (defined as 50% inhibitory concentration [IC50] or EC50 ≤10
µmol/L) on any of the tested receptors, ion channels, or enzymes except for CB1, in which
oveporexton showed antagonistic activity with an IC50 of 3.2 µmol/L (1666 ng/mL). Activity at
CB-1 is approximately 1700-fold higher than the Cmax,ss,unbound (0.98 ng/mL) and approximately
63-fold the Cmax,ss (26.4 ng/mL) at the MRHD of 4 mg/day (2 mg twice daily), suggesting no
significant CB-1-related off-target effects in humans.
Regarding the assessment of oveporexton functional activity at the progesterone B receptor,
a nonclinical information request was sent to the Applicant on May 20, 2026, asking if any
additional specific assays to assess functional activity at the progesterone B receptor were
conducted. The Applicant responded that no further progesterone B receptor assays were
performed (Response letter of May 22, 2026) and stated:in the first study, oveporexton
showed 62% inhibition of ligand binding to the PR-B at a concentration of 10 µmol/L
(5,205 ng/mL). Since a single concentration was tested in this assay and no concentrationresponse relationship was obtained, an IC50 value was not calculated. Under a conservative
assumption that the IC50 would be one-tenth of the concentration producing 62% inhibition of
ligand binding (i.e., 1 µmol/L (520.5 ng/mL)), this concentration is more than 500-fold higher
than the human Cmax,ss,unbound (0.98 ng/mL) at the MRHD of 4 mg/day (2 mg twice daily). Given
the low likelihood of clinically relevant off-target effects mediated by PR-B, no additional
specific assays beyond those described above were conducted to assess functional activity on
the PR-B.
Although there are no nonclinical data to determine if oveporexton has functional activity at
the PR-B receptor, it appears that the clinical relevance of any potential adverse effects is
minimal.

51
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
A computational approach to evaluate the structural similarity between oveporexton and
every controlled substance named in the U.S. Controlled Substances Act (CSA), inclusive of
Schedule I through V (Study No. TKD-SDS-15870) indicated no structural similarity of
oveporexton to substances specified in the CSA.
Because other orexin-2 agonist drugs developed by the Applicant caused urinary urgency and
frequency in clinical trials, a urinary function study in C57BL/6J mice with oveporexton was
conducted. The results of the study demonstrated that orally administered oveporexton at
doses of 3 mg/kg and 10 mg/kg caused significant increases in void frequency without
affecting total void volume or water intake (Study No. Neuroscience2023-AK-001).
Safety Pharmacology
The safety pharmacology of oveporexton was evaluated in a series of good laboratory
practices (GLP)-compliant in vitro and in vivo studies to assess potential effects in the CNS,
cardiovascular, and respiratory systems.
CNS
The effects of oveporexton on the CNS were evaluated in male Sprague-Dawley rats
administered single doses of 0, 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg/day by oral gavage (6 rats/group)
(Study No. B200314). Modified Irwin’s multiple observation method was used to examine the
effects on general physical condition and behavior, and body temperature was also
measured. Observations were conducted at pre-dose and at 1, 2, 4, 8, and 24h post-dose.
Body temperature increased at 20 and 100 mg/kg (+1.0°C and+0.5°C, respectively compared
to controls) and a decrease occurred at 1000 mg/kg (-0.7°C compared to controls). The
changes in body temperature may be related to pharmacological action of oveporexton;
based on literature data, orexin-A induces both hypothermia and hyperthermia, and orexin-B
induces hyperthermia in rats (Szekely et al., 2002).
Restlessness was observed in all animals at 20 and 100 mg/kg and in 3 of 6 animals at
1000 mg/kg at 1 and 2 h post-dose, and slight increases in spontaneous activity were
observed in 1 or 2 animals at 20 and 100 mg/kg at 1 h post-dose (Study No. B200314). These
findings were also considered to be due to the pharmacological actions of oveporexton, as
OX2R agonists have been reported to increase locomotor activity in rats (Samson et al., 2010).
The lowest observed effect level (LOEL) for effects on the CNS in male rats was 20 mg/kg,
which provides approximately a 127-fold exposure margin based on Cmax at the LOEL of
3350 ng/ml (taken from Day 1 Cmax value in male rats from the 4-week toxicity Study No. B8713) compared to the total Cmax,ss of 26.4 ng/ml at the MRHD in humans.
The abuse potential of oveporexton was assessed in several in vivo studies in rats. See the
Controlled Substance Staff review for a review of the nonclinical and clinical abuse potential
studies.

52
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Cardiovascular
In in vitro cardiovascular assessments, oveporexton produced a minimal but statistically
significant inhibitory effect on the hERG current at 3 μg/mL (the highest concentration tested)
of 9.0% compared to the vehicle control when tested using HEK293 cells stably expressing
hERG channels under physiological conditions and temperature. The IC50 value for hERG
inhibition was >3 μg/mL, which provides an approximate safety margin of >3000-fold
compared with the human Cmax,ss,unbound (0.98 ng/mL) at the MRHD (Study No.B240294). The
potential effects of oveporexton on late Nav1.5 and Cav1.2 current amplitudes at physiologic
temperatures were tested at 1, 3, 10, 100 μmol/L in HEK293 cells stably expressing Nav1.5
and Cav1.2 channels (Study No. TPI-24-01). Oveporexton did not block late Nav1.5 current but
blocked the Cav1.2 current with an IC50 of 85.8 μM (step pulse) and 7.9 μM (ramp pulse). The
IC50 of 7.9 μM oveporexton (4112 ng/mL) corresponds to an approximate exposure margin of
>4000-fold compared with the human Cmax,ss,unboud of 0.98 ng/mL at the MRHD.
In vivo evaluation of cardiovascular function was performed in a GLP-compliant study in
cynomolgus monkeys (4 males, 5 to 6 years of age) using a telemetry system after single oral
gavage administration of oveporexton at doses of 0, 3, 30, and 300 mg/kg, administered in a
Latin Square design with a washout period of 7 to 14 days between doses (Study No. SBL010416). Systemic blood pressure (systolic (SBP), diastolic (DBP), and mean (MBP)), left
ventricular pressure (LVSP, LVEDP, and LV dP/dt max), heart rate, and ECG parameters (PR
interval, QRS duration, QT interval, and QTc interval) were measured at pre-dose and at 1, 2,
4, 8, 12, 18, and 24 h post-dose. TK analysis was conducted at 3 mg/kg after the telemetry
data acquisition.
Increased systemic blood pressure and LVSP were noted between 1 and 4 h post-dose at all
doses, with maximum increases compared to controls of +27, +19, and +23 mm Hg in SBP,
DBP, and MBP, respectively, and +28 mm Hg in LVSP, observed 1 h post-dose at 300 mg/kg. At
300 mg/kg, significant increases in LV dP/dt max and heart rate were noted in one animal at
1h post-dose (+1618 mm Hg/sec and +42 beats/min, respectively). These increases may be
attributed to the pharmacological action of oveporexton at OX2R in the CNS, because it has
been reported that intracerebroventricular administration of orexin increases arterial blood
pressure and heart rate in rats mainly via OX2R (Huang et al., 2010). These changes were nonadverse because the degree of change was small and/or the changes were transient. A
decrease in LV dP/dt max was also noted at 300 mg/kg (-639 mm Hg/sec) from 8 to 18h.
Although mechanisms for this change were not clear, there were no effects on other
hemodynamic parameters (systemic blood pressure or HR) at the same time points.
Therefore, the decreased LV dP/dt max was considered non-adverse. There were no reported
adverse ECG findings.
In conclusion, a single oral administration of oveporexton to male monkeys induced increases
in systemic blood pressure and LVSP at ≥3 mg/kg and a transient increase in LV dP/dt max and
heart rate and then a decrease in LV dP/dt max at 300 mg/kg. Although a no observed effect
level (NOEL) was not identified in this study, the Cmax at the LOEL of 3 mg/kg (449 ng/mL) is
17-fold higher than the clinical Cmax,ss at the MRHD suggesting low clinical relevance.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
In vivo evaluation of cardiovascular function was also conducted as part of the 4-, 13-, and 39week GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicity studies in monkeys (Studies No. B-8714, B-8802,
and B-8934, respectively). In the repeat-dose toxicity studies in male and female monkeys
(n=3 to 4/sex/group), oveporexton was administered by oral gavage once daily at 30, 100,
and 300 mg/kg/day. ECGs were measured pre-dose, and at the end of the dosing period. No
drug-related changes in ECG parameters were noted in any of the studies. At the NOEL of
300 mg/kg/day for ECG effects in the 39-week study, the highest dose tested in monkeys, the
sex-combined exposures provided an approximate 429-fold margin compared to the
Cmax,ss,unbound and an approximate 568-fold margin compared to the total Cmax,ss at the MRHD.

Respiratory
The effects of oveporexton on the respiratory system were evaluated in male Sprague-Dawley
rats administered single doses of 0, 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg by oral gavage (8 animals/group)
(Study No. B200362). Respiratory rate (RR), tidal volume (TV), and minute volume (MV) were
measured using whole-body plethysmography at pre-dose and 1, 2, 4, 8, and 24 h post-dose.
Statistically significant increases in RR at 8 h post-dose at 20 and 100 mg/kg and in MV at 8 h
post-dose at 100 mg/kg were observed compared to the vehicle group. At 1000 mg/kg, there
were no statistically significant changes for RR or MV. There were also no statistically
significant changes in tidal volume at any dose level.

54
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Table 2. Respiratory Rate in Rat Respiratory Safety Pharmacology Study

Source: Study Report No. B200362, Table 1, p. 19

Table 3. Minute Volume in Rat Respiratory Safety Pharmacology Study

Source: Study Report No. B200362, Table 3, p. 21

The non-dose dependent effect of oveporexton on respiratory function in rats correlates with
the effects on the CNS observed in the rat safety pharmacology study (Study No. B200314) in
which increased restlessness and spontaneous activity were more notable at 20 and
100 mg/kg than at 1000 mg/kg. Therefore, the increased activity might have contributed to
the increased RR and MV observed in rats at 20 and 100 mg/kg as a secondary factor. The
increase in RR and MV may also be attributed to the direct pharmacological action of
oveporexton in the CNS as published literature data suggest orexin-A, which acts on both
orexin receptor subtypes 1 and 2, elicits cardiovascular and respiratory excitation, and
administration of orexin-A into the lateral ventricles of the brain of mice induces a rise in RR
and tidal volume (Sakurai et al., 1998). The exposure margin for oveporexton effects on
respiratory function is approximately 127-fold, which is based on the Cmax at the LOEL of
20 mg/kg (Cmax value taken from the 4-week toxicity study in male rats, dose 20 mg/kg, Day 1;
Study No. B-8713), compared to the Cmax,ss at the MRHD.

Major Findings
Rapid absorption in rats after single oral (PO) doses of 10 and 30 mg/kg, with tmax 2 to 3 h postdose and t1/2 of 3 h AUC24h increased in a greater than dose proportional manner from 10 to
30 mg/kg. The tmax and t1/2 values after a PO [14C]oveporexton dose of 100 mg/kg (Study No.
313N-2106) were 2 and 3.8 h, respectively, consistent with the results at 10 and 30 mg/kg. High
oral bioavailability in rats (>100%) with much lower oral bioavailability observed in monkeys
(32%)
Rapid absorption in monkeys after single PO doses 1 or 3 mg/kg, with tmax 2 to 3 h post-dose and
t1/2 4 to 5 h. AUC24h increased dose proportionally from 1 to 3 mg/kg. The tmax and t1/2 values
after a PO [14C]oveporexton dose of 100 mg/kg (Study No. 313N-2106) were 4.7 and 7.2 h,
respectively, suggesting a slightly delayed absorption and prolonged elimination at the higher
dose of 100 mg/kg compared to 1 and 3 mg/kg.

Study No. 313N2106

After a single PO administration of 100 mg/kg [14C]oveporexton to male pigmented and albino
rats, [14C]oveporexton was widely distributed in the tissues of pigmented rats with quantifiable
levels in 30 of 43 sampled tissues through 24 h post-dose. The greatest exposure (AUCall >3000
h*μg equiv/g) was observed in adrenal cortex, adrenal gland, and liver. Tissues with low
exposure (AUCall <100 h*μg equiv/g) included the spinal cord, brain, eye lens, and bone. At 336 h
post-dose, the only tissues with quantifiable levels of radioactivity were adrenal gland (adrenal
cortex and medulla), liver, and gastric mucosa. Albino rats showed a pattern of tissue
distribution and exposure of [14C]oveporexton similar to that observed in pigmented rats. There
was no evidence of melanin binding in pigmented rats.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
Single-dose toxicity studies and repeat-dose toxicity studies were performed in SpragueDawley rats (2-, 4-, 13- and 26-weeks duration) and in Cynomolgus monkeys (2-, 4-, 13- and
39-weeks duration). Exposure based safety margins were calculated based on a clinical
population pharmacokinetic AUC24,ss value at the MRHD of 369.6 h*ng/mL in patients from
Study TAK-861-3001.
Study Title/Number: Thirteen-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of TAK-861 in Rats With a
Recovery Period of 4-Weeks/B-8803
•

Degeneration of ameloblasts in the incisors occurred at the high dose of 1500 mg/kg, with
corresponding clinical observations of whitening and fractures. Whitening and fractures in
the incisors were still noted during the recovery period due to time required for rat incisor
renewal (40-50 days), however, the severity of ameloblast degeneration decreased by the
end of recovery period.

•

Dilatation of kidney proximal and distal tubules occurred in high dose females associated
with increased plasma blood urea nitrogen (BUN) levels, increased urine volume, and
increased kidney weights; findings were reversible; Decreased body weight for high dose
males.

•

NOAEL: 100 mg/kg for both sexes, associated with a mean Cmax of 9090 and 12,400 ng/mL,
and a mean AUC24h of 128,000 and 164,000 h*ng/mL in males and females, respectively, in
Week 13 of dosing. Safety margins at NOAEL compared to the MRHD are 346- and 444-fold
for males and females, respectively, based on AUC.

Major Findings
A single HD (1500 mg/kg/day) female was terminated early on Day 10 of dosing due to
moribund condition. The animal presented with decreased movement, bradypnea,
hypothermia, and emaciation prior to termination. A marked decrease in body weight from
Day 4 to Day 10 (-34% from Day 1 values). Macroscopic findings of reduced organ size
(likely due to weight loss) of the mesenteric lymph node, spleen, and thymus, and multiple
dark red foci in the glandular stomach were noted. Minimal single cell necrosis and mild
regeneration of the collecting ducts of the kidney were noted. There were changes in
hematology and clinical pathology parameters that are likely due to deteriorating health
condition. The underlying cause of moribundity was not determined; however, a drug
related cause cannot be ruled out.
Whitening of incisors at HD of 1500 mg/kg with all animals affected by the end of the
dosing period. Fracture of the incisors was also noted starting in weeks 11 and 12 of
dosing. Incisor whitening was still present at the end of the recovery period; however, the
incidence of incisor fractures was lower at the end of recovery indicating reversibility. This
finding corresponded with histopathology of ameloblast degeneration, associated with
fluorosis.
Significant reduction in mean body weight (-9%) compared to controls in HD males at the
end of the dosing period. At the end of the recovery period, mean body weight remained
lower (-11%) compared to controls while mean body weight gain was similar to control. In
females, mean body weight and body weight gain were similar to controls during the
dosing period however, during recovery, HD females lost weight and mean body weight
was reduced (-8%) compared to controls.
Food consumption was transiently decreased at HD in both males and females during the
first two weeks of the dosing period.
No drug-related findings.

Significant decreases (up to -6%) in RBC counts compared to controls in males at
≥20 mg/kg/day. At 1500 mg/kg/day, significant increases in PLT (+13%) in males; WBC
(+36%) and MONO (+69%) in females; RDW (+12-21%) and Retic (+30-34%) in both and
significant decreases in MCV (-4%) and MCH (-7%) in females; HGB (-7- 8%), HCT (-5%)
and MCHC (-2-3%) in both sexes. All hematology changes were considered non-adverse
due to the low magnitude of the changes and no correlated microscopic findings in any
organs or tissue.
At the end of the recovery period, signiﬁcant decreases in MONO (males); and MCH and
Retic (females) at 1500 mg/kg/day. The changes were generally within historical control
ranges.
A significant increase (+55%) in ALT levels in males at 1500 mg/kg/day. A significant and
dose-related increase in total cholesterol in males (up to +101%) at ≥100 mg/kg/day and in
females (up to +174%) at ≥20 mg/kg/day. A significant increase in BUN (up to +58%) in
females at 1500 mg/kg/day. At the end of the recovery period no significant differences
compared to controls were noted.
Significant increase in urine volume (122%) and water intake (23%) in HD females
compared to controls. At the end of recovery period, no significant differences compared
to controls were noted.
Enlarged liver at ≥100 mg/kg/day, whitening and fracture of the incisors at 1500 mg/kg/day
in both males and females. In the kidney, focal white discoloration in a single HD female
with no correlated microscopic finding. At the end of recovery period, macroscopic finding
of incisor whitening was still present; however, the occurrence of incisor fracture was
reduced (males) or completely resolved (females).

General Toxicology, Additional Studies
Single- and repeat-dose toxicity studies of 2- and 4-weeks duration were also conducted in
rats and monkeys.
Rats
A single-dose non-GLP toxicity study was conducted in rats (Study No. 19-341/ac) assessing
single doses of 0, (0.5% [w/v] methylcellulose (MC) solution), 100, 300, and 1000 mg/kg by
oral gavage. All rats survived and no adverse clinical signs were noted; no other parameters
were evaluated besides mortality and clinical signs.
Similar doses were used in an exploratory 2-week repeat-dose non-GLP-compliant toxicity
study in rats (Study No. N-2315), in which oral gavage doses of 0, (0.5% [w/v] MC solution),
30, 100, and 1000 mg/kg were evaluated to inform dose selection for the definitive toxicology
studies. There were no drug-related findings in survival and clinical signs. Body weight
decrease occurred in males at 1000 mg/kg compared to control, and food consumption was
decreased in both males and females at 1000 mg/kg compared to control. In clinical
chemistry, the following changes were noted compared to control: increases in total
cholesterol in males at 1000 mg/kg and in females at ≥30 mg/kg; higher γ-GTP at 1000 mg/kg
in both sexes; higher ALT, glucose, calcium, and total protein and lower A/G ratio in females
at 1000 mg/kg, and higher triglycerides in females at ≥30 mg/kg. Histopathology findings
were noted in the liver, thyroid, adrenals and ovary. In the liver, hypertrophy of the
centrilobular hepatocytes was observed at ≥100 mg/kg in association with increased liver
weight and macroscopic liver enlargement at 1000 mg/kg. In the thyroid gland, follicular cell
hypertrophy was observed at 1000 mg/kg. These findings were consistent with induction of
hepatic drug-metabolizing enzymes and therefore, were considered adaptive and nonadverse. In the adrenal gland, increased diffuse vacuolation of adrenal cortex was found at
1000 mg/kg. In the ovary, vacuolation in interstitial cells of the ovary was observed at
≥100 mg/kg, and inflammation of the interstitial gland of the ovary at 1000 mg/kg.
Inflammation of the interstitial gland of the ovary at 1000 mg/kg was considered adverse.
However, vacuolation without cytotoxic changes observed in the adrenal gland at 1000 mg/kg
and ovary at ≥100 mg/kg were considered non-adverse. The NOAEL for males was
1000 mg/kg/day, and the NOAEL for females was 100 mg/kg/day.
In the 4-week repeat-dose GLP-compliant toxicity study in rats (Study No. B-8713), doses of 0,
(0.5% [w/v] MC solution), 20, 100, and 1000 mg/kg were evaluated, including determination
of plasma fluoride concentrations. A similar toxicity profile was observed in the 4-week study
in rats as compared to the subsequent 13- and 26-week studies. In the incisors, degeneration
74
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
of ameloblasts was observed at 1000 mg/kg; however, no abnormalities were noted in clinical
and macroscopic observation of the incisors. In the liver, hypertrophy of centrilobular
hepatocytes was observed at ≥100 mg/kg, accompanied by liver enlargement at ≥100 mg/kg
and increased liver weight in males at ≥100 mg/kg and females at ≥20 mg/kg. In this study,
the determination of hepatic drug metabolizing enzyme activities revealed increases in
aminopyrine N-demethylase and 7-ethoxyresorufin O-deethylase activity in males at
≥100 mg/kg (up to +154%) and females at ≥20 mg/kg (up to +234%) and in p-nitrophenol
UDP-glucuronosyltransferase activity at ≥100 mg/kg (up to +293%). Therefore, findings in the
liver were considered to be caused by hepatic drug-metabolizing enzyme induction, an
adaptive and non-adverse change. In the adrenal gland, increased diffuse vacuolation in the
cortex was observed in males at ≥100 mg/kg and females at 1000 mg/kg. In the ovary,
interstitial cell vacuolation was noted at ≥100 mg/kg. The vacuolation in these organs was
considered non-adverse because the changes were minimal, not cytotoxic, and were not
accompanied by organ weight changes. Inflammation of the interstitial gland of the ovary
(found in 1 animal at 1000 mg/kg/day in the preceding 2-week rat study and considered
adverse) was not observed in the present study. Therefore, the NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg for
both males and females.
Fluoride released from oveporexton via metabolism was detected in rat plasma. The mean
plasma fluoride Cmax and AUC24h increased in a less than dose-proportional manner and were
comparable between both sexes.
Table 25. Mean Plasma Fluoride Parameters Following 4-Week Oral Administration of
Oveporexton to Rats

Monkeys
A single-dose non-GLP toxicity study was conducted in monkeys (Study No. 19-340/ac)
assessing single escalating doses of 0, (0.5% [w/v] methylcellulose (MC) solution), 100, 300,
and 1000 mg/kg by oral gavage, to inform dose selection for repeat-dose toxicity studies.
Based on the following findings at 1000 mg/kg, the high dose for the subsequent repeat dose
was capped at 300 mg/kg: vomiting in all animals at 1000 mg/kg which could potentially
impact animal health with repeated doses and delayed tmax at 24 h post-dose suggesting a
potential increase in systemic exposure by repeated dosing. Together, these results indicated
75
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
that administration of oveporexton at 1000 mg/kg in monkeys was not tolerable for repeatdose toxicity studies.
In a 2-week repeat-dose non-GLP-compliant toxicity study in monkeys (Study No. N-2316) oral
gavage doses of 0, (0.5% [w/v] MC solution), 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg were evaluated to assess
the potential toxicity and toxicokinetic profile of oveporexton, including determination of
plasma fluoride concentrations. Toxicity was limited to a single incidence of vomiting in one
HD female on Day 1, and non-adverse increases in white blood cell, neutrophil and/or
monocyte counts, and in triglycerides at 300 mg/kg without any related microscopic findings.
Increased liver weights with minimal hypertrophy of hepatocytes and/or macroscopically
enlarged liver were observed in 1 male at 100 mg/kg and in all animals at 300 mg/kg. The
morphological characteristics of microscopic findings in the liver were compatible with
hepatic drug-metabolizing enzyme induction and were considered adaptive and non-adverse.
In the 4-week repeat-dose GLP-compliant toxicity study in monkeys (Study No. B-8714), doses
of 0, (0.5% [w/v] MC solution), 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg were evaluated, including
determination of plasma fluoride concentrations. A similar toxicity profile was observed in
the 4-week study as compared to the subsequent 13- and 26-week studies. The main findings
included changes in hematology and/or clinical chemistry parameters at ≥30 mg/kg and
microscopic findings in the liver. The observation of minimal hepatocyte hypertrophy in males
at ≥100 mg/kg and in females at 300 mg/kg was determined to be associated with a hepatic
drug-metabolizing enzyme induction: increases in 7-ethoxyresorufin O-deethylase at
≥30 mg/kg in males (up to +136% versus control), in aminopyrine N-demethylase at
300 mg/kg in males (+59% versus control) and at ≥100 mg/kg in females (+57% and +61%
versus control) , and in p-nitrophenol UDP-glucuronosyltransferase activity at 300 mg/kg in
males (+35%) and at ≥30 mg/kg in females (up to +30%). Therefore, similar to rats, the
microscopic findings in the liver were considered to be non-adverse adaptive changes. The
NOAEL for both the 2-week and 4-week monkey studies was the HD of 300 mg/kg/day for
males and females.
Fluoride released from oveporexton via metabolism was detected in monkey plasma. The
mean Cmax and AUC24h for plasma fluoride increased in a less than dose-proportional manner
for both sexes. No clear sex-related differences in fluoride Cmax or AUC24h were observed at
any dose.

Genetic Toxicology
In Vitro Reverse Mutation Assay in Bacterial Cells (Ames)
Study Title/Number: Bacterial Reversion Assay of TAK-861/T-3244
Key Study Findings
There was no increase in the mean number of revertant colonies for all tester strains and all
drug concentrations, both with and without metabolic activation (S9), compared to the
respective negative control. Therefore, the study was negative, and TAK-861 is considered
non-mutagenic under the conditions of this assay.
GLP compliance: Yes
Test system: Bacterial strains S. typhimurium TA100, TA1535, TA98, TA1537, E. coli WP2 uvr A
Study is valid: Yes
In Vitro Assays in Mammalian Cells
Study Title/Number: In Vitro Micronucleus Test of TAK-861 in TK6 Cells/T-G492
Key Study Findings
There was a statistically significant increase in the frequency of micronucleated cells at the
highest concentration tested of 60.0 μg/mL compared to the negative control for the 24-hour
continuous treatment. The mean percent frequency of 1.7% was within the historical control
range of 0.45 to 1.85, but outside the average 95% confidence interval of 0.79%. The effect
was also concentration dependent. Therefore, it was concluded that chromosomal aberration

was induced with continuous treatment of 60.0 μg/mL TAK- 861 under the conditions of this

Other Genetic Toxicity Studies
None
Carcinogenicity
Oveporexton was not carcinogenic in two adequately conducted GLP rodent carcinogenicity
studies.
26-Week Oral Gavage Carcinogenicity Study in Tg RasH2 Mice (Study B-8968)
Oveporexton was administered once daily for 26 weeks by oral gavage to CByB6F1-Tg
(HRAS)2Jic mice (Tg-rasH2 mice) (25/sex/group) at 0 (vehicle control 0.5% [w/v]
methylcellulose solution), 6, 20, 60, and 600 mg/kg for males and 0, 3, 30, and 300 mg/kg for
females. A positive control group (15/sex) was administered N-methyl-N-nitrosourea
(75 mg/kg, IP). Additional satellite groups of nonTg-rasH2 mice (20 /sex/group) were used to
evaluate TK parameters of oveporexton in plasma. The dose selection was based on
approximately one-third of the respective lethal doses in a 1-week and 4-week toxicity study
in nonTg-rasH2 mice for males and females, respectively (Study No. N-2552 and No. B-8817),
in agreement with the recommendation of the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment
Committee (ECAC meeting minutes of June 16, 2022).
There was no drug-related, or statistically significant, increase in tumors in Tg-rasH2 mice at
doses up to 600 and 300 mg/kg in males and females, respectively. The nonclinical statistical
reviewer’s (Dr. Hepei Chen) analysis confirmed that “no statistically significant dose response
78
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
relationship or pairwise comparisons in any tested tumor were noted in either male or female
mice. For both male and female mice, the pairwise comparisons between the positive control
group and the vehicle control group showed p-values less than 0.05 for many tumors,
confirming animal expressed oncogenes and responded to carcinogenic insult as expected.”
The ECAC concluded that the mouse carcinogenicity study was adequate and that there was
no evidence of drug-related neoplasms in male and female mice in the 6-month study (ECAC
meeting minutes of June 2, 2026).
The highest tested doses of 600 and 300 mg/kg in males and females, respectively were
associated with a mean Cmax of 23,400 and 12,500 ng/mL, and a mean AUC24h of 148,000 and
64,100 h*ng/mL, respectively, on Week 26, which is approximately 400-and 173-fold the
MRHD, for males and females, respectively, based on AUC.
24-Month Oral Gavage Carcinogenicity Study of TAK-861 in Rats (Study B-8962)
Oveporexton was administered once daily for 24 months by oral gavage to Sprague Dawley
rats (60/sex/group) at 0 (vehicle control 0.5% [w/v] methylcellulose solution), 1, 20, and
100 mg/kg for both males and females. Additional satellite groups (8/sex/group) were used to
evaluate TK parameters of oveporexton in plasma. The basis for dose selection was AUC ratio
with the high dose achieving an AUC ratio greater than 25-fold the clinical exposure, in
agreement with the recommendations of the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment
Committee (ECAC meeting minutes of March 3, 2022).
There were no drug-related neoplastic or non-neoplastic changes at any dose level. The
nonclinical statistical reviewer’s (Dr. Hepei Chen) analysis concluded that a statistically
significant increase (p-value=0.0477) was noted in the mid-dose group in the incidence of
hepatocellular adenoma in liver of male rats, without a corresponding statistically significant
dose response relationship. No other statistically significant dose response relationship or
pairwise comparisons were observed in the tumor data for either male or female rats.
The ECAC concluded that the rat carcinogenicity study was adequate and that there was no
evidence of drug-related neoplasms in male and female rats in the 2-year study (ECAC
meeting minutes of June 2, 2026).

Embryo-Fetal Development
Study Title/Number: Study of TAK-861 for Effects on Embryo-Fetal Development in Rats/R1276
Key Study Findings
•

Decrease in maternal body weight at 750 mg/kg compared to control, associated with
decreased food consumption. Trend to a decrease in fetal weight and ossification of fetal
metatarsal bones at 750 mg/kg, finding was secondary to the lower maternal body weight
and considered non-adverse.

•

NOAEL for maternal toxicity: 100 mg/kg based on a decrease in maternal body weight at
750 mg/kg; NOAEL for embryo-fetal development: 750 mg/kg.

•

8580 ng/mL for Cmax and 128,000 for AUC24h; and at the NOAEL for embryo-fetal toxicity
(750 mg/kg):24,900 ng/mL for Cmax and 431,000 h*ng/mL for AUC24h on GD 17.

Dose selection was based on a 13-week oral gavage toxicity study of TAK-861 in rats
(Study No. B-8803, at doses of 0, 20, 100, and 1500 mg/kg/day), where mortality (1
HD female) and fluorosis-related dental fractures were observed at 1500 mg/kg/day,
and on the preliminary embryofetal development study in rats (Study No.R-1267 at
doses of 0, 10, 100, and 1000 mg/kg/day), where low body weights in dams,
associated with decreased food consumption and fetal growth inhibition, were
observed at 1000 mg/kg. Therefore, in the present study, the high dose was selected
at 750 mg/kg/day, half of the lethal dose of 1500 mg/kg, and the mid and low doses
were 100 and 10 mg/kg/day, respectively. The dams were dosed during the period of
organogenesis from Gestation Day (GD) 6 to 17, then sacrificed and necropsied on GD
20. Note: Two females in the vehicle control group were found to be non-pregnant
and were excluded from the evaluation. TK assessments (Cmax and AUC24h) were
performed on GDs 6 and 17 in additional satellite animals.

None
None observed
Statistically significant decrease in maternal body weight (up to -14%) and
suppressed body weight gain accompanied by decreased food consumption was
observed at HD compared to control from GD 8 to the end of the study (GD 20). At
MD, decreased body weight compared to control was observed from GDs 8 to 14
secondary to decreased food consumption; at LD, suppressed body weight gain was
observed only in the early phase of dosing (GD 6 to 8); however, these changes in
mid- and low dose groups were non-adverse because they were transient and/or
slight.
Maternal liver enlargement at HD in 9 of 20 dams (non-adverse adaptive change due
to the induction of hepatic drug metabolizing enzymes).
Fetal weights tended to decrease at HD compared to control, secondary to
decreased maternal food consumption and lower maternal body weight. The
changes did not reach statistical significance, and the group mean was slightly lower
than the historical control data at the test facility, therefore the findings were
considered non-adverse. Ossification of fetal metatarsal bones tended to decrease at
HD, secondary to the decrease in maternal body weight at this dose, but the effect
was minimal and considered non-adverse. An increased incidence of fetal umbilical
artery transposition was noted at HD [7.2%, significantly higher than that in the
control group (0%) as well as in the historical control (2.1%)]. The Applicant
considered the finding to be non-adverse because this variation has been reported
as spontaneously occurring in this strain of rats without affecting postnatal viability
(Kuwagata et al., 2019) and no abnormalities were noted in any other blood vessels.
The Applicant’s conclusion that the finding is non-adverse is acceptable because
there were no other correlating adverse toxicities observed in HD fetuses, but the
finding is considered drug related.
