Major Findings
Decrease in total weight gain at HD over the entire dosing period (-100% compared to
control), associated with a decrease in food consumption (up to -67% compared to
control). At LD and MD, maternal weight gain was transiently decreased in the early
dosing period in association with reduced food consumption; however, these findings
were non-adverse because they were minimal and/or transient, limited to the early
phase of dosing, and fully reversible upon dosing cessation. Body weight gain increased
compared to controls in all dose groups during GD 21 to 28 in correlating with an
increase in food consumption.
There were no drug-related changes in placental or uterine weights in any dose group
compared to control.
There were no drug-related effects on the number of resorptions, post-implantation loss,
number of live fetuses, or sex ratio at any dose. Note: A statistically significant lower
mean number of fetuses at HD compared to control was not drug related because it was
due to the lower mean number of corpora lutea in this group which is not drug- related,
because the number of corpora lutea is fixed at the time of ovulation, before the start of
dosing.
There were no drug-related external, visceral or skeletal malformations in any group. At
HD, increased incidences of a visceral variation (absent accessory lung lobe), and of a
skeletal variation (27th presacral vertebra) were noted. These variations are commonly
observed in rabbits and are considered non-adverse.
There were no drug-related effects on skeletal ossification.

Dose selection was based on the definitive embryo-fetal development study in rats
(Study No. R-1276), at oveporexton oral doses of 0, 10, 100, and 750 mg/kg/day, with a
NOAEL of 100 mg/kg/day for maternal toxicity (low body weight). In the present study,
the high dose was set at 10 mg/kg, which the Applicant stated was expected to provide
sufficient systemic exposure compared to the maximum anticipated clinical exposure.

Deviation from study
protocol affecting
interpretation of results:

Pregnant F0 females were dosed from gestation day (GD) 7 through postnatal
Lactation Day (LD) 20, corresponding to the period from implantation to weaning.
Animals were allowed to rear their progeny until LD 21 and were euthanized on LD 21.
Assessments of F1 generation pups included: viability, clinical signs, body weights,
external anomalies, physical development, reflex function, behavior (open field and
water T-maze tests), sexual maturation, reproductive function, and macroscopic
evaluation of the next F2 generation. After the observations and measurements on
Postnatal Day (PND) 4, F1 litter size was adjusted to 8 animals per litter (4 males and 4
females, where possible). On PND 21, 2 male and 2 female offspring were selected
randomly from each litter for observation after weaning. The rest of the offspring were
euthanized. F1 reproductive function tests were performed for 1 male and 1 female in
each litter. At 11 to 12 weeks of age, F1 males and females in the same group were
housed together on one-to- one basis avoiding sibling mating. For F1 females that had
successful copulation, body weight gain (GD 0 to 14) was determined, and they were
euthanized on GD 14. The major organs/tissues in the thoracic and abdominal cavities
were assessed macroscopically, and the numbers of corpora lutea, implantations, live
and dead F2 embryos, and external abnormalities were determined. Toxicokinetics was
not evaluated in this study.

Major Findings
Increased food consumption (up to 9%) was observed during the lactation period at all doses;
the changes were small, transient and had no effect on body weights. There were no drugrelated clinical signs and no effects on maternal body weight, no macroscopic findings at
necropsy, and no effects on reproductive function, including the gestation period duration,
gestation index, birth index, number of implantations, live and dead newborns and sex ratio.
Increased F1 body weights compared to controls were observed during the late lactation
period at MD and HD (up to 7% and 8% in males and females, respectively), that correlated
with the increase in maternal F0 food consumption during the lactation period; however, they
were considered non-adverse because the changes were small and there were no effects on
any other examination endpoints.
At all dose levels, the vaginal opening indices on Postnatal Day (PND) 35 were lower than
control (60 to 60.5% in all drug-treated groups vs. 84% in control). However, this delay was
considered non-adverse since vaginal opening was 100% completed in all animals on PND 42
(which is generally considered the time of sexual maturation completion in rats), and there
were no abnormalities in F1 reproductive function.

Juvenile Toxicity
Study Title/Number: A 6-Week Repeated Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of TAK-861 in 28-day old
Rats with a 4-Week Recovery Period/SBL010-561
Key Study Findings
•

Drug-related changes in clinical pathology at ≥10 mg/kg: decreases in erythrocyte count,
hemoglobin, hematocrit, and mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration in females at
≥10 mg/kg, decrease in total bilirubin in both sexes at ≥10 mg/kg, and increase in gammaglutamyl transferase in females at 50 mg/kg. These changes were non-adverse because they
were minimal, recovered following the 4-week recovery period and/or were not associated
with histopathological findings.

•

Drug-related microscopic ﬁndings in the liver in females at 50 mg/kg: slight hypertrophy of
centrilobular hepatocytes at the end of dosing period, accompanied by increased liver
weight in both sexes at 50 mg/kg. These ﬁndings recovered following the 4-week recovery
period and were non-adverse as they were attributable to induction of hepatic drugmetabolizing enzymes and similar to ﬁndings in adult rats.

•

NOAEL: 50 mg/kg for both sexes, associated with a mean Cmax of 5,450 and 6,130 ng/mL and
a mean AUC24h of 70,800 and 82,000 h*ng/mL in males and females, respectively

These effects correlated with microscopic findings in the liver and were reversible following the 4week recovery period.
Bone Evaluation Bone Length
There were no changes in bone length at the end of the dosing period.
Bone Density
At the end of the dosing period, statistically significant increases in bone density of the proximal
femur at ≥10 mg/kg and distal femur at 50 mg/kg were found in males; however, these changes
were not adverse because they were minimal and there were no statistically significant changes in
bone mineral density of the total femur. At the end of the recovery period, statistically significant
increase in bone mineral density of the total and proximal femur was found in females at
50 mg/kg; however, these changes were not adverse because the magnitude of effect was
minimal, and individual values were within the control group range.

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
Takeda Pharmaceuticals U.S.A., Inc. (Takeda) is seeking approval of oveporexton immediaterelease tablets for the treatment of NT1 in adults. The proposed tablet strengths are 0.5 mg,
1 mg, and 2 mg, with the recommended dosage of 1 mg or 2 mg orally twice daily (the first
dose after awakening and second dose 3 to 5 hours later). The recommended maximum total
daily dosage is 4 mg. Orexin 2 receptor agonism is thought to be the mechanism responsible
for oveporexton-mediated wakefulness effects in patients with NT1.
The clinical pharmacology program in this submission included single and multiple ascending
dose safety and pharmacokinetic (PK) studies, dedicated food effect study, mass-balance
study, QT assessment report, drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies, and organ impairment
studies (effects of mild (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) hepatic
impairment (HI), and severe renal impairment (RI; estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR)
<30 mL/min) on the PK of oveporexton). A relative bioavailability study (rBA) was conducted
to bridge two early phase formulations (T1 and T2 formulation) while the to-be-marketed
formulation (T3 formulation) was bridged to T2 formulation via population pharmacokinetic
(PK) model-based approach. T3 formulation was used in the two pivotal phase 3 safety and
efficacy studies, confirmatory food effect study and organ impairment studies. Population PK
and exposure-response (ER) analyses were conducted to characterize the relationship
between systemic exposures and efficacy/safety response of oveporexton.
Following oral administration, oveporexton showed dose-proportional PK characteristics over
the dose range of 1 to 75 mg. The rBA study and the popPK model-based bridging showed
that the exposures following T1, T2, and T3 formulations are comparable. With twice daily
(BID) dosing, steady state is achieved within approximately five days, with an accumulation
ratio of approximately 1.6. High-fat meal did not appreciably affect the PK of oveporexton.
Oveporexton is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4. The human mass balance study findings
suggest that oveporexton was the major moiety in the circulation and it accounts for
approximately 60% of total circulating moieties. The metabolites, M4 and M30, each account
for approximately 5% of total moieties in the circulation. Of the administered dose,
approximately 11% was recovered in urine (0.1% as unchanged oveporexton) and 80.5%
excreted in feces. No clinically relevant QT prolongation effects were observed with
concentration up to 16 times of the mean peak plasma concentration (Cmax) at steady state
for the maximum recommended dosage. Concomitant administration with strong CYP3A4
inhibitor increased oveporexton area under the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC∞) by
7.4-fold. Therefore, concomitant use of oveporexton with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors is
contraindicated. Concomitant administration with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor increased
oveporexton AUC∞ by 2.9-fold. Dosage reduction of oveporexton (i.e., 0.5 mg BID, with a
maximum total daily dose of 1 mg) is recommended when concomitantly administered with
moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.

91
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The dedicated PK trials and population PK analysis showed that exposures to oveporexton
were not appreciably increased in patients with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate
(Child-Pugh B) HI or patients with severe RI (eGFR <30 mL/min) compared to participants with
normal hepatic and renal function. Exposure-response analyses suggest that the probability of
insomnia, urinary events, and micturition urgency increased with increasing exposures.
However, the probabilities to have insomnia, urinary events, and micturition urgency in
patients with HI (mild to moderate) or RI (moderate to severe) were largely within the range
of those in patients with normal hepatic and renal function. As a result, the effect of HI and RI
were not considered clinically significant, and no dosage adjustment is required for these
conditions. A post-marketing requirement (PMR) study is recommended to evaluate the
effect of severe HI (Child-Pugh C) on the PK of oveporexton.
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology (OCP) finds that the clinical pharmacology data are
sufficient to support the safety and efficacy of oveporexton immediate-release tablets and
recommends approval of oveporexton 0.5 mg, 1 mg, and 2 mg immediate-release tablets for
the treatment of narcolepsy type 1 in adults.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
In the development program of oveporexton, three types of formulations were used. These
include immediate-release (IR) formulations (T1 [phase 1 tablets], T2 [phase 2 tablets], and T3
[phase 3 tablets]). However, the to-be marketed formulation of oveporexton is different from
the clinical trial formulations. T3 formulation was used in both pivotal safety and efficacy
studies (TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002), food effect study (TAK-861-1010), and hepatic and
renal impairment studies (TAK-861-1004 and TAK-861-1005). Because the core tablet
composition of the to-be-marketed is same as that of the T3 formulation, the Applicant
demonstrated a bridge between the to-be-marketed formulation and T3 formulation using in
vitro dissolution study. The results from the in vitro dissolution suggest that the bridge
between the formulations is adequate (refer to the integrated Office of Product Quality
review for additional information).
T2 formulation was used in the phase 2 dose ranging study (TAK-861-201) and clinical DDI
study (TAK-861-1007) and the T1 formulation was used in the early phase PK and tolerability
studies. A comparative bioavailability study (TAK-861-1007) was conducted to establish the
PK bridge between T1 and T2 formulations, and a population PK (popPK) model-based
approach was used to establish a PK bridge between T2 and T3 formulations along with the
results of an in vitro dissolution study. Overall, although this is not pivotal (T3 was used in
phase 3 trials), the PK bridging among T1, T2, and T3 formulations is deemed to be adequate.
The proposed dosage regimens (1 mg or 2 mg orally twice daily) are acceptable based on the
demonstration of favorable efficacy compared to placebo and acceptable safety results from
the two pivotal phase 3 trials (TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002).
The effect of food on the PK of oveporexton was evaluated using T3 formulation at the
highest strength (2 mg T3 tablet) in Study TAK-861-1010. Administration with high-fat meal
delayed the time to reach peak plasma concentration (Tmax) of oveporexton for 1.5 hours but
92
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
did not appreciably change the exposures (Cmax, AUClast, and AUCinf) of oveporexton when
compared to administration under fasted conditions. Therefore, the product can be taken
with or without food.
Oveporexton did not have any clinically significant effect on the QT interval with
concentration up to 11 times of the peak plasma concentration at steady state (Cmax,ss) for the
maximum recommended dosage (2 mg BID). No thorough QT study was conducted as a
waiver request for a thorough QT study was granted by the Agency based on the results of
clinical QT assessment in Study TAK-861-1001. The results of concentration-QTc analysis
suggest that the risk of QTc prolongation effects of oveporexton is low.
Positive exposure-safety relationships were identified for the most common adverse events
(insomnia, urinary events, and micturition urgency) and the overall percentage of events are
comparable between 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID based on the pooled data from the two pivotal
phase 3 studies.
The effect of HI on oveporexton PK was evaluated in a dedicated study (TAK-861-1004) in
patients with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) HI and population
PK analysis. In participants with mild HI, total oveporexton AUC∞ was 28% higher and Cmax
was 17% higher compared to participants with normal hepatic function. In participants with
moderate HI, AUC∞ was 23% higher and Cmax was 15% lower compared to controls. The
population PK analysis, which included 10 participants with moderate hepatic, showed that
mild or moderate HI increased median Cavg,ss by up to 43%. The effect of severe HI (Child-Pugh
Class C) was not studied. Based on the exposure-response analysis for safety, these elevated
exposures caused by mild and moderate HI are not expected to result in clinically meaningful
risks (See Section 16.4.4 Pharmacometrics Assessment for additional information), and no
dosage adjustment is required. A post-marketing requirement (PMR) to evaluate the effect of
severe HI (Child-Pugh Class C) on oveporexton PK is recommended to determine whether
there is a need to adjust the dosage for the safe use of oveporexton in patients with severe
HI.
The effect of RI on the PK of oveporexton was evaluated in a reduced design study (TAK-8611005) in patients with severe RI (estimated glomerular filtration rate [eGFR] ≥15 to
<30 mL/min; not requiring dialysis) and through population PK analysis. In participants with
severe RI, oveporexton AUC∞ was 45% higher, AUCt was 35% higher, and Cmax was 16% lower
compared to matched participants with normal renal function (eGFR 90 mL/min or greater).
The population PK analysis included participants across a broader range of renal function: 83
with mild RI, 4 with moderate RI, 10 with severe RI, and 397 with normal renal function. The
results suggest that moderate to severe RI increased Cavg,ss by up to 49%. The mild RI had no
effect, and moderate-to-severe RI had minimal effects on the clearance of oveporexton.
These findings are consistent with the minimal role of renal elimination in oveporexton
clearance, as renal clearance accounts for only approximately 11% of total elimination, with
less than 1% of the dose excreted as unchanged drug in urine. Based on the exposureresponse analysis for safety, these elevated exposures caused by severe RI are not expected
to result in clinically meaningful risks in patients with mild, moderate and severe RI (not
requiring dialysis) (See Section 16.4.4 Pharmacometrics Assessment for additional
93
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
information). As the PK of oveporexton in patients with severe RI on dialysis (eGFR
<15 mL/min) has not been studied, the reviewer proposed an avoid use recommendation in
patients with eGFR <15 mL/min on dialysis in the prescribing information.
The drug-drug interaction (DDI) potential of oveporexton was evaluated through in vitro
studies, clinical DDI trials, and physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling. Given
the predominant role of CYP3A4 in oveporexton metabolism, the primary focus was on
interactions with CYP3A inhibitors and inducers, both as an object and as a precipitant. When
oveporexton is concomitantly administered with strong CYP3A inhibitors, it resulted in a 7.4fold increase in AUC∞, a 67% increase in Cmax, and approximately a 5-fold increase in terminal
half-life compared to oveporexton administered alone. Coadministration of fluconazole, a
moderate CYP3A inhibitor, with oveporexton 10 mg resulted in a 2.86-fold increase in AUC∞,
a 50% increase in Cmax, and approximately a 2-fold increase in terminal half-life compared to
oveporexton alone. Based on these substantial increases in exposures and prolongation of
half-life, concomitant use of oveporexton with strong CYP3A inhibitors is contraindicated and
a dosage reduction is recommended (0.5 mg BID) when oveporexton is concomitantly used
with moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
In the clinical DDI study, coadministration of phenytoin, a strong CYP3A inducer, with
oveporexton 10 mg resulted in an 81% decrease in AUC∞ and a 56% decrease in Cmax
compared to oveporexton alone. The terminal half-life of oveporexton decreased from
approximately 32 hours to 14 hours in the presence of phenytoin. Based on these substantial
decreases in exposure, concomitant use of oveporexton with strong CYP3A inducers should
be avoided. In addition, PBPK modeling suggested that coadministration with moderate
CYP3A inducer will result in a 55% decrease in Cmax and 72% decrease in AUC∞. Therefore,
concomitant use of oveporexton with moderate CYP3A inducers should also be avoided.
PBPK modeling suggested that coadministration with weak CYP3A inhibitors and weak CYP3A
inducers will not have clinically significant effects on the PK of oveporexton. Therefore,
dosage adjustment is not required when oveporexton is concomitantly administered with
weak CYP3A inhibitors and weak CYP3A inducers.
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
The mechanism of action of oveporexton for the treatment of NT1 is presumed to be through
agonism of the orexin receptor 2 (OX2R). The orexin neuropeptide signaling system plays a
role in wakefulness.
Oveporexton showed dose proportional increase in exposures (Cmax and AUC0­t) at steady
state over the dosage range of 1 mg twice daily to 2 mg twice daily. With two times daily
administration, steady state was reached within 5 days, with an accumulation ratio of
approximately 1.6 after an oveporexton dosage of 1 mg twice daily to 2 mg twice daily.
Absorption
Oveporexton median (min, max) time to maximum plasma concentration (Tmax) is 1.5 hours
(1 to 4 hours) at steady state.
94
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Effect of Food: No clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of oveporexton
were observed following administration with a high-fat high-calorie meal.
Distribution
The apparent steady state volume of distribution of oveporexton is approximately 325 L.
Protein binding is high (>96%) and concentration-independent in human plasma. The whole
blood to plasma concentration ratio ranged from 0.61 to 0.90.
Elimination
The mean terminal elimination half-life of oveporexton is approximately 23.2 hours.
Metabolism: oveporexton is primarily eliminated by hepatic metabolism. CYP3A4 is the
predominant CYP enzyme involved in oveporexton metabolism with no major metabolites
identified in circulation.
Excretion: After a single 25-mg radiolabeled oral dose of oveporexton, 80.5% of the total dose
was recovered in feces and 11.0% was recovered in urine. Renal elimination of unchanged
drug accounts for <1% of the total clearance of oveporexton.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
General Dosing
The recommended dosage of oveporexton is 1 mg or 2 mg twice daily. The first dose should
be taken after awakening and the second dose should be taken 3 to 5 hours later.
The maximum recommended total daily dosage is 4 mg orally.
Therapeutic Individualization
Patients With Hepatic Impairment
Avoid use of oveporexton in patients with severe HI (Child-Pugh C). No dosage adjustment is
required when administering oveporexton to patients with mild or moderate HI (Child-Pugh A
or B).
Concomitant Use of CYP3A Inducers
Avoid use of oveporexton in patients taking strong and moderate CYP3A inducers.
Concomitant Use of CYP3A Inhibitors
Avoid use of oveporexton in patients taking strong CYP3A inhibitors. If oveporexton is used
concomitantly with moderate CYP3A inhibitors, reduce the oveporexton dosage to 0.5 mg
twice daily.

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
Clinical Pharmacology Questions
Are the proposed dosage regimens acceptable for the general patient population for which
the indication is being sought?
Yes, the proposed dosing regimens (1 mg or 2 mg twice daily) of oveporexton are acceptable
for the general patient population with narcolepsy type 1 (NT1). The proposed dosage
regimens of oveporexton showed favorable efficacy and acceptable safety compared to
placebo in the pivotal phase 3 efficacy and safety studies. Refer to Section 8 for additional
details about efficacy of 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID dosage regimens.
The clinical development program evaluated different dosing frequencies (once-daily (QD)
and twice-daily (BID)) to ensure sustained daytime wakefulness. Data from the phase 1b
study (TAK-861-1001) established that, although QD dosing improved symptoms, a BID splitdosing regimen was necessary to maintain efficacy throughout the entire waking day.
Pharmacodynamic (PD) measurements demonstrated that BID dosing yields significantly
stronger improvements in the Maintenance of Wakefulness Test (MWT) compared to QD
dosing. With the same total daily amount of oveporexton (4 mg/day), the 2 mg BID dosing
regimen achieved higher reductions in terms of MWT (24.39 versus 14.74) at Week 4
compared to 4 mg QD dosing (Table 41). In addition, the magnitude of effect is comparable
between the 2 mg BID and 4 mg BID cohorts, which suggests that by adopting a split-dosing
regimen, similar efficacy can be achieved with a lower total daily dosage. The BID
administration schedule provided sustained therapeutic plasma concentrations and symptom
control throughout the patient's waking hours, which provided superior efficacy compared to
a single-dose regimen with the same total daily dosage. Therefore, the BID dosing regimen
was selected for phase 2 and phase 3 studies.

The Applicant conducted a phase 2 dose-ranging study (TAK-861-2001) to support the 1-mg
and 2-mg twice-daily dosages for the subsequent phase 3 pivotal trials. Four dosage levels
were evaluated: 0.5 mg BID, 2 mg BID, 2 mg BID titrated to 5 mg BID (the 2 mg/5 mg group),
and 7 mg QD. In this study, oveporexton demonstrated statistically significant and clinically
meaningful improvements compared with placebo for the change from Baseline to Week 8 in
mean sleep latency on the MWT (p≤0.001 for all dosages) at all tested dosages. However,
0.5 mg BID showed relatively lower mean sleep latency compared to other dosages, and
comparable mean sleep latency (MSL) values were observed for the 2 mg BID group and the
2 mg/5 mg group. Increasing the dosage from 2 mg BID to 2 mg/5 mg per day yielded only an
additional 1.91 minutes of sleep latency on the MWT. This marginal gain was not considered
clinically significant. Administration of 7 mg QD resulted in relatively decreased change from
Baseline to Week 8 in mean sleep latency (19.5 min versus 27.6min (2 mg BID); 19.5 min
versus 30.7 min (2 mg/5 mg per day) compared to 2 mg BID and 2 mg/5 mg per day dosing.

A similar pattern is observed for weekly cataplexy rate (WCR), which is derived post hoc from
the sum of estimated daily counts over 2-week intervals divided by 2. For the four dosage
regimens tested (0.5 mg BID, 2 mg BID, 2 mg/5 mg BID, 7 mg QD) in the phase 2 study (TAK861-2001), only the 2 mg BID group and 2 mg/5 mg BID group showed statistically significant
improvement at Week 8 in terms of incidence rate ratios (IRRs) of WCR when compared to
the placebo group. Please see Section 8.1.2 for additional details. The results from this study
supported selection of 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID for subsequent two pivotal phase 3 studies.
These dosage regimens of oveporexton demonstrated favorable efficacy and acceptable

safety compared to placebo in the phase 3 studies and are deemed to be appropriate for the
general patient population with NT1.
Is there an alternative dosage regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (age, sex, race/ethnicity, body weight,
hepatic impairment and renal impairment)?
No, the population PK analysis and dedicated PK studies suggest that the intrinsic factors
(age, sex, race/ethnicity, body weight, HI (mild and moderate) and RI) did not appreciably
affect the PK of oveporexton. Therefore, no dosage adjustment is recommended based on
age, sex, race/ethnicity, body weight, HI, and RI in adult patients with NT1.
In Study TAK-861-1001 Part B and Part C, the Applicant compared the PK of oveporexton in
healthy elderly Japanese participants (aged 65 years and older) and in healthy Japanese
participants 18 to 55 years of age following 5 mg QD dosing for 14 days. The geometric mean
ratios of PK parameters (Cmax,ss and AUC0-24h) are similar between young adults and elderly
subjects following the administration of oveporexton 5 mg QD (Table 42). These findings are
consistent with the population PK analysis, where age was not a significant covariate in the
final model for oveporexton PK (Section 16.4.3 Population PK Review). Therefore, dosage
adjustment is not required in the elderly population for oveporexton.

Effect of Hepatic and Renal Impairment
Two dedicated safety and PK studies were conducted to evaluate the effect of mild (ChildPugh Class A) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) HI (TAK-861-1004), and severe RI (eGFR 15 to
<30 mL/min, not requiring dialysis; TAK-861-1005) on the PK of oveporexton along with
additional population PK analysis. Given that oveporexton is mainly metabolized by CYP3A4,
HI is expected to have a significant effect on its PK. However, the results from a dedicated HI
study suggest that oveporexton’s Cmax and AUC∞ increased by 16% and 28%, respectively, in
patients with mild HI, and 16% decrease in Cmax and 23% increase in AUC∞ in patients with
moderate HI, when compared to participants with normal hepatic function (Table 43).
Exposure-safety analyses suggest that the probability of adverse events (e.g., insomnia,
urinary events, and micturition urgency) in subjects with mild or moderate HI is generally
comparable to patients with normal hepatic functions (See Section 16.4.4 Pharmacometrics
Assessment for additional information). Therefore, the effect of HI (mild or moderate) on the
PK of oveporexton is not considered to be clinically meaningful. No dosage adjustment is
required for patients with mild or moderate HI.
100
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The effect of severe HI (Child-Pugh Class C) on the PK of oveporexton has not been studied.
Therefore, the review team recommends avoiding the use of oveporexton in patients with
severe HI given the potential risk of elevated systemic exposures and associated safety risks
to patients with NT1 and severe HI. A post-marketing requirement study to evaluate the
effects of severe HI (Child-Pugh Class C) on the PK of oveporexton will be issued.

a

In Study TAK-861-1005, subjects with severe RI (eGFR less than 30 mL/min, not on dialysis)
showed 16% lower Cmax and 45% higher AUC∞ compared to subjects with normal renal
function. Exposure-safety analyses suggest that the probability of adverse events in subjects
with severe RI is generally comparable to patients with normal renal functions (See Section
16.4.4 Pharmacometrics Assessment for additional information). Therefore, the effect of RI
(mild, moderate, or severe) on the PK of oveporexton is not considered to be clinically
meaningful, and dosage adjustment based on RI is not required for oveporexton.
Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the
appropriate management strategy?
No clinically relevant food effects on the PK of oveporexton were observed. However,
clinically significant drug-drug interactions were observed with strong and moderate CYP3A4
inhibitors and strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers.
Food Effects
The effect of high-fat meal on the PK of oveporexton was evaluated with the highest strength
(2 mg T3 tablet) of to-be-marketed formulation in Study TAK-861-1010. Twelve subjects
101
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
received oveporexton either following an overnight fast or a high-fat, high-calorie breakfast
(800 to 1000 calories and 50% or more fat) conditions. Similar PK characteristics were
observed for oveporexton under fasted and fed conditions.
Figure 9. Mean Plasma Oveporexton Concentration Versus Time Profiles After a Single Oral
Dose of 2 mg TAK-861 Under Fasted (Treatment a) and Fed (Treatment B) Conditions in
Healthy Adult Participants

Source: TAK-861-1010 CSR, Figure 2.

The results showed that the plasma PK parameters of oveporexton, Cmax, AUClast, and AUC∞
were approximately 3% lower, 5% higher, and 6% higher, respectively, after TAK-861 dosing
under fed conditions (Treatment B) compared to the fasted conditions (Treatment A). The
90% confidence intervals around the geometric mean ratios of PK parameters were within the
regulatory limits of 80.00% to 125.00%. Therefore, the effect of food on the PK of
oveporexton is deemed clinically not meaningful. Oveporexton can be administered with or
without food.

Drug-Drug Interactions (DDIs)
Given that CYP3A4 is the predominant contributor (97.3%), with lesser contributions from
CYP2C9 (0.1%) and CYP2D6 (2.6%), clinical DDI studies were conducted to evaluate the effects
of strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors and a strong CYP3A4 inducer on the PK of
oveporexton (TAK-861-1007). PBPK simulations were also performed to evaluate the effects
of weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 and moderate and weak inducers of CYP3A4.
Following coadministration of fluconazole (moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor) compared to
oveporexton alone, Cmax, AUC∞, and AUClast increased 50%, 2.8-fold, and 2.6-fold, respectively
(Table 45). A dosage reduction to 0.5 mg BID is recommended when oveporexton is
concomitantly administered with moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors. Coadministration of
oveporexton with itraconazole (strong CYP3A4 inhibitor) resulted in 67%, 7.4-fold, and 7.4fold increases in Cmax, AUC∞, and AUClast, respectively (Table 45) compared to oveporexton
administration alone. Therefore, avoiding the use of oveporexton with strong CYP3A4
inhibitors is recommended.

Physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling was utilized to evaluate the effect of
weak CYP3A4 inhibitor on the exposure of oveporexton. Coadministration of a weak CYP3A
inhibitor is predicted to resulted in a 30% and 43% increase in Cmax and AUC of oveporexton.
Based on the exposure-safety relationship, no dosage adjustment is required when
oveporexton is concomitantly administered with weak CYP3A4 inhibitors.
The effect of strong CYP3A4 inducer, phenytoin, on the PK of oveporexton was also evaluated
in Study TAK-861-1007. Coadministration of phenytoin (strong CYP3A4 inducer) with
oveporexton was associated with approximately 56%, 81%, and 80% lower Cmax, AUC∞, and
AUClast when compared to oveporexton alone (Table 46). Such lower systemic exposures are
anticipated to cause substantial loss of efficacy of oveporexton for the treatment of NT1.
Therefore, avoiding the use of oveporexton with strong CYP3A4 inducers is recommended.

PBPK model suggest that coadministration of a weak CYP3A-family inducer is predicted to

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
In addition, coadministration of a moderate CYP3A inducer is predicted to resulted in a 55%
and 72% decrease in Cmax and AUC of oveporexton (See Section 16.4.4 Pharmacometrics
Assessment for additional information). Such lower systemic exposures are anticipated to
cause substantial loss of efficacy of oveporexton for the treatment of NT1. Therefore,
avoiding the use of oveporexton with moderate CYP3A4 inducers is recommended.
Oveporexton is not expected to directly induce or inhibit CYP enzymes at clinically relevant
concentrations (unbound Cmax at steady state of the maximum recommended dosage, 2 mg
BID: 0.46 ng/mL) based on the in vitro DDI study. Furthermore, PBPK modeling also suggest
that the risk of oveporexton to decrease or increase systemic exposures of CYP3A substrates
via CYP3A induction or inhibition is considered very low. Therefore, oveporexton is not
expected to affect the PK of other drugs when concomitantly administered.
Is the PK bridge between the to-be-marketed formulation and clinical trial formulations
adequately established?
Yes, the PK bridge between the to-be-marked formulation and clinical trial formulations is
adequately established.
In the development of oveporexton, three clinical trial formulations were administered to
participants, including T1 tablet (phase 1 studies), T2 tablet (phase 2 dose ranging study and
clinical drug-drug interaction studies), and T3 tablet (phase 3 studies, food effect study, and
hepatic and renal impairment studies) formulations. The to-be-marketed formulation shares
the same core tablet composition as the T3 formulation. However, the color coating and
debossing of the to-be-marketed formulation is different from the T3 formulation. The
Applicant demonstrated a bridge between the to-be-marketed formulation and T3
formulation based on the in vitro dissolution study. The results from the in vitro dissolution
study suggest that the in vitro dissolution characteristics across all strengths (0.5 mg, 1 mg,
and 2 mg) are similar between the to-be-marketed formulation and T3 formulation. This
suggests that the bridge between the to-be-marketed formulation and T3 formulation is
adequate. See the Integrated Review of Office of Pharmaceutical Quality archived on June 24,
2026, for additional information.
A comparative bioavailability study (TAK-861-1007) was conducted to establish a PK bridge
between T1 and T2 formulations, and a population PK (popPK) model-based approach was
used to establish a bridge between T2 and T3 formulations along with the results of an in
vitro dissolution study.

As shown in the table above, the exposures following administration of T1 and T2
formulations are comparable. The 90% confidence intervals of the GMRs of the PK
parameters were generally within the 80% to 125% interval except for the upper limit of the
90% CI for Cmax. However, such minor increase in upper 90% CI of Cmax is not expected to be
clinically significant. Therefore, the bridge between the T1 and T2 formulations is deemed to
be adequate.
The T3 formulation is further bridged to T2 formulation via a population PK model-based
approach. PK data from studies TAK-861-1007, TAK-861-1010, and TAK-861-1011 were used
for the comparison of population PK model-simulated oveporexton concentration-time
profiles with observed oveporexton concentration-time profiles. The results showed that the
exposures (Cavg,ss and Cmax,ss) following the administration of T2 and T3 formulations are
comparable. In addition, in vitro dissolution study showed that T3 and T2 formulation exhibit
similar dissolution profiles, with f2 values ≥50 in three different pH dissolution media (refer to
the Integrated Review of Office of Pharmaceutical Quality dated June 24, 2026). The findings
support the bridge between T2 and T3 formulations.

Were there any QT prolongations observed at the highest therapeutic dosage (i.e., 2 mg
BID) or supratherapeutic dosage (i.e., 75 mg QD)?
Oveporexton did not prolong the QT interval or affect the electrocardiogram (ECG)
parameters at the maximum recommended therapeutic dosage, 2 mg BID.
No dedicated thorough QT study was conducted for oveporexton. The effect of oveporexton
on QTc interval was evaluated in Study TAK-861-1001. The highest exposures evaluated was
achieved with a 75-mg single oral dose and this dosage regimen provided an 11-fold coverage
for the maximum proposed therapeutic dosage (2 mg BID) based on Cmax values (See
Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies QT Study Review dated December
18, 2024, for additional information). These results provided adequate coverage over the
highest exposure scenario in clinical settings.

Does oveporexton exert dose-proportional PK characteristics?
Yes. Following single-dose and multiple-dose (i.e., at steady-state) administration in healthy
adults, oveporexton showed dose proportional increase plasma exposures (Cmax and AUC)
over the dose range of 1 to 75 mg.

7 Sources of Clinical Data and Review Strategy
Table of Clinical Studies
Clinical trial efficacy and safety data for the treatment of NT1 in adults are provided by two
phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies TAK-861-3001 and TAK-8613002, and the long-term extension phase 2/3 study TAK-861-2003. Additional supportive data
are provided by the phase 2 Study TAK-861-2001. Safety data from the phase 2 study TAK861-2002 in patients with NT2, as well as safety data from phase 1 studies, are included in the
integrated summary of safety to provide a comprehensive assessment of the safety profile of
TAK-861 across the clinical development program. See Table 51 for a listing of all clinical trial
sources of safety and efficacy data.

Review Strategy
The efficacy review focuses on Study TAK-861-3001 and Study TAK-861-3002, the phase 3
randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter trials of oveporexton in patients
16 years of age and older with NT1; the Applicant also references these phase 3 studies in
draft labeling. The Applicant has submitted studies TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002 as
(b)
substantial evidence of effectiveness of oveporexton for the treatment of NT1 in patients (4)
Additional supportive clinical efficacy data for NT1 are provided by the phase
2 Study TAK-861-2001. The biometrics reviewer for this application has conducted the
primary efficacy analysis review and also reviewed the Applicant’s statistical analyses. The
review Division consulted the Division of Clinical Outcome Assessment (DCOA) for review of
the clinical outcome assessments used in the efficacy trials.
The safety review includes detailed safety analyses of the two aforementioned phase 3
efficacy studies, TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002, given their placebo-controlled data. The
clinical reviewer also performed a detailed review of phase 2/3 study TAK-861-2003, which
provides information about long-term safety. For each of these studies, the clinical reviewer
reviewed the study reports, adverse event (AE) database, laboratory database,
electrocardiogram (ECG) database, vital sign database, and participants’ scores on the
Columbia Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS). In addition, the clinical reviewer reviewed
the Applicant’s integrated summary of safety (ISS) database, which includes data from
additional narcolepsy trials as well as trials of oveporexton in healthy volunteers, for serious
adverse events (SAEs). The clinical reviewer also considered the Applicant’s draft label, and
the most recent Periodic Benefit Risk Evaluation Report (PBER).
The Division consulted the Drug-Induced Liver Injury (DILI) team in the Division of Hepatology
and Nutrition (DHN) and the Division of Urology, Obstetrics, and Gynecology (DUOG) for
expert review of hepatic and urological treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs). The
Division consulted the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies (QT/IRT) for expert
review of the drug effect on QT interval based on data from the development program. FDA
Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) were consulted for review of data related to human abuse
liability potential. The Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health (DPMH) were consulted on
issues related to use in pregnancy and lactation and provided language for labeling consistent
with the Pregnancy and Lactation Labeling Rule (PLLR).

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

8 Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Study TAK-861-3001
8.1.1.1.

Study Design

Overview and Objective
Study Title
“A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of
TAK-861 for the Treatment of Narcolepsy with Cataplexy (Narcolepsy Type 1)”
Primary Objectives
The study’s primary objective was to assess the efficacy of oveporexton compared to placebo
with regard to EDS as measured by the MWT.
Secondary Objectives
Secondary objectives included assessment of between-group difference in:
•

Changes in EDS as measured by the Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS) total score

•

Changes in cataplexy episodes as measured by the weekly cataplexy rate (WCR)

•

Changes in sustained attention as measured by the psychomotor vigilance test (PVT)

•

Changes in overall narcolepsy symptoms measured by the Patient Global Impression of
Change (PGI-C) scale

•

Changes in overall severity of narcolepsy symptoms measured by the Narcolepsy Severity
Scale for Clinical Trials (NSS-CT)

•

Changes in functional impacts of narcolepsy as assessed by the Functional Impacts of
Narcolepsy Instrument (FINI)

•

Changes in quality of life of participants, as assessed by the Short Form-36 Survey (SF-36).

Trial Design
Study Design Overview
Study TAK-861-3001 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 12-week
parallel-group trial to assess the safety and efficacy of two oral dosages of oveporexton for
the treatment of NT1.

115
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The study began with a screening period of up to 50 days, which could be extended on a caseby-case basis with Sponsor approval. During screening, participants were required to
discontinue their narcolepsy medications, including antidepressants and sodium or multisalt
oxybate. Starting no later than Day -16, participants completed an electronic diary to track
cataplexy episodes. To be eligible, participants had to complete the diary for at least 11 of the
14 days between Day -16 and Day -3 and had to experience at least four partial and/or
complete cataplexy episodes per week during this period.
Throughout the study, participants were required to stay overnight at the study site on three
occasions: Days -2 to 1 (with one mandatory overnight at Day -2 and one optional overnight
at Day -1), Days 55 to 56, and Days 83 to 84. After completing the 12-week treatment period,
participants had the option to enroll in a long-term extension study under a separate protocol
(Study TAK-861-2003). Those who chose not to participate in the extension study completed
two follow-up visits at approximately 7 and 28 days after their final dose of study drug and
could restart their restricted medications (except those associated with liver function test
elevations) 7 days after the last dose. Participants who terminated the trial early were
required to complete an early termination visit as soon as possible and a follow-up visit
approximately 28 days after the last dose of trial intervention.
For a schematic of the study design, see Figure 11.

Trial Location
This multi-center study was conducted at 41 sites: 20 trial sites in Europe, 12 trial sites in
North America, and 9 trial sites in Asia Pacific.
Diagnostic Criteria
The diagnosis of NT1 for participant screening was based on criteria from either the
International Classification of Sleep Disorders, Third Edition (ICSD-3) or its revised version
(ICSD-3-Text-Revised, ICSD-3-TR). According to ICSD-3 or ICSD-3-TR, both Criteria A and B
must be met for a diagnosis of NT1. Criterion A required that participants experience daily
periods of irrepressible need to sleep or daytime lapses into sleep occurring for at least 3
months. Criterion B required the presence of one or both of the following: (1) cataplexy with
a mean sleep latency of ≤8 minutes and two or more SOREMPs on an MSLT performed
according to standard techniques (a SOREMP within 15 minutes of sleep onset on the
preceding nocturnal polysomnogram could replace one of the SOREMPs on the MSLT), or (2)
CSF hypocretin-1 concentration measured by immunoreactivity of either ≤110 pg/mL or less
than one-third of mean values obtained in normal subjects with the same standardized assay.

117
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Key Eligibility Criteria
Key inclusion criteria were:
Besides having an ICSD-3 or ICSD-3-TR diagnosis of NT1 as previously described, for
enrollment participants must have had the following:
•

Age 18 to 70 years (inclusive). Participants aged 16 and 17 years could be included in some
countries/trial sites (United States, Japan).

•

Participants were required to have an ESS score of ≥11 at screening. If a participant had an
ESS score of <11 at screening but was taking medication to treat NT1 symptoms, the ESS
could be repeated after discontinuation or tapering of medications, prior to the Day -2 visit.
The ESS is an 8-question self-report measure that asks patients to rate the likelihood that
they would fall asleep while doing common daily activities, including sitting and reading,
watching television, sitting in a public place, riding in a car or on public transport, lying
down to rest in the afternoon, sitting and talking to someone, sitting quietly after lunch, and
sitting in a stopped car. Each item on the ESS is rated from 0 (would never doze) to 3 (high
chance of dozing), with a maximum total score of 24. An ESS score higher than 10 suggests
that the subject is experiencing higher than average, or mild-to-moderate, sleepiness during
daily activities.

•

Participants had ≥4 partial or complete episodes of cataplexy per week, calculated as the
weekly average over the 14-day period from Days -16 to -3 of the screening period.
Participants must have completed self-reported cataplexy questions in the e-diary for at
least 11 of 14 days during Days -16 to -3 to be considered compliant.

•

Participant positive for the HLA genotype HLA-DQB1*06:02 (positive results for either
homozygous or heterozygous alleles were considered "positive" and acceptable) or results
from radioimmunoassay indicated the participant's CSF orexin/hypocretin-1 concentration
was ≤110 pg/mL (or less than one-third of the mean values obtained in normal participants
within the same standardized assay).

Key exclusion criteria were:
•

The participant had a current medical disorder, other than narcolepsy with cataplexy,
associated with EDS (e.g., restless leg syndrome) or apnea-hypopnea index (AHI) ≥15 on Day
-2 nocturnal polysomnography (nPSG). Of note, participants with moderate-to-severe
obstructive sleep apnea could be eligible if they were compliant with treatment and had an
AHI <15 on treatment. The AHI measures the average number of apnea (complete breathing
pauses) and hypopnea (partial obstruction) events per hour of sleep, used to diagnose and
grade OSA. A normal range for adults is less than 5 events per hour, with 5 to 15 classified
as mild.

•

The participant: (a) had a history of myocardial infarction; (b) had a history of clinically
significant hepatic disease, thyroid disease, coronary artery disease, cardiac rhythm
abnormality or heart failure; or (c) had any medical condition (such as unstable
cardiovascular, pulmonary, renal or gastrointestinal disease, or an ongoing condition
118

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
causing bladder outlet obstruction or urinary retention [e.g., benign prostatic hyperplasia or
pelvic organ prolapse with obstructive voiding symptoms, neurogenic bladder, or urethral
stricture]) that would preclude enrollment in the view of the investigator.
•

The participant had a clinically significant history of head injury or head trauma.

•

The participant had a history of epilepsy, seizure, or convulsion (except for a single febrile
seizure in childhood).

•

The participant had a history of cerebral ischemia, transient ischemic attack (<5 years from
screening), intracranial aneurysm, or arteriovenous malformation.

•

Participants were excluded if they had one or more of the following psychiatric disorders: a
current or past history of manic or hypomanic episodes, schizophrenia, any other psychotic
disorder, obsessive compulsive disorder, intellectual disability, organic mental disorders, or
mental disorders due to a general medical condition as defined in the DSM-5; a current
diagnosis or history of substance use disorder as defined in the DSM-5 (though participants
with a history of substance use disorder more than 12 months before baseline could be
enrolled after consultation with the Sponsor); a current active major depressive episode or
an active major depressive episode in the past 6 months; or any current unstable psychiatric
disorder. Neurodevelopmental disorders such as attention deficit hyperactivity disorder
were not excluded unless their severity prevented termination of prohibited medications.

•

The participant was unable to refrain from or anticipated using excluded food products,
beginning by Day -7 and continuing until 7 days after the last dose, or prohibited
medications (including known cytochrome P450 type 3A inducers and moderate and strong
inhibitors).

•

Blood pressure >140 mm Hg (systolic) or >90 mm Hg (diastolic) during screening

•

Resting heart rate <40 or >100 beats per minute during screening

•

QTcF >450 milliseconds (genetically male) or >470 milliseconds (genetically female) on
screening ECG

•

Positive test for hepatitis B surface antigen, hepatitis B core antibody, hepatitis C virus
antibody, or HIV antibody/antigen

•

Renal creatinine clearance ≤30 mL/min

•

ALT or AST values >1.5 times the upper limit of normal or GGT >3 times the upper limit of
normal

•

Positive pregnancy test at screening or Day -2

•

Breastfeeding

•

Positive urine screen for drugs of abuse and/or positive alcohol test (with exceptions for
prescribed medications and CBD products)

•

Excessive caffeine consumption (>600 mg per day)

119
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
•

Alcohol consumption exceeding 2 standard drinks per day on average

•

Nicotine dependence likely to affect sleep or unwillingness to refrain from smoking during
confinement

•

Considered at imminent risk of suicide or injury to self/others, attempted suicide within
past year, or positive answers on Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS) Items 4 or
5 within past year

•

Study site employee or immediate family member/dependent of study site employee

•

Unwillingness to refrain from driving/operating dangerous machinery during sleepiness or
medication changes

•

Usual bedtime later than midnight, nighttime/variable shift work within past 6 months, or
recent/planned travel with significant jet lag

•

Unlikely to comply with protocol or unsuitable per investigator opinion

•

Considered vulnerable as defined by local regulations.

Overall, the inclusion and exclusion criteria are acceptable. The Agency agreed to the
inclusion of participants 16 to 17 years of age at the Type C meeting held on December 13,
2023. The adequacy of the small number of pediatric subjects 16 to 17 years enrolled to
support generalization of the results to the pediatric population 16 to 17 years is one focus of
this review. The ESS score threshold of ≥11 and the WCR requirement of ≥4 episodes per
week are acceptable, as these criteria ensured enrollment of participants with at least mild
NT1. The use of ICSD-3 diagnostic criteria is appropriate given that these criteria require
objective evaluation of NT1 through either polysomnography or CSF orexin level
measurement. The allowance for mild hypopnea (AHI 5 to 15) is acceptable. The liver function
assessment requirements, including the thresholds for ALT, AST, and GGT values, are in
accordance with recommendations from the DILI team, which was consulted during the
development program. For additional details regarding hepatic safety monitoring, see the
dedicated DILI team review.
Study Treatments, Dosage Selection, and Compliance
At the beginning of the 12-week-treatment period, on Day 1 participants were randomized in
a 2:3:3 randomization ratio to receive:
•

Matching placebo tablet, 1 placebo tablet BID, at least 3 hours apart

•

Oveporexton tablet, 1 mg tablet BID, at least 3 hours apart

•

Oveporexton tablet, 2 mg tablet BID, at least 3 hours apart

120
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Starting on the morning of Day 1, the study drug was administered at approximately the same
time each day for 12 weeks, with the following schedules:
•

For all days during the treatment period except clinic MWT days, participants were
instructed to take the first dose no later than 10 am and the second dose at least 3 hours
after the first morning dose, but no later than 1 pm.

•

On clinic MWT days (Days 1, 56, and 84), the drug was administered as close to 8 am and 11
am as possible, respectively.

Investigators assessed compliance based on tablet dispensation and return for participants in
the safety set. Compliance percentage was calculated as:

Concomitant Medications
Any medication or supplement used for the treatment of NT1 was required to be
discontinued. Drugs associated with a risk of causing severe HI were prohibited. Known
inducers and moderate or strong cytochrome P450 type 3A inhibitors were also excluded.
The medication taper plan took into consideration the medication pharmacokinetics (e.g.,
half-life), as illustrated in Table 52.

Meals and Dietary Restrictions/Activity
Grapefruit and Seville oranges (including juices) were prohibited from Day -7 until 7 days after
the last dose; alcohol was restricted 3 days before check-ins/visits and limited to ≤2 drinks per
day at other times (or per local guidelines if more conservative); caffeine was prohibited on
nPSG/MWT days and limited to ≤600 mg/day otherwise; and nicotine smoking was allowed
outside the center but prohibited during confinement periods, with all restrictions subject to
local legal age requirements. Participants abstained from strenuous exercise for 72 hours
before check-in until checkout for all study visits, could engage in usual activities at other
times except driving or operating hazardous machinery during periods of heightened
sleepiness/fatigue or medication weaning/changes, and were required to follow local
regulations and investigator guidance on driving restrictions.
Subject Completion, Dose Discontinuation, and Withdrawal
Subjects were considered to have completed the double-blind portion of the study if they
completed Week 12.
Dosage adjustment was not allowed in the study. Discontinuation of participants from
treatment or the study could occur for the following reasons: voluntary withdrawal of
consent, loss to follow-up, pregnancy, major protocol deviations, appearance of an AE that
would impose an unacceptable risk to the participant's health if participation continued (such
as clinically significant LFT abnormalities, suicidal ideation/behavior as indicated by C-SSRS
item 4 or 5 "yes" response or per investigator's judgment, or BP and HR increases, such as
SBP >160 mm Hg, DBP >100 mm Hg, or HR >100 bpm, or the participant was unwilling to
continue because of the AE.
Participants who discontinued study drug were asked to remain in the study for all protocolspecified visits (especially Week 12), were required to have blood chemistry performed at
approximately 2 weeks and 28 days after the last dose and did not need follow-up visits if
they completed the Week 12 visit and had discontinued study drug more than 28 days prior.
Schedule of Assessments
All participants checked into the clinic in the afternoon on Days -2, 55, and 83. Discharge
occurred after the last study assessment of the last day was performed.
See Table 53 and Table 54 for the Study TAK-861-3001 schedule of assessment and
procedures.
