Administrative Structure
According to the Applicant, an oveporexton program-level external Data Monitoring
Committee (DMC) reviewed unblinded safety data quarterly and had access to data from the
trial for safety assessment.
Per Applicant’s report, a Liver Safety Evaluation Committee (LSEC), independent from the
DMC, reviewed any cases in which there was concern for possible DILI, trial intervention
discontinuations due to liver-related AEs, and liver-related SAEs.
Trial sites were monitored by
Monitoring visits were conducted as
directed by the site monitoring plan, and documentation of the visits was completed and
filed. Monitors were provided access to participant and site records to review adherence to
the protocol; compliance with GCP; and the completeness, accuracy, and consistency of the
data.
(b) (4)

A Sponsor representative trained the investigators to comply with GCP and to conduct the
trial in accordance with the trial protocol.
The investigator or designee was to maintain 100% accountability for all trial interventions
during his or her entire participation in the trial.
Each clinical trial site was responsible for performing internal quality management of trial
conduct, data, and biological specimen collection, documentation, and completion. Quality
control procedures and a risk assessment plan were to be developed and followed.
Accredited central laboratories were used to ensure consistency of analyses and reporting of
results for clinical laboratory tests. Where local laboratories were used, their participation in
internal and external quality control, quality assurance, and accreditation schemes were
evaluated by the trial monitors.
Audits of the investigator trial sites and other facilities used during the trial were included as
part of the independent Takeda quality assessment performed by Takeda or designees.
A designated vendor generated the randomization schedule using the permuted block
method with the stratification factors (region and prior use of medication for NT1) and
randomization ratio for the IRT system.
Summary
In general, the trial appears adequately planned. During the development program, the
Applicant explored several dosages in the NT1 population and selected the dosages for the
current study based on efficacy and safety/tolerability measures from the phase 2 trial (TAK861-2001). At the EOP2 meeting held on June 12, 2024, the Applicant explained that the
129
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
0.5 mg BID dosage in the phase 2 study showed an effect on the endpoint measuring
cataplexy rate but did not survive multiplicity correction. Therefore, they planned to conduct
the phase 3 studies with a 1 mg BID dosage arm of TAK-861 (instead of 0.5 mg BID),
anticipating a positive result on both measures of EDS and cataplexy rate. On the other hand,
they did not plan to include an arm with the highest dosage used in phase 2 (2 mg + 5 mg 3
hours apart) because, although it showed positive results on both EDS and cataplexy
endpoints, it increased AEs by more than 20% with minimal improvement in endpoints
compared to the lower dosages. The dosage selection appears adequate, and the Applicant's
dosage strategy appears reasonable.
The randomization ratio of 2:3:3 (placebo: oveporexton 1 mg BID: oveporexton 2 mg BID)
appears acceptable. Per Biometrics, this allocation provides adequate sample sizes for both
dosage groups while maintaining a placebo control group (which is reasonable given that NT1
is a rare disease)—maximizing the number of participants receiving active treatment (helping
optimize the safety information gained from the limited patient population) and minimizing
the number of participants receiving placebo.
The procedures for maintaining the blind appear adequate, with all participants, irrespective
of dosage level, receiving the same number of tablets and bottles.
Excluding all other medications that could have affected EDS or cataplexy appears adequate
to isolate oveporexton's effect. The exclusion of medications associated with causing severe
HI was appropriate during the development program, considering the cases of DILI observed
with a drug of similar chemical structure (firazorexton).
The establishment of an independent DMC for quarterly review of unblinded safety data and
a LSEC to review potential DILI cases and liver-related adverse events was appropriate and
strengthened the safety oversight of this program, particularly given the hepatotoxicity
concerns from the structurally related compound firazorexton.
Study Endpoints
Primary Endpoint
The primary endpoint was the change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency during
four Maintenance of Wakefulness Test (MWT) trials.
MWT is an objective measure used to assess a person's ability to remain awake during
soporific (sleep-inducing) conditions. In the context of NT1, the MWT is used to evaluate the
severity of EDS and the effectiveness of treatments aimed at promoting wakefulness. The test
consists of four trials conducted throughout the day at 2-hour intervals (see Table 54). During
each trial, the participant is connected to polysomnography (PSG) electrodes that
continuously monitor brain waves, eye movements, breathing, heart rate, and muscle tone to
objectively detect sleep onset. The participant sits in a comfortable, semi-reclined position in
a quiet, dimly lit room and is instructed to try to stay awake for up to 40 minutes. The trial
ends either when the participant falls asleep (defined by specific PSG criteria showing sleep
onset as recorded by the electrodes) or when the maximum time is reached if the participant
130
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
remains awake. The primary outcome is the mean sleep latency across all trials—that is, the
average time it takes for the participant to fall asleep as objectively determined by the PSG
recordings. Longer sleep latencies indicate a greater ability to maintain wakefulness,
suggesting better control of EDS. In NT1 patients, baseline MWT sleep latencies are typically
very short, reflecting their severe difficulty staying awake. Effective treatments should
increase these latencies, demonstrating improved wakefulness.
Secondary Endpoints
The multiplicity protected secondary endpoints were:
•

Weekly Cataplexy Rate (WCR): Irregular Sleep-Wake Rhythm Rate (IRR) of WCR at Week 12
(i.e., treatment effect of oveporexton compared to placebo on cataplexy as measured by
the WCR at Week 12).
- WCR was derived from the partial and full episodes of cataplexy reported by
participants in the eDiary daily throughout the trial, averaged over 2-week periods.
Specifically, participants completed a daily electronic patient-reported outcome
(ePRO) diary to record the cataplexy episodes (both partial and full) that they
experienced the previous day, and the day before the previous day if the diary from
the previous day was not completed. WCR at baseline was derived as the average
number of cataplexy episodes over a consecutive 2-week period prior to the Day 1
visit, adjusting for non-missing diary days. WCR was calculated every 2 weeks
(Baseline, Week 2, Week 4, Week 6, Week 8, Week 10, and Week 12) as:
(Total number of cataplexy episodes over the non-missing diary days for a
given 2 weeks / number of non-missing diary days in these 2 weeks) × 7
- Cataplexy episodes attributed to any day after end-of-study (EOS) day were not
included in WCR calculation. In the event that a participant completed their Week 12
visit but the visit did not fall exactly on Day 84, the last two-week period for WCR
calculation was the period from Day 71 to the earliest of Day 84 and their EOS day. If a
diary attributed to a given day reported 0 or more episodes of cataplexy, the day was
counted as a non-missing diary day. If a given day did not have any diary entry
attributed to it, then it was counted as a missing diary day.

•

The Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS): Change from Baseline to Week 12 in ESS total score.
- ESS is a validated, self-administered questionnaire used to measure subjective
daytime sleepiness. The ESS asks participants to rate their likelihood of dozing off or
falling asleep in eight different everyday situations, such as sitting and reading,
watching television, sitting inactive in a public place, or as a passenger in a car. Each
situation is rated on a 4-point scale from 0 (would never doze) to 3 (high chance of
dozing). The total ESS score ranges from 0 to 24, with higher scores indicating greater
subjective sleepiness. In NT1 patients, baseline ESS scores are typically elevated,
reflecting their severe EDS. A reduction in ESS score following treatment indicates
improvement in subjective daytime sleepiness. In this study, the ESS was administered
to assess sleep propensity on selected days indicated in the Schedule of Assessment
131

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Table 53. Participants was asked to evaluate their subjective sleepiness based on
recalling their most recent daily life experiences.
•

Patient Global Impression of Change (PGI-C): PGI-C score at Week 12.
- The PGI-C is a patient-reported assessment that captured the overall change in a
participant's narcolepsy symptoms as perceived by the participant compared to
baseline. The Patient Global Impression rating scales are designed to capture the
global effect of treatment on daytime sleepiness and overall narcolepsy symptoms
from the patient's perspective. The PGI-C measured change due to treatment relative
to Baseline using a 7-point scale ranging from "very much improved" to "very much
worse." Per the Applicant’s report, instructions were provided to participants to help
ensure consistency in the assessments.

•

Narcolepsy Severity Scale for Clinical Trials (NSS-CT): Change from Baseline to Week 12 in
NSS-CT total score.
- The NSS-CT is a 15-item self-administered questionnaire that assessed the severity and
consequences of the five major narcolepsy symptoms: daytime sleepiness, cataplexy,
hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and disrupted nighttime sleep (DNS). The total score
ranged from 0 to 57, with higher scores indicating greater symptom severity. In a
validation study by Dauvilliers et al., the mean symptom number, NSS-CT total score,
and number of narcolepsy symptoms were statistically significantly different between
treated and untreated patients, and the symptom number was associated with
diagnosis delay, age at onset, and ESS and Beck Depression Inventory scores
(Dauvilliers et al. 2020). This patient-reported outcome measure may provide a
comprehensive assessment of the full spectrum of narcolepsy symptoms and their
impact on patients.

•

The Functional Impacts of Narcolepsy Instrument (FINI): Change from Baseline to Week 12
in all six FINI domain scores (Tiredness, Cognitive Functioning, Cataplexy, Social Activities,
Everyday Activities, and Everyday Responsibilities).
- FINI is a 28-item instrument developed by the Applicant to measure the functional
impacts of narcolepsy across six domains that the Applicant reports are meaningful to
narcolepsy patients: Tiredness (items 1 to 7), Cognitive Functioning (items 8 to 12),
Cataplexy (items 13 to 17), Social Activities (items 18 to 21), Everyday Activities (items
22 to 25), and Everyday Responsibilities (items 26 to 28). Each item asked participants
about the impact that narcolepsy had on their daily functioning during the past 7 days
and was scored from 0 to 4, where 0 indicated the best health and 4 the worst. There
was no total score; for each participant, an average score was calculated for each
domain if at least half the items in the domain had been answered by summing the
responses in the domain and dividing by the number of completed items. The average
domain score was then standardized to a 0 to 100 scale, where 0 indicated the best
health and 100 the worst health, calculated as (average score/4) × 100. A decrease
from Baseline indicated improved health.

132
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
•

Short Form-36 Survey (SF-36): Change from Baseline to Week 12 in SF-36 mental and
physical component summary scores.
- The SF-36 is a 36-item, participant-reported survey that assessed participant health
across eight scaled domains: vitality, physical functioning, bodily pain, general health
perceptions, physical role functioning, emotional role functioning, social role
functioning, and mental health. Each scale was directly transformed into a 0 to 100
scale, where higher scores indicated less disability and better health (0 = maximum
disability; 100 = no disability). When scores for all eight scales were available, two
summary component scores were derived: the Physical Health Component Score and
the Mental Health Component Score, both positively scored so that higher scores
represented better health-related quality of life. This generic health status measure
provided a broad assessment of the impact of the disorder and its treatment on
overall physical and mental well-being.

•

Psychomotor Vigilance Test (PVT): Change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean number of
lapses on the PVT.
- The PVT is a simple reaction performance task that aims to measure sustained
attention with no learning effects over repeated administration. According to the
Applicant, the PVT is sensitive to the effects of total sleep loss, partial sleep restriction,
naps, and circadian variation in the healthy population. The duration of the test is 10
minutes.

Exploratory Endpoints:
•

NSS-CT individual question scores: change from Baseline to Week 12 was analyzed
separately for EDS, cataplexy, hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and DNS.

•

Nocturnal Polysomnography (nPSG): The nPSG is a standardized procedure used to assess
various sleep parameters, including sleep latency and sleep architecture. In this study, nPSG
was used to evaluate the effects of oveporexton on sleep latency and nighttime sleep
architecture. Participants with narcolepsy have disrupted nighttime sleep, with frequent
awakenings and shorter REM latency that is associated with sleep paralysis and
hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations. In this study, there was an exploratory evaluation
for changes in nighttime sleep metrics after oveporexton multiple dosing. The raw
electroencephalogram data obtained in this study could be used for an exploratory
evaluation of pharmacodynamic effects. Participants who used continuous PAP regularly at
home were instructed to use continuous PAP during the nPSG.

•

Clinical Global Impression Scale-Change (CGI-C) and Clinical Global Impression Scale-Severity
(CGI-S): The CGI provides an overall clinician-determined summary measure of a
participant's treatment experience that takes into account all available information,
including knowledge of the participant's history, psychosocial circumstances, symptoms,
behavior, and the impact of the symptoms on the participant's ability to function. The CGI-C

133
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
and CGI-S serve as global clinician-reported assessments of symptom severity and change of
symptoms (relative to baseline), respectively.
- The CGI-S assesses the severity of overall narcolepsy symptoms using a 7-point Likerttype scale ranging from "normal, not at all ill" to "among the most extremely ill."
- The CGI-C is a clinician-rated assessment that evaluates the overall change in a
participant's narcolepsy symptoms compared to Baseline. The CGI-C uses a 7-point
Likert-type scale ranging from "very much improved" to "very much worse" to rate the
extent to which participants' current overall narcolepsy symptoms had changed since
Baseline.
The Applicant stated that clinicians were trained on what aspects to take into consideration
when rating the CGI-C to ensure consistency of responses. These global assessments
complemented objective and patient-reported measures by incorporating the clinician's
comprehensive evaluation of treatment response across all aspects of the participant's
condition.
•

Patient Global Impression-Severity (PGI-S) and Patient Global Impression-Change (PGI-C):
The PGI-S requires the participant to rate his/her disease severity at the time of assessment
on a 5-point scale ranging from “none” to “very severe.” The PGI-C measures change due to
treatment relative to baseline on a 7-point scale ranging from “very much improved” to
“very much worse”.

•

Daily e-Diary: Participants completed a daily e-diary to record self-reported total sleep time,
cataplexy events, and sleep quality. In cases where the e-diary became unavailable, a site
could use alternative methods to collect these data with approval from the Sponsor or
designee.

•

Cogstate Battery—Continuous Paired Associate Learning (CPAL), One Back Test (ONB), and
International Digit Symbol Substitution Test-Symbols (iDSST-s): These cognitive tests
measured visual associate memory (CPAL), working memory (ONB), and processing speed
(iDSST-s) to assess the impact of narcolepsy and treatment on cognitive functioning.

•

EuroQol 5-Dimension, 5-Level (EQ-5D-5L): The EQ-5D-5L is a standardized, generic (nondisease-specific), participant-reported measure of health-related quality of life. The
descriptive system comprises five dimensions (mobility, self-care, usual activities,
pain/discomfort, and anxiety/depression), each rated on a five-level severity scale, from
which dimension scores can be presented as a health profile or converted to a single
summary utility index. Participants also self-rate their overall health on a visual analog scale
(VAS) ranging from 0 to 100 (where 100 is the best imaginable health state).

•

Treatment Satisfaction Questionnaire for Medication (TSQM) is a generic patient-reported
outcome assessment that measured satisfaction with treatment across four dimensions:
effectiveness, side effects, convenience, and global satisfaction, with scores ranging from 0
to 100 where higher scores indicated better treatment satisfaction.

•

The British Columbia Cognitive Complaints Inventory-Extended (BC-CCI-E) is a 9-item scale
that assessed perceived cognitive difficulties and their functional and quality of life impacts,
134

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
with total scores ranging from 0 to 18 where higher scores indicated greater impact of
cognitive difficulties.
•

The Karolinska Sleepiness Scale (KSS) is a 10-point, self-rating scale that measured
subjective sleepiness at a particular moment, with higher scores indicating greater
sleepiness; it was administered prior to the PVT to assess the participant's arousal state at
the time of assessment.

Statistical Analysis Plan
The primary endpoint of MWT was derived from four MWT sessions (10 am, 12 pm, 2 pm, and
4 pm) recorded on Day -1, Week 8 (Day 56), and Week 12 (Day 84) for each subject using the
mean of the sleep latency (SL). If there were two or more missing values, the mean was
assigned as missing for that day. The change from Baseline for the mean of the four MWT
session was derived for each subject at each post-dose visit.
The primary estimand was defined by the Applicant as:
•

Treatment: TAK-861 (two dosage levels: 1 mg BID or 2 mg BID) or placebo.

•

Population: Targeted patient population as defined through the inclusion/exclusion criteria.

•

Endpoint: Change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency from the MWT.

•

Population level summary: The mean of the endpoint was estimated for each treatment
group and comparison to placebo was based on the difference in means between treatment
groups.

•

Strategy for Addressing Intercurrent Events: The treatment policy strategy was used to
address all intercurrent events. The anticipated intercurrent events included premature
discontinuation of study drug. Participants who discontinued study drug were asked to
remain in the study and continue all protocol-specified visits and procedures.

The primary endpoint was analyzed using the linear mixed-effect model for repeated
measures (MMRM), using change from Baseline in mean SL as the response. The model
included visit, treatment, treatment-by-visit interaction, baseline mean SL, and electronic
data capture (EDC)-derived indicator for prior use of medication for narcolepsy (requiring
washout) as fixed effects.
The following multiple imputation method was used to impute missing data: for subjects who
did not complete the study, regardless of treatment group, all missing data were imputed
under the assumption that the joint distribution of observed and missing outcomes is the
same as that from the placebo group. For subjects that completed the study, but still have
missing data, regardless of treatment group, all missing data were assumed to be missing at
random (MAR) and were imputed under this assumption. Multiple imputation was used to
account for uncertainty in the imputation process. Analysis results from all imputed datasets
were combined using Rubin’s method. Estimated treatment difference along with the 95% CI
and the corresponding p-value for the treatment effects at each visit were reported.

135
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
All efficacy analyses were presented in the Full Analysis Set (FAS), defined as all participants
who were randomized. All participants in the FAS were analyzed according to the treatment
group to which they were randomized.
The hierarchical multiple testing procedure employed a graphical testing approach. The
primary and secondary efficacy endpoints were grouped into two families, where family 1
included hypotheses associated with change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency
on the MWT, change from Baseline to Week 12 in ESS total score, and WCR at Week 12.
Family 2 included hypotheses associated with the remaining secondary efficacy endpoints
(change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean number of lapses on the PVT, PGI-C score at Week
12, change from Baseline to Week 12 in NSS-CT total score, change from Baseline to Week 12
in six FINI domain scores, and change from Baseline to Week 12 in SF-36 mental and physical
component summary scores). The multiple testing approach for each family is presented in
Figure 12 and Figure 13, respectively.
Figure 12. Multiple Testing Procedure, Family 1 (Study TAK-861-3001)

Source: Statistical Analysis Plan v2.0, page 19

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Figure 13. Multiple Testing Procedure, Family 2 (Study TAK-861-3001)

Source: Statistical Analysis Plan v2.0, page 20

Sensitivity analyses provided by the Applicant included using Tobit model to account for
ceiling effect in MWT and exploring alternative missing data handling approaches.
For the secondary endpoints, the estimand was defined similarly to the primary estimand.
The continuous secondary endpoints of ESS, NSS-CT, FINI, and SF-36 were analyzed using an
MMRM model similar to the primary analysis method. WCR was analyzed using a generalized
estimating equations (GEE) model featuring a negative binomial distribution and a log link
function. The response variable was the total number of cataplexy attacks within a given 2week period, and the log of (the number of nonmissing diary days over the given 2-week
period/7) was included as offset in the model to estimate the weekly cataplexy rate. The
model included visit, treatment, and treatment-by-visit interaction, baseline WCR, and EDCderived indicator for prior use of medication for narcolepsy (requiring washout) as
137
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
explanatory variables. An unstructured working correlation matrix was used initially in the
model. If there were convergence issues with the model, an exchangeable working
correlation matrix was used, followed by an independent working correlation matrix, until
convergence was reached. The estimated incidence rate of weekly cataplexy for each
treatment group and the associated 95% CIs were extracted from the model, along with the
incidence rate ratio (IRR) of weekly cataplexy (TAK-861/placebo) for all TAK-861 treatment
groups, and the associated 95% CIs and two-sided p-values. PVT was analyzed using a
constrained longitudinal data analysis (cLDA) model after a square root transformation, and
PGI-C responder status was analyzed by GEE featuring a binomial distribution and a logit link
function. The responder indicator was used as the response variable in the model. The model
included visit, treatment, and treatment-by-visit interaction, baseline PGI-S (as a continuous
variable), and EDC-derived indicator for prior use of medication for narcolepsy (requiring
washout) as explanatory variables.
The statistical analysis plan was aligned with the Agency a priori, with the exception of several
minor technical comments. Most notably, there was a concern about the model chosen for
the secondary endpoint of PVT, which utilized a non-traditional cLDA model and relied on
delta method approximations. For this reason, when analyzing PVT, the Agency also explored
a more standard MMRM approach consistent with the primary analysis model. In general, the
analyses proposed by the Applicant were suitable for the analysis of the endpoints of interest.
The same applies to the statistical analysis of TAK-861-3002.
Given that the Applicant is seeking an indication for NT1 as a whole rather than for individual
symptoms, the endpoint selection strategy must reflect the multidimensional complexity of
the disorder. NT1 is characterized by a well-described pentad of symptoms (excessive
daytime sleepiness, cataplexy, hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and
disrupted nocturnal sleep, as reviewed in Bassetti et al., 2019, and described in ICSD-3, and
no single endpoint is sufficient to capture the breadth of the disease burden or the full scope
of a treatment's effect on NT1. Accordingly, the endpoint hierarchy was structured with two
guiding principles: (1) ensuring coverage of the NT1 symptom pentad across the primary and
secondary endpoints, and (2) balancing objective, performance-based measures with patientcentered, patient-reported outcomes (PROs) to mitigate ascertainment bias while preserving
clinical meaningfulness from the patient's perspective.
•

Objectivity and Clinical Meaningfulness of the Primary Endpoint: The MWT was selected as
the primary endpoint because it provides an objective, PSG-confirmed measure of a
patient's ability to sustain wakefulness under standardized soporific conditions—a core
functional deficit in NT1. As an instrumented, observer-independent measure of sleep onset
latency, the MWT minimizes the potential for response bias inherent in self-reported
assessments of sleepiness. Furthermore, improvement in MWT latency represents a
clinically meaningful outcome, as the inability to maintain wakefulness is central to the
disease definition, carries significant safety implications (e.g., driving, occupational
functioning), and is a common treatment target in the NT1 patient community. The Agency
considered the MWT to be an appropriate outcome measure for the primary analysis given

138
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
its established use in narcolepsy clinical trials and its capacity to objectively quantify the
severity of EDS and treatment response.
•

Endpoint Coverage of the NT1 Symptom Pentad and Disease Specificity: Among the
multiplicity-protected secondary endpoints, the WCR was prioritized as the first secondary
endpoint because cataplexy is the pathognomonic feature of NT1, distinguishing it from
other central disorders of hypersomnolence, including NT2 and idiopathic hypersomnia. The
inclusion of WCR as a co-primary or high-priority secondary endpoint therefore ensures that
a therapeutic claim for NT1 is directly supported by evidence of efficacy specific to the
condition. Cataplexy is directly attributable to orexin deficiency—the presumed underlying
pathophysiology of NT1—and the symptom’s reduction constitutes a meaningful clinical
benefit independent of improvements in EDS.

•

Patient-Centered Endpoints and Alignment with Patient Perspective: Consistent with the
Agency's patient-focused drug development (PFDD) framework, the endpoint hierarchy was
also designed to incorporate the patient's perspective on treatment benefit. The Epworth
Sleepiness Scale (ESS), although not without problems (e.g., requiring patients to imagine
how they would react in hypothetical situations), was included as a multiplicity-protected
secondary endpoint because it captures subjective daytime sleepiness—a dimension of
disease burden that patients consistently identify as among the most disabling features of
NT1 (Voice of Patient Report, Public Meeting: September 24, 2013, Report Date: June 2014,
CDER 2014)—and because it has been widely used as a PRO in narcolepsy clinical programs.
Its inclusion alongside the MWT allows for a concordance assessment between objective
wakefulness capacity and the patient's subjective experience of sleepiness, which are
related but distinct constructs.

•

The Narcolepsy Severity Scale for Clinical Trials (NSS-CT) was further included as a protected
secondary endpoint because, as a validated, disease-specific, 15-item patient-reported
instrument, it comprehensively addresses all five components of the NT1 symptom pentad
(daytime sleepiness, cataplexy, hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and DNS). This breadth of
coverage is particularly important in the context of an indication claim for NT1 as a whole,
as it ensures that the secondary endpoint set captures the full disease profile rather than a
subset of symptoms.

•

Exploratory Objective Endpoints: Biological Plausibility and Mechanistic Evidence: Although
classified as exploratory, the nPSG assessment—with particular attention to REM sleep
latency—was considered an important additional dimension of the trial's evidentiary
framework. Given that NT1 is defined by orexin-mediated dysregulation of REM sleep, and
that abbreviated or abnormal REM sleep latency is one of the objective biological hallmarks
of the disease, changes in nPSG-derived REM latency following oveporexton treatment
would provide corroborating mechanistic and pharmacodynamic evidence of the drug's
effect on the core neuropathological substrate of NT1. As an objective, PSG-confirmed
measure, nPSG REM latency findings—if directionally consistent with the primary and
secondary endpoints—support the biological plausibility of the observed clinical effects and
strengthen the overall characterization of the drug's benefit-risk profile.

Protocol Amendments
The initial protocol was amended once locally for the EU (July 15, 2024) and once globally
(October 23, 2024). The first participant was enrolled on August 21, 2024, under the original
protocol.
Noteworthy Changes
Initial Protocol EU Version 1 (July 15, 2024):
•

Changed the primary endpoint for EDS assessment from MWT to ESS total score exclusively
in the EU/EEA (MWT became a secondary endpoint in EU/EEA only).

•

Clarified that pregnant participants were to be withdrawn from treatment but preferably
not from the trial.

•

Clarified analysis set definitions: safety set analyzed according to treatment received; full
analysis set (FAS) analyzed according to randomized treatment assignment.

•

Moved the PVT number of lapses endpoint from Family 1 to Family 2.

•

Added missing data handling section specifying placebo-based multiple imputation for
intercurrent events (ICEs).

Global Protocol Amendment 1 (October 23, 2024):
•

Incorporated the EU-specific changes regarding ESS as primary endpoint in EU/EEA.

•

Specified that primary and secondary endpoints (MWT, ESS, NSS-CT, FINI, PVT lapses, SF-36)
were measured as change from Baseline at Week 12.

•

Changed PVT assessment from single 3 pm session to mean of two intraday sessions.

•

Allowed ESS retesting after medication discontinuation/tapering if screening ESS was <11 in
participants taking NT1 medications.

•

Clarified that pregnant participants were to be withdrawn from treatment but preferably
not from the trial.

•

Clarified that all participants in the safety set were to be analyzed according to the
treatment they received.

•

Clarified that the FAS consisted of all participants who were randomized and all participants
in the FAS were to be analyzed according to the treatment group to which they were
randomized.

•

The endpoint of number of lapses on the PVT was moved from Family 1 to Family 2.

•

Added a section on missing data handling. Clarified that placebo-based multiple imputation
would be used to handle missing data due to ICEs in the primary analysis.

140
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The protocol amendments do not appear to have substantially affected the conduct or
integrity of the trial. The first participant was enrolled on August 21, 2024, under the original
protocol, and the amendments were implemented relatively early in the trial timeline.
The Global Protocol Amendment 1 was implemented primarily in response to FDA feedback.
Notably, during the development program, the Agency informed the Applicant that the ESS
was not considered an adequate primary endpoint because it is a subjective measure and
recommended the MWT as the preferred primary endpoint due to its objective nature. The
EU-specific amendment, which designated ESS as the primary endpoint exclusively in the
EU/EEA, reflects regional regulatory differences but does not impact the U.S. regulatory
assessment, where MWT remains the primary endpoint.
Additionally, at the EOP2 meeting, the Agency recommended that the Applicant change their
MWT endpoint from assessment at Week 12 to change from Baseline at Week 12, as the
trajectory of change over time provides important information for labeling to inform
healthcare providers about the expected treatment response pattern. The Applicant
incorporated this recommendation into Global Protocol Amendment 1, which specified that
primary and secondary endpoints were to be measured as change from Baseline at Week 12.
The other amendments primarily consisted of clarifications to analysis set definitions, missing
data handling procedures, and minor adjustments to assessment timing (e.g., PVT
measurement from two intraday sessions rather than a single session), none of which appear
to compromise the trial's scientific validity or interpretability.
8.1.1.2.

Study Results

Compliance With Good Clinical Practices
The Applicant states that “This trial was conducted with the highest respect for the individual
participants in accordance with the protocol and ethical principles that have their origins in
the Declaration of Helsinki and in compliance with IRB regulations stated in Title 21 of the US
Code of Federal Regulations Part 56, ICH Harmonised Tripartite Guideline for GCP E6, IECs and
EU Clinical Trials Regulation No 536/2014, and all applicable industry regulations and
applicable national and local laws and regulations,“ and “The clinical trial protocol, protocol
amendments, the investigator’s brochure, a sample ICF, and other trial-related documents
were reviewed and approved by the local or central IRBs/IECs of all trial sites.”
The Applicant has provided attestation that Study TAK-861-3001 was conducted in
accordance with Good Clinical Practice (GCP).
Financial Disclosure
The Applicant has adequately disclosed financial interests and arrangements with clinical
investigators. The Applicant did not disclose any interests or arrangements that raised
questions about the integrity of the study data.

Patient Disposition
A total of 234 individuals were screened for the trial, from whom 168 participants were
enrolled across 41 sites in Japan, Europe, and North America. Of the 66 screen failures, 57
participants did not meet the inclusion/exclusion criteria, six withdrew consent, one
experienced an adverse event, and two were classified as "other" (no additional information
available). All 168 enrolled participants were randomized to one of three groups: placebo
(n=41), oveporexton 1 mg BID (n=61), or oveporexton 2 mg BID (n=66). In total, 167
participants received at least one dose of the trial intervention. One participant in the
oveporexton 1 mg BID group was randomized but not treated, did not complete the pre-dose
pharmacokinetic (PK) collection, and was subsequently lost to follow-up.
Of the 167 treated participants, 10 (6.0%) discontinued the trial. The reasons for
discontinuation are summarized by treatment group in Table 55 below.

The screen failure rate was 28.2% (66/234), which is reasonable for a trial with specific
eligibility criteria in a rare disease population. The majority of screen failures (86.4%; 57/66)
were due to not meeting inclusion/exclusion criteria, which does not raise concerns about the
representativeness of the enrolled population.
Few participants discontinued the overall program. The discontinuation rate was slightly
higher in the placebo group (12.2%; 5/41) compared to the overall oveporexton group (4.0%;
5/126).
The specific adverse events (AEs) and protocol deviations that led to these discontinuations
are described in detail in the paragraphs that follow.
Protocol Violations/Deviations
Participant-Level Deviations
Significant protocol deviations affected 52 of 168 participants (31.0%) in the randomized
sample. The overall proportion of participants with at least one deviation was similar
between the placebo (29.3%) and overall oveporexton (31.5%) groups.
Key Deviation Categories by Treatment Group:
•

Good Clinical Practice (GCP) Deviations (12.5% of all participants): This was the most
frequent category and was balanced between the placebo (12.2%) and overall oveporexton
(12.6%) groups. The majority of these (n=14) were administrative issues and were described
as “site staff recorded participant credentials used to access the Signant Health TrialMax
systems.” Seven cases involved breaches of participant confidentiality.

•

Informed Consent Deviations (8.9% of all participants): These were more frequent in the
overall oveporexton group (9.4%) than the placebo group (7.3%). The deviation for a
"consenting process was missed" occurred in six participants, total. This deviation was
heavily concentrated in the oveporexton 2 mg BID group, affecting five participants (7.6% of
that group), compared to only one participant (2.4%) in the placebo group.

143
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
•

Eligibility Criteria Deviations (5.4% of all participants): Nine participants were randomized
despite not meeting the study's eligibility criteria. This deviation was more frequent in the
placebo group (7.3%) compared to the overall oveporexton group (4.7%).
- Placebo group:
o One participant was randomized with an Epworth Sleepiness Scale (ESS) score below
11, failing to meet inclusion criterion 6.
o One participant was randomized based on a diagnostic PSG/MSLT conducted more
than 15 years prior without a repeat assessment before Day -2, failing to meet
inclusion criterion 5.
o One participant was randomized with an Apnea-Hypopnea Index (AHI) at or greater
than 15, meeting exclusion criterion 1. This participant was subsequently
discontinued from the study.
- Oveporexton group:
o One participant was randomized despite having a current medical disorder
associated with Excessive Daytime Sleepiness (EDS), meeting exclusion criterion 1.
o Three participants were randomized with a Weekly Cataplexy Rate (WCR) score of 3
or less, failing to meet inclusion criterion 7.
o One participant was randomized despite being at imminent risk of suicide or having
attempted suicide within the year prior to screening, meeting exclusion criterion 22.
o One participant was randomized with a Body Mass Index (BMI) outside the required
range of 18 to 40 kg/m², failing to meet inclusion criterion 4.

•

Safety Deviations (3.6% of all participants): These were substantially more frequent in the
placebo group (7.3%) than the overall oveporexton group (2.4%). This was driven by the use
of prohibited concomitant medications, which was reported in two placebo participants
(4.9%) and two oveporexton participants (1.6%).

•

Missing Endpoint Assessments (1.2% of all participants): Both instances of this deviation
occurred in the overall oveporexton group (1.6%), with none reported in the placebo group.

•

Discontinuations Due to Protocol Deviations (n=3): Two occurred in the placebo group (one
for enrolling an ineligible participant who met an exclusion criterion for Apnea-Hypopnea
Index (discussed previously), one for prohibited substance use, i.e., cocaine and marijuana)
and one occurred in the oveporexton 2 mg BID group (for prohibited medication use, i.e.,
amphetamine/dextroamphetamine).

144
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Trial-Level Deviations
At the trial level, eight trial sites reported at least one significant site-level deviation. These
included:
•

Protocol Implementation (four sites): Issues included lack of staff training, non-compliance
with IRB/IEC requirements, insufficient resources.

•

Informed Consent (three sites): All deviations in this category were for a "consenting
process was missed."

•

Safety (two sites): Deviations involved safety assessments being performed by nonauthorized staff.

•

GCP (one site): The deviation was for inadequate PI oversight.

Additionally, the Applicant identified a few additional protocol deviations after the database
lock (DBL) and submitted an amendment detailing these findings.
•

Participant-Level Deviations: Four deviations were reclassified from non-significant to
significant. The Applicant stated that the treatment assignments for these four participants
would remain blinded, justifying that while the deviations were reclassified as significant,
they were not considered substantial enough to warrant unblinding. The reclassified
deviations included:
- One instance of failure to perform a protocol-required repeat ECG.
- One missed C-SSRS assessment.
- Two critical informed consent process failures: one case of a missed consent form for
the optional DNA process and another involving an improperly timed signature.

•

Site-Level Deviation: One additional significant deviation was reported for a site that failed
to maintain Investigational Product (IP) accountability logs as required by the pharmacy
manual.

An analysis of protocol deviations revealed numerical imbalances between the treatment
arms in several key categories. For instance, the rate of certain safety-related deviations (e.g.,
prohibited medication use) appeared higher in the placebo group. However, a particularly
noteworthy imbalance in eligibility criteria occurred in the oveporexton group, where three
participants were randomized with a WCR of 3 or less, compared to zero such participants in
the placebo group. Additionally, the few instances of missing endpoint assessments occurred
exclusively in the oveporexton arm.
The potential impact of these imbalances on the overall trial results is likely minimal, and in
the case of the WCR discrepancy, would have biased the results against the observed
treatment effect. Specifically, by including participants with a lower cataplexy burden at
Baseline, the potential magnitude of improvement in the oveporexton group was limited,
which may have masked the true treatment effect on this endpoint. For the other deviations,
the observed differences are based on very small absolute numbers of events. When dealing
with such low counts, even a minor difference of one or two participants can create a large
145
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
percentage difference that is not statistically robust and is likely attributable to random
chance.
Therefore, while these specific imbalances are noted for completeness in this review, it is
unlikely that they meaningfully affected the interpretation of the trial's primary efficacy and
safety outcomes. Given that the most significant numerical imbalance would have biased the
results in the opposite direction of what was observed, and other differences are driven by a
small number of events, there is no evidence of a systematic bias that would invalidate the
study’s overall findings.
Demographic Characteristics
Demographic characteristics were generally similar across the treatment groups (Table 57). Of
the 168 randomized participants, 35% were White and 4% were Black. For another 35% of
participants, race was categorized as unknown, as these data were not collected due to local
regulations.
The study population was predominantly female, accounting for 58.3% of the overall
oveporexton group and 58.5% of the placebo group. The median age of participants was 28
years, with a range of 16 to 65 years. The study enrolled only three adolescent participants
(1.8%) 16 and 17 years of age across three trial sites (two in the United States, one in Japan),
one in each of the three study arms. These participants were distributed among the
treatment arms: one in the placebo group, one in the oveporexton 1 mg BID group, and one
in the oveporexton 2 mg BID group. There was only one participant aged ≥65 years.

1

The demographic and baseline characteristics, including age
, were well-balanced
across the placebo and oveporexton treatment groups. A notable limitation is the lack of data
in the older adult population; although the protocol allowed for enrollment up to age 70, only
one participant aged 65 was included. Therefore, the effects of oveporexton in this
population cannot be characterized from this study.
.

Although the trial's multinational enrollment means the overall population may not be
directly representative of the United States, certain factors support the generalizability of the
.
findings. The
distribution aligns with recent U.S.-based epidemiological data on NT1
(Ohayon et al., 2023). Given that the underlying pathophysiology of NT1 does not differ
across populations, and the standard of care for diagnosis and treatment is similar in the E.U.
and U.S., the large proportion of European participants does not present a concern for
generalizability. To address the geographic diversity, a subgroup analysis of the North
American cohort was performed to confirm that the efficacy results in this specific population
are consistent with those of the overall study group; see the discussion of study
demographics under Study 3002 (Section 8.1.2.2).

147
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., Disease Characteristics, Important Concomitant Drugs)
The baseline disease characteristics of the participants were generally well-balanced across
the placebo and oveporexton treatment arms:
•

Disease History and Severity of Sleepiness: The treatment groups were highly comparable in
terms of disease history. The median age at symptom onset (approximately 15 years) and
the median age at NT1 diagnosis (approximately 21 years) were consistent across all arms.
Furthermore, the key baseline measures for EDS were severe and well-matched. The mean
MWT sleep latency was extremely low (around 5.0 minutes), and the mean ESS total score
was very high (around 18.5), indicating a population with a significant disease burden at
study entry.

•

Baseline Cataplexy Rate: A numerical imbalance was noted in the baseline WCR. The mean
WCR was higher in the placebo group (48.9) compared to the overall oveporexton group
(40.0). However, the median values were more closely aligned (28.5 versus 26.0,
respectively), and the standard deviations were very large in all groups. This suggests that
the difference in means is likely driven by a few participants with extremely high cataplexy
rates and may not represent a clinically significant systematic imbalance in cataplexy
severity across the groups.

•

Diagnostic Confirmation and Prior Treatment: All participants had confirmed diagnoses of
NT1 through PSG/MSLT and HLA positivity or documented historical CSF orexin levels
≤110 pg/mL per trial inclusion criteria. The proportion of participants that were HLA positive
(95.8%) or had documented low CSF orexin levels (44.5% combining participants with CSF
reported in numeric or categorical values) were similar across groups. The high rate of prior
use of NT1 medications requiring washout (approximately 80%) was also consistent across
all groups, indicating that the trial enrolled a predominantly treatment-experienced
population.

•

Lifestyle Factors: Baseline lifestyle characteristics such as caffeine and tobacco use were
similar across groups. A slightly higher percentage of current alcohol users was noted in the
placebo group (63.4%) compared to the overall oveporexton group (53.5%), though this
small difference is unlikely to be clinically meaningful.

•

PAP Ventilation Use: The rate of Positive Airway Pressure (PAP) use for comorbid
obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) in the overall oveporexton group (7.1%; 9/126) was nearly
three times that of the placebo group (2.4%; 1/41).

In conclusion, although the treatment groups were highly comparable for most key
parameters, minor numerical imbalances were noted for baseline cataplexy rates, current
alcohol use, and the rate of PAP ventilation use. A careful consideration of each is warranted.
The numerical imbalance in current alcohol users is highly unlikely to have influenced the
primary efficacy results. The protocol not only prohibited alcohol consumption on the days of
key assessments, such as the MWT, but also required participants to be admitted to the
clinical unit the day prior to the MWT. This confinement provides a robust layer of control,
ensuring compliance with the alcohol restriction and effectively mitigating any potential for
148
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
acute alcohol effects to confound these critical measurements. Similarly, the imbalance in
PAP use, while noted, is based on a very small number of participants, and its clinical
significance is likely limited.
The higher mean baseline WCR in the placebo group would likely lead to more conservative
treatment effects (i.e., it could artificially inflate the placebo response and, in turn, diminish
the observed treatment difference between oveporexton and placebo), and as such is not of
great concern.
Therefore, despite these minor imbalances, their potential impact on the study's conclusions
is likely minimal due to mitigating factors in the trial's conduct and statistical plan (i.e.,
baseline WCR is included as a covariate in the primary analysis model). The treatment groups
remained highly comparable with respect to disease history, severity of sleepiness, and
diagnostic confirmation, providing a solid basis for evaluating the treatment effects of
oveporexton.
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Treatment Compliance
Overall treatment compliance was very high in both groups, with a median compliance rate of
99.7% in the overall oveporexton group and 98.8% in the placebo group. A difference was
observed in the rate of low compliance; while no participants in the oveporexton group had a
compliance rate below 70%, two participants (4.9%) in the placebo group fell below this
threshold. A slightly higher proportion of participants in the oveporexton groups (28.6%,
36/126) had a compliance rate over 100% compared to the placebo group (17.1%, 7/41),
suggesting some instances of taking more than the prescribed dosage.
An information request (IR) was issued to the Applicant seeking clarification on this finding. In
response, the Applicant attributed the above-threshold compliance rates to reconciliation
discrepancies during tablet accountability, such as tablets being dropped or lost, or
participants failing to return tablets that had been stored in a secondary container, rather
than to actual excess dosing. All cases of compliance exceeding 100% were reviewed for
potential overdose and associated adverse events and were further assessed for possible
protocol deviations. Where an overdose was confirmed, a special situation form was
completed, and a significant protocol deviation was filed accordingly. In Study TAK-861-3001,
one participant (subject ID # 63015-406) was identified as having unintentionally taken one
extra pill, which was documented as a significant protocol deviation. These issues likely had
no significant impact on efficacy results.
Comorbidity
Concurrent medical conditions were frequently reported, affecting approximately 58% of
participants, and were generally balanced between the placebo (61%) and overall
oveporexton (57%) groups. Among the most common conditions, nervous system disorders
were reported in 12.6% of participants and were well-balanced across treatment arms. This
149
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
category was followed by metabolism and nutrition disorders, where a numerical imbalance
was observed, with a higher frequency in the overall oveporexton group (14.3%) compared to
the placebo group (7.3%). Seasonal allergy was also common at 12% and was balanced across
groups.
Two comorbidities of particular relevance to this trial, sleep apnea and psychiatric disorders,
were also examined. A notable imbalance was observed for sleep apnea syndrome, which was
reported in 4% of participants in the oveporexton group but in zero participants in the
placebo group. In contrast, the prevalence of psychiatric disorders was found to be wellbalanced, affecting 9.8% of participants in the placebo arm and 7.9% in the overall
oveporexton arm.
Past and Concomitant Medications
The trial enrolled a heavily treatment-experienced population, with a high rate of prior
medication use for NT1 that was generally comparable between groups. Approximately 92%
of participants in the overall oveporexton group and 88% in the placebo group had a lifetime
history of using at least one NT1 therapy.
Although the use of common prior therapies such as modafinil (52.7% of total participants)
and methylphenidate (27.5% of total participants) was well-balanced, numerical imbalances
were noted for other specific agents. Lifetime use of sodium oxybate was more frequent in
the overall oveporexton group (42.9%) compared to the placebo group (29.3%). A similar
trend was observed for venlafaxine hydrochloride, which was reported by 15.9% of
participants in the oveporexton group versus 9.8% in the placebo group. The high level of
active treatment prior to the study was further evidenced by the large and well-balanced
proportion of participants—approximately 80% in both the oveporexton and placebo arms—
who required washout from their existing NT1 medications.
Regarding medication use during the trial, the rate of concomitant medication was also high
and balanced, reported in approximately 58% of participants across all groups. The most
frequently reported therapeutic class was anti-inflammatory products, where a slight
imbalance was observed, with use being somewhat lower in the overall oveporexton group
(20.6%) compared to the placebo group (29.3%).
Importantly, a review of the concomitant medication list revealed that a few participants
were on prohibited medications during the study. One participant each was reported as
taking atomoxetine, venlafaxine, milnacipran, amphetamine aspartate, or dexamphetamine.
Most of these instances were appropriately identified and recorded as significant protocol
deviations. However, the use of sertraline by one participant was not recorded as a deviation,
which prompted an IR to be sent to the Applicant for clarification of this oversight. In
response, the Applicant clarified that sertraline was a prior medication that had been
discontinued before randomization, and that its erroneous inclusion in the concomitant
medication dataset resulted from a date imputation error in the electronic data capture
system.

150
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Rescue Medication Use
No rescue medications were specified in the protocol.
Summary
A thorough review of baseline characteristics, comorbidities, and medication history confirms
that the treatment groups were generally well-balanced; any minor differences are based on
small absolute numbers of participants and are unlikely to have a clinically meaningful impact
on the interpretation of the efficacy and safety results.
The use of prohibited concomitant medications by a few participants was noted as a trial
conduct issue, and an IR was issued to the Applicant for clarification. The Applicant responded
adequately, and no further issues were identified; this matter is therefore not considered to
affect the overall baseline comparability of the randomized groups.
With respect to treatment compliance, the higher proportion of participants in the
oveporexton groups with compliance rates exceeding 100% is acknowledged; however,
following the Applicant's clarification, this finding appears attributable to tablet
accountability reconciliation discrepancies rather than systematic excess dosing and is
therefore not considered to meaningfully affect the reliability of the exposure data.
Therefore, despite these minor observations, the study arms are considered highly
comparable, providing a solid foundation for the evaluation of oveporexton's treatment
effects.
Efficacy Results–Primary Endpoint
Both dosages of oveporexton achieved highly statistically significant improvements in the
change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency on the MWT. The Least Square (LS)
mean changes and treatment differences in sleep latency on the MWT are displayed in Table
58. The change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency on the MWT was significantly
higher in oveporexton 2 mg BID group than placebo (LS mean difference [95% CI] =17.20
[13.66, 20.73]; p<0.0001). Similarly, the change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep
latency on the MWT was significantly higher in oveporexton 1 mg BID group than placebo (LS
mean difference [95% CI] =13.83 [10.23, 17.44]; p<0.0001).

Lastly, the primary endpoint results were generally consistent across subgroups of region,
race, and sex (Figure 15). Although the study was not designed or powered to demonstrate
efficacy within subgroups, observed treatment effects across subgroups trended in the same
direction in favor of oveporexton and most subgroups reached nominal statistical
significance. One notable exception was the subgroup of North America, which consisted of
33 participants from the United States and Canada—the results were directionally consistent
with the rest of the world, but did not reach nominal significance. The age subgroup was not
investigated, as almost all participants in the studies were between 18 and 65 years of age.

Data Quality and Integrity
There were no data quality or integrity issues noted during the review.
Efficacy Results–Secondary and Other Relevant Endpoints
All secondary endpoints included in the multiple testing procedure were statistically
significant. The results of the secondary endpoint analyses were supportive of the primary
endpoint analysis and also yielded a very robust treatment effect. None of the results were
sensitive to missing data or modeling assumptions.
Both dosages of oveporexton achieved a highly statistically significant improvements in the
change from Baseline to Week 12 in ESS total score, WCR, NSS-CT total score, mean number
of lapses in PVT, PGI-C Responder status at Week 12, FINI (all six domains of tiredness,
cognitive functioning, cataplexy, social activities, everyday activities, everyday
responsibilities), and SF-36. The results for all of these multiplicity protected endpoints are
presented in Table 60.

Dose/Dose Response
The efficacy results across the primary and secondary endpoints suggest a dose-response
relationship between oveporexton and improvement in narcolepsy symptoms.
Oveporexton demonstrated robust and statistically significant efficacy at both the 1-mg and
2-mg BID dosages across the primary and key secondary endpoints. Both dosages produced
156
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
meaningful improvements in sleep latency, excessive daytime sleepiness, cataplexy rate, and
patient-reported outcomes relative to placebo. The 2-mg BID dosage consistently yielded
numerically greater improvements across the majority of evaluated endpoints, providing
evidence of a dose-response relationship. The weekly cataplexy rate results did not follow
this same pattern—both dosages produced robust reductions relative to placebo, with
overlapping confidence intervals suggesting the numerical difference between dosages is
unlikely to be clinically meaningful. Taken together, the efficacy data support the clinical
benefit of oveporexton in patients with NT1.
Durability of Response
This study compared MWT scores at baseline and at the end of treatment and did not assess
the durability of response. However, a separation between oveporexton and placebo was
observed as early as Week 8 (Figure 14).
There are no placebo-controlled data beyond 12 weeks. Overall, efficacy after 12 weeks of
dosing indicates reasonable durability of effect over time for NT1. An additional randomized
withdrawal study for maintenance treatment was not felt to be necessary given this duration
of effectiveness.
Persistence of Effect
The persistence of effect is the effect of the drug over time after treatment is stopped or
withheld.
The Applicant did not report on persistence of effect; however, oveporexton and its PK profile
are intended for chronic daily dosing, and there is no expectation for a meaningful
persistence of effect after stopping oveporexton.
Efficacy Results–Additional or Exploratory COA (PRO) Endpoints
The NT1 pentad comprises five core clinical features: EDS, cataplexy, hypnagogic/
hypnopompic hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and disturbed/disrupted nighttime sleep (DNS).
Although the primary and secondary endpoints adequately captured the effect of
oveporexton on EDS (via MWT and ESS) and cataplexy (via weekly cataplexy rate), a
comprehensive assessment of the drug's effect on the complete NT1 pentad requires
evaluation of the remaining three symptoms. These symptoms—hypnagogic/hypnopompic
hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and DNS—are critical components of the disease burden
experienced by patients with NT1. Hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations and sleep
paralysis are REM-related phenomena that occur at sleep-wake transitions; DNS encompasses
both sleep architecture abnormalities and sleep instabilities.
To assess oveporexton's effect on these symptoms, we reviewed secondary and exploratory
endpoints that the Applicant included to determine whether oveporexton demonstrates
efficacy across the full spectrum of NT1 pentad symptoms. Although these analyses were not
multiplicity protected, and as such, need to be interpreted with caution, they are informative
when assessing the overall impact of oveporexton on the NT1 pentad.

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
In addition to the sensitivity analyses above, the Statistical Reviewer also conducted an
analysis focusing on study sites, to explore whether any one study site yielded results strongly
different from the rest of the study, or whether the efficacy results were primarily driven by a
single site. The results are presented in Figure 16, where the primary analysis is repeated,
leaving out one site at a time, with the results plotted against the site being left out. The
results were highly consistent and not dependent on any one site.
Figure 16. Sensitivity Analysis: Impact of Each Site on the Sleep Latency on the MWT Change
From Baseline Treatment Effect vs. Placebo at Week 12 in the Full Analysis Set (TAK-8613001)

Source: Statistical reviewer

Integrated Review of Effectiveness
Overall, for the Study TAK-861-3001, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial of
oveporexton 1 mg and 2 mg, the review team confirms the Applicant’s primary finding that
both dosages of oveporexton are shown to be superior compared with placebo for the
primary endpoint of change from Baseline to Week 16 in mean sleep latency on the MWT.
The study yielded highly statistically significant results on the primary endpoint and all
secondary endpoints included in the multiple testing procedure. The results were robust to
missing data or modeling choices due to large magnitude of effect and low amount of missing
data. As such, any reasonable sensitivity analysis did not have a notable effect on the results.
In addition, exploratory endpoints that evaluate other aspects of the NT1 symptom pentad
compared to the endpoints included in the multiple testing procedure also generally
supported the finding that oveporexton yielded an effect on all of the symptoms in the
pentad.
The Applicant is seeking an indication for NT1 as a whole, rather than a symptom-specific
indication such as EDS or cataplexy, which have historically been the focus of approved
160
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
narcolepsy therapies. This approach represents a novel regulatory paradigm for NT1 and, to
be supported, requires demonstration of a meaningful treatment effect across all core
components of the disease. For NT1, these core components are defined by the NT1 pentad:
EDS, cataplexy, hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and DNS. The
efficacy results from Study TAK-861-3001 are reviewed below through the lens of each
pentad symptom.
•

Excessive Daytime Sleepiness (EDS): The evidence supporting an effect of oveporexton on
EDS is robust. Both dosages achieved highly statistically significant improvements on the
MWT (the primary endpoint) and the ESS, two well-established objective and subjective
measures of EDS, respectively. Additionally, NSS-CT items 1 through 7, which capture the
frequency and burden of EDS symptoms, were also nominally significant for both dosages,
further corroborating the effect of oveporexton on this domain (data not shown).

•

Cataplexy: Both dosages of oveporexton demonstrated a robust and statistically significant
reduction in WCR, with incidence rate ratios of 0.34 and 0.38 for the 1-mg and 2-mg BID
dosages, respectively, reflecting a greater than 60% reduction in cataplexy episodes relative
to placebo. NSS-CT items 8 through 10, which assess the frequency and bother of cataplexy
symptoms, were also nominally significant for both dosages, providing additional subjective
corroboration of the effect on this domain.

•

Hypnagogic/Hypnopompic Hallucinations: Both dosages of oveporexton showed nominally
significant reductions in hallucination frequency and bother as measured by NSS-CT items
11 and 12, both in the overall population and in the subgroup of participants with at least
one hallucination episode per week at baseline. The magnitude of effect was large and
consistent across dosages, supporting a meaningful treatment effect on this pentad
symptom.

•

Sleep Paralysis: Similarly, both dosages showed nominally significant reductions in sleep
paralysis as measured by NSS-CT items 13 and 14, both in the overall population and in the
subgroup of participants with at least one sleep paralysis episode per week at baseline.
These results provide clear evidence of an effect on this pentad symptom.

•

Disturbed/Disrupted Nighttime Sleep (DNS): The DNS domain presents a more complex
picture than the other pentad symptoms, in part due to the wake-promoting mechanism of
action of oveporexton. Positive results were observed for NSS-CT item 15 (nominally
significant for the 2-mg BID dosage), nPSG-measured REM latency (nominally significant for
both dosages), number of nights per week of good or excellent sleep quality (nominally
significant for the 1-mg BID dosage), and indirect measures of sleep-related quality of life
including the SF-36 Vitality domain and the FINI Tiredness domain (nominally and
statistically significant, respectively, for both dosages). However, nocturnal awakenings as
measured by nPSG appeared numerically increased with oveporexton relative to placebo,
which is not unexpected given the drug's wake-promoting properties. The clinical review
team notes that DNS is a multidimensional domain that extends beyond the number of
nocturnal awakenings; it encompasses the subjective perception of sleep quality and
broadly captures poor sleep experiences, including low sleep quality, nightmares, and
161

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
parasomnias—many of which are REM-related phenomena. Given that oveporexton
restores REM sleep regulation, as evidenced by the nominally significant improvement in
REM latency, the clinical review team considers it biologically plausible and clinically
reasonable to conclude that oveporexton improves the DNS domain as a whole,
notwithstanding the absence of a significant effect on nPSG-measured nocturnal
awakenings.
Taken together, the efficacy data from Study TAK-861-3001 provide evidence of a treatment
effect across all five components of the NT1 pentad. The team considers the evidence from
Study TAK-861-3001 supportive of the Applicant's approach to the NT1 indication.
Study TAK-861-3002
8.1.2.1.

Study Design

Overview and Objective
Study Title:
A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study to Evaluate the Efficacy and Safety of
TAK-861 for the Treatment of Narcolepsy with Cataplexy (Narcolepsy Type 1)
Primary Objectives:
The study’s primary objective was to assess the efficacy of oveporexton compared to placebo
with regard to EDS as measured by the MWT.
Secondary Objectives:
Secondary objectives included assessment of between-group difference in:
•

Changes in EDS as measured by the ESS total score

•

Changes in cataplexy episodes as measured by the WCR

•

Changes in sustained attention as measured by the PVT

•

Changes in overall narcolepsy symptoms measured by the PGI-C scale

•

Changes in overall severity of narcolepsy symptoms measured by the NSS-CT

•

Changes in functional impacts of narcolepsy as assessed by the FINI

•

Changes in quality of life of participants, as assessed by the SF-36.

162
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Trial Design
Study Design Overview:
Study TAK-861-3002 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 12-week
parallel-group trial to assess the safety and efficacy of one oral dosage of oveporexton for the
treatment of NT1.
As with Study TAK-861-3001, the study began with a screening period of up to 50 days, which
could be extended on a case-by-case basis with Sponsor approval. During Screening,
participants were required to discontinue their narcolepsy medications, including
antidepressants and sodium or multisalt oxybate. Starting no later than Day -16, participants
completed an electronic diary to track cataplexy episodes. To be eligible, participants had to
complete the diary for at least 11 of the 14 days between Day -16 and Day -3 and had to
experience at least 4 partial and/or complete cataplexy episodes per week during this period.
Throughout the study, participants were required to stay overnight at the study site on three
occasions: Days -2 to 1 (with one mandatory overnight at Day -2 and one optional overnight
at Day -1), Days 55 to 56, and Days 83 to 84. After completing the 12-week treatment period,
participants had the option to enroll in a long-term extension study under a separate protocol
(Study TAK-861-2003). Those who chose not to participate in the extension study completed
two follow-up visits at approximately 7 and 28 days after their final dose of study drug and
could restart their restricted medications (except those associated with liver function test
elevations) 7 days after the last dose. Participants who terminated the trial early were
required to complete an early termination visit as soon as possible and a follow-up visit
approximately 28 days after the last dose of trial intervention. For a schematic of the study
design, see Figure 17.

Trial Location
This multi-center study was conducted at 28 sites: 20 trial sites in Europe, 7 trial sites in Asian
Pacific, and 20 trial sites in Australia. Notably, no sites in North America recruited for this
trial.
Diagnostic Criteria
The key diagnostic criteria for enrollment into Study TAK-861-3002 were identical to those of
the registrational Study TAK-861-3001. For a detailed description of these criteria, see the
corresponding section of the review for Study TAK-861-3001.
Key Eligibility Criteria
The key eligibility criteria for enrollment into Study TAK-861-3002 were identical to those of
the registrational study, TAK-861-3001. For a detailed description of these criteria, see the
corresponding section of the review for Study TAK-861-3001.

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Study Treatments, Dosage Selection, and Compliance
At the beginning of the 12-week-treatment period, on Day 1 participants were randomized in
a 1:2 randomization ratio to receive:
•

Matching placebo tablet, 1 placebo tablet BID, at least 3 hours apart

•

Oveporexton tablet, 2 mg tablet BID, at least 3 hours apart

Starting on the morning of Day 1, the study drug was administered at approximately the same
time each day for 12 weeks, with the following schedules:
•

For all days during the treatment period except clinic MWT days, participants were
instructed to take the first dose no later than 10 am and the second dose at least 3 hours
after the first morning dose, but no later than 1 pm.

•

On clinic MWT days (Days 1, 56, and 84), the drug was administered as close to 8 am and 11
am as possible, respectively.

Compliance, Prohibited Concomitant Medications, Meals and Dietary Restrictions/Activity,
Subject Completion, Dose Discontinuation, and Withdrawal
Several key aspects of trial conduct in Study TAK-861-3002 were identical to those of the
registrational Study TAK-861-3001. This includes compliance assessment, prohibited
concomitant medications, definition of study completion, reasons for dosage discontinuation,
as well as meals and dietary restrictions or allowed activity. For a detailed description of
these characteristics, see the corresponding section of the review for Study TAK-861-3001.
Schedule of Assessments
As for Study TAK-861-3001, all participants checked into the clinic in the afternoon on
Days -2, 55, and 83. Discharge occurred after the last study assessment of the last day was
performed.
See Table 62 and Table 63 for the Study TAK-861-3002 schedule of assessment and
procedures.
