NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Review of adverse events and laboratory, vital sign, and electrocardiogram data from the two
placebo-controlled pivotal trials of oveporexton (TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002),
supplemented by data from the long-term extension study TAK-861-2003 and the broader
integrated summary of safety database, reveals a safety profile that is largely consistent with
the known pharmacological effects of OX2R agonism. The overall incidence of TEAEs was
substantially higher in oveporexton-treated participants compared to placebo; however, this
difference is driven predominantly by expected on-target pharmacodynamic effects rather
than by unanticipated toxicities.
The most common adverse reactions for oveporexton were insomnia, pollakiuria, micturition
urgency, and salivary hypersecretion, all occurring at substantially higher rates than placebo.
These events are mechanistically consistent with OX2R agonism and its known effects on
sleep-wake regulation, bladder function, and autonomic tone, and are therefore considered
on-target pharmacological effects of the drug. Although insomnia events were predominantly
of limited functional impact and urinary events were similarly manageable, their strikingly
high frequency relative to placebo—and the potential for these effects to affect patients'
quality of life and treatment adherence—warrants their inclusion in the Warnings and
Precautions section of product labeling, to ensure that patients and prescribers are
adequately informed prior to treatment initiation and can implement appropriate
management strategies, including dosing timing optimization and sleep hygiene counseling
for insomnia.
Discontinuations due to AEs were numerically slightly higher in the oveporexton group
compared to placebo. The most clinically notable discontinuations involved cases of exerciseassociated rhabdomyolysis in the oveporexton group, characterized primarily by laboratory
findings—markedly elevated CPK and transaminases meeting protocol-defined
discontinuation criteria—rather than clinical symptoms, with no renal impairment in any case.
A broader pattern of CPK elevations above markedly abnormal value thresholds was observed
at a numerically higher rate in oveporexton-treated participants compared to placebo across
the pivotal trials, with a similar pattern persisting in the LTE study. Although the majority of
CPK elevations were asymptomatic, transient, and attributable to physical activity—a
confounder that is particularly relevant in this population given that effective treatment of
narcolepsy may lead to increased physical activity and return to exercise—a causal
contribution of oveporexton cannot be definitively excluded. Product labeling should address
CPK elevations with appropriate guidance on patient counseling.
Hepatic enzyme elevations leading to discontinuation were observed at a low and
comparable rate between oveporexton and placebo groups in the pivotal trials, with most
cases having identifiable alternative etiologies. No cases met Hy's Law criteria across the
development program, in contrast with similar products previously studied by the Applicant.
The reader is referred to the DILI team review for a detailed assessment of hepatic safety.
Similarly, urinary AEs are addressed in detail in the DUOG review.

283
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Laboratory findings were otherwise unremarkable. Beyond the CPK elevations noted above, a
modest but detectable increase in LDL cholesterol and total cholesterol was observed in
oveporexton-treated participants compared to placebo; however, the magnitude of these
changes was small, triglycerides and HDL were largely unaffected, and no cardiovascular or
lipid-related AEs were observed at a higher rate in the oveporexton arms. These findings are
not considered clinically meaningful at this time so no warning is necessary; but the lab data
will be briefly noted in Section 6 of labeling, and routine post-marketing pharmacovigilance
monitoring is appropriate.
Vital sign assessments revealed transient increases in blood pressure and heart rate on the
first day of treatment, which returned to near baseline by the end of the treatment period
and no other trends were noticed on LTE study data as well. Product labeling should reflect
the observed transient increases in blood pressure and heart rate.
In the LTE study, one death due to myocardial infarction and one non-fatal myocardial
infarction were reported; the latter occurred approximately 13 hours after drug
administration in the LTE, though the patient had already been exposed to oveporexton
during the parent study. Both patients had significant pre-existing cardiovascular risk factors,
including obesity and hypertension, and one was additionally a current smoker. Although
these events warrant acknowledgment, a causal relationship to oveporexton is not
established: no cardiovascular SAEs were observed in the placebo-controlled pivotal studies;
QTc interval was unremarkable across the development program; and oveporexton did not
demonstrate major exacerbation of cardiovascular risk factors—including blood pressure,
heart rate, lipids, or glucose—beyond the transient first-day hemodynamic effects noted
above or possible minor lipid increases over time.
A small number of malignancy cases were identified in the LTE study, spanning histologically
and etiologically distinct cancer types. These cases are considered likely attributable to
background incidence, given the absence of nonclinical carcinogenicity signals, the lack of
biological plausibility for OX2R agonism-related oncogenesis, and the elevated background
cancer risk reported in the narcolepsy population. Routine post-marketing pharmacovigilance
for malignancy is appropriate.
The safety profile was generally consistent across demographic subgroups, though subgroup
analyses are limited by small and unbalanced sample sizes. No meaningful pediatric safety
data are available. Notably, the clinical development program enrolled virtually no
participants aged 65 years and older, and the number of U.S.-enrolled participants was
limited, leaving the safety profile in these groups not fully characterized. Post-marketing data
will be essential to identify any safety signals specific to older patients, in whom age-related
changes in pharmacokinetics, comorbidity burden, and polypharmacy may alter the risk
profile, as well as any population-specific findings relevant to the broader U.S. patient
population.
Overall, the safety profile of oveporexton is characterized predominantly by on-target
pharmacological effects of OX2R agonism that are generally manageable and decrease in
frequency with continued exposure. The absence of deaths or SAEs attributable to the drug in
the placebo-controlled studies, the low discontinuation rate, the lack of QTc prolongation, the
284
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
absence of clinically meaningful metabolic or hematologic toxicity, and the absence of suicidal
ideation and behavior or any additional psychiatric signal are reassuring.
The key safety considerations that warrant attention in product labeling and post-marketing
surveillance include insomnia, urinary symptoms, and salivary hypersecretion, as on-target or
class-related effects, as well as CPK elevations. The overall safety profile is considered
acceptable and manageable for the treatment of NT1, provided that appropriate labeling and
routine post-marketing surveillance measures are implemented.

Statistical Issues
Potential for Functional Unblinding and Robustness of Efficacy Evidence
The relatively high incidence of TEAEs—notably insomnia and urinary symptoms, each
occurring in approximately 50% of treated participants compared to near zero in the placebo
group—raises the theoretical concern of functional unblinding, whereby participants and/or
investigators may infer treatment assignment based on the presence or absence of these side
effects. Of note, these potential adverse events were explicitly disclosed in the informed
consent forms, meaning that participants were aware of their possibility prior to enrollment.
This prior awareness may have further contributed to the potential for functional unblinding,
as participants experiencing these symptoms could reasonably infer that they were receiving
active treatment rather than placebo. If functional unblinding occurred, it could potentially
inflate efficacy estimates through expectation bias, particularly for subjective outcome
measures.
Nonetheless, both of these potential sources of functional unblinding—the high incidence of
recognizable adverse events disclosed in the informed consent and the randomization ratio
favoring active treatment—do not raise great concern in the context of this review. This is
because the primary efficacy endpoints relied upon in this trial are objective and
physiologically grounded, and therefore not meaningfully susceptible to expectation bias or
conscious reporting tendencies. Efficacy was assessed using the MWT, a measure that
quantifies a participant's physiological ability to remain awake under standardized conditions.
Critically, efficacy was further corroborated by nocturnal polysomnography (nPSG), which
demonstrated a normalization of sleep architecture, including a recovery of REM latency to
approximately 60 minutes—a finding consistent with restoration of orexin-mediated sleepwake regulation and strikingly distinct from the severely shortened REM latency (often just a
few minutes) characteristic of NT1. These polysomnographic findings represent objective,
physiologically grounded evidence of drug effect that cannot be attributed to participant or
investigator expectation.
Taken together, the convergence of objective wakefulness measures and normalized sleep
architecture on nPSG provides strong support for a genuine pharmacological effect of
oveporexton, independent of any potential influence of functional unblinding arising from
either the adverse event profile or the randomization design. The consistency of these
objective findings across endpoints substantially strengthens confidence in the robustness of
the efficacy conclusions.
285
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

Conclusions and Recommendations
The evidence from the two pivotal phase 3 studies—TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002—
provides substantial evidence of the effectiveness of oveporexton for the treatment of NT1.
The evidence is characterized by the following key features that collectively support a high
degree of confidence in the robustness and clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect:
•

Large, highly statistically significant treatment effects on the primary endpoint (MWT) and
all pre-specified secondary endpoints in Study TAK-861-3001, with independent replication
in Study TAK-861-3002.

•

Objective, physiologically grounded primary and secondary endpoints (MWT, nPSGmeasured REM latency), which are not susceptible to expectation bias and provide strong
evidence of a genuine pharmacological effect, independent of any potential influence of
functional unblinding. As discussed in Section 8.3, the relatively high incidence of
treatment-emergent adverse events—notably insomnia and urinary symptoms—disclosed
in the informed consent forms, combined with a randomization ratio favoring active
treatment, raised a theoretical concern for functional unblinding; however, this concern is
substantially mitigated by the objective nature of the primary and secondary endpoints.

•

Demonstrated dose-response relationship across the majority of endpoints, providing
additional mechanistic support for the observed treatment effects and further reducing the
likelihood that the efficacy findings are attributable to expectation bias alone.

•

Broad coverage of the NT1 pentad, including EDS, cataplexy, hypnagogic/hypnopompic
hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and disturbed/disrupted nighttime sleep, supporting the
Applicant's symptom-agnostic approach to the NT1 indication.

•

Robust and consistent results across sensitivity analyses, subgroups, and study sites, with
no evidence of dependence on any single site or patient subgroup.

•

Meaningful improvements in patient-reported functional outcomes and quality of life,
including the FINI, SF-36, and PGI-C, demonstrating that the objective treatment effects
translate into clinically meaningful benefit from the patient's perspective (of note, FINI, SF36 were not considered appropriate for labeling but support the general concept of
clinically meaningful benefit).

The clinical review team concludes that the evidence from Studies TAK-861-3001 and TAK861-3002 meets the statutory standard of substantial evidence of effectiveness for
oveporexton in the treatment of NT1 and supports the Applicant's proposed symptomagnostic indication.

9 Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
FDA did not hold an advisory committee (AC) meeting during this marketing application
review. The team agreed that outside expertise was not necessary because there were no
issues that would benefit from advisory committee discussion.
The safety profile did not raise significant safety issues that were unexpected or that required
discussion with an AC; there were no significant efficacy issues, as both pivotal phase 3
studies yielded highly significant and robust results across the primary and all pre-specified
secondary endpoints.

10 Pediatrics
Oveporexton has been granted orphan drug designation in the United States for the
treatment of NT1, the proposed indication; therefore, pediatric studies are not required
under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) under section 505B(a)(3) of the Federal Food,
Drug, and Cosmetic Act.
At the time the Applicant submitted the original NDA, orphan drug designation had not yet
been granted. Accordingly, the Applicant submitted an agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan

(b) (4)

In light of the subsequently granted orphan drug designation, no further requirement to
evaluate the safety and effectiveness of oveporexton in the pediatric population is applicable.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
Oveporexton (Orzeyful) is a first-in-class OX2R agonist approved for the chronic treatment of
NT1 in adults. Because NT1 affects individuals of reproductive age and requires long-term
pharmacotherapy, characterizing the safety of oveporexton in pregnancy, lactation, and
special populations is essential to support safe use in the postmarket setting.
The clinical development program excluded pregnant and breastfeeding women; thus, no
human data on pregnancy or infant outcomes, or on breast milk transfer was available. The
nonclinical reproductive and developmental toxicology program identified findings at relevant
exposures that cannot be directly extrapolated to humans without confirmatory postmarket
data.
Additionally, the preapproval hepatic impairment study (TAK-861-1004) evaluated only mild
and moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A and B); the effect of severe hepatic
impairment (Child-Pugh C) on oveporexton pharmacokinetics was not studied, despite
oveporexton being almost entirely cleared by hepatic CYP3A4-mediated metabolism (~91%
fmCYP3A in vivo).
To address these gaps, the following postmarketing studies are required:
•

5020-1: Conduct a retrospective pregnancy cohort study using claims or electronic
health record data with medical chart validation that is adequately powered to assess
major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, preterm births,
and small-for-gestational-age births in women exposed to oveporexton during
pregnancy compared to appropriate comparator population(s).

•

5020-2: Collect data from a prospective pregnancy exposure registry, preferably a
disease-based multiproduct pregnancy registry, using a cohort analysis that compares
the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women exposed to drug name regardless
of indication during pregnancy with unexposed comparator population(s) in a timely
manner. Align the study protocol with protocol(s) outside the United States to reach
the target sample size. The registry should identify and record pregnancy
complications, major and minor congenital malformations, pregnancy complications,
spontaneous abortion, stillbirths, elective pregnancy terminations, preterm births,
small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal
growth and development. Outcomes described in the protocol will be assessed
throughout pregnancy. Infant outcomes, including effects on postnatal growth and
development, will be assessed through at least the first year of life.

•

5020-3: Conduct a lactation study to measure concentrations of oveporexton and its
major metabolites in breast milk using a validated assay. A mother-infant pair study is
recommended. Assess the effects on the breastfed infant, if available, based on study
population.

293
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
•

5020-4: Evaluate the effect of severe hepatic impairment on safety and
pharmacokinetics of oveporexton.

Clinical Pharmacology
In Vitro Studies
Protein Binding
Study Method: The in vitro protein binding of [³H]-oveporexton in rat, monkey, and human
plasma, and the binding of [¹⁴C]-oveporexton in human serum albumin (HSA) solution, α1-acid
glycoprotein (AGP) solution, and human liver microsomes (HLM) were determined by
equilibrium dialysis at 37°C for 8 hours. Total radioactivity (TRA) was measured by liquid
scintillation counting (LSC).
Results: In vitro protein binding indicated that the binding of [³H]-oveporexton to human
plasma proteins is high (>96%) and concentration independent.
Table 108. Mean Oveporexton Protein Binding to HSA, AGP, and Human Liver Microsomes

Source: Study report DDA-A41-059.

In Vitro Metabolism
Study Method: The in vitro metabolic stability of oveporexton was investigated using
hepatocytes from rats, monkeys, and humans, including CYP reaction phenotyping using
recombinant human CYP-expressing microsomes (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and
3A4), hepatocyte incubations (mice, rats, monkeys, and humans), and human liver
microsomes (HLM).
Results: Oveporexton (1 μmol/L) was metabolized by CYP2C9, 2D6, and 3A4 in the presence
of a NADPH-generating system. The intrinsic clearance for CYP2C9, 2D6, and 3A4 was 0.0165,
0.0147, and 0.275 μL/min/pmol P450, respectively. Oveporexton was not metabolized by the
other CYP-expressing microsomes (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, or 2C19). The relative contribution
ratios of CYP2C9, 2D6, and 3A4, calculated using the Relative Activity Factor (RAF) method,
were 2.6%, 0.1%, and 97.3%, respectively.
Oveporexton was metabolized to 11 metabolites in hepatocytes derived from animals and
humans. Unchanged oveporexton accounted for 95.6% of the total circulating radioactivity,
with two metabolites (M5 and M6) each accounting for <3.5% of radioactivity. In urine,
unchanged oveporexton was not detected, and 4 metabolites accounted for 0.4% of the
300
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
administered dose. In feces, unchanged oveporexton accounted for 2.4% of the administered
dose, and 9 metabolites were identified, with values ranging from 1.7% to 12.9% of the
administered dose.
Figure 29. Metabolic Pathways of Oveporexton in Mice, Rat, Monkey, and Human

Source: Study report DDA-A41-028

Clinical Implications: Oveporexton is metabolized primarily by CYP3A4, and eliminated
predominantly via fecal excretion of metabolites, with minimal renal involvement. No major
circulating metabolites (>10%) were identified in plasma.
In Vitro Drug-Drug Interactions (DDIs)
CYP Inhibition
Study Method: The inhibitory effects of oveporexton (0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, and 30 μmol/L) on the
marker activities of 7 CYP isoforms (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4/5) were
evaluated in human liver microsomes. In addition, time-dependent inhibition (TDI) effects of
oveporexton on CYP activities were examined in human liver microsomes pre-incubated for
30 minutes with oveporexton in the presence of NADPH.
Results: Oveporexton inhibited CYP2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4/5 activities with halfmaximal inhibitory concentration (IC₅₀) values of 21, 18, 13, 4.6, and 18 μmol/L (midazolam
301
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
1′-hydroxylation), respectively. Oveporexton did not inhibit CYP1A2, 2B6, or 3A4/5
(testosterone 6β-hydroxylation) activities (IC₅₀ >30 μmol/L). In the pre-incubation study,
oveporexton inhibited CYP2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A4/5 (midazolam 1′-hydroxylation and
testosterone 6β-hydroxylation) activities with IC₅₀ values of 29, 22, 16, 3.7, 4.0, and 4.7
μmol/L, respectively. Oveporexton did not inhibit CYP1A2 or 2B6 activity (IC₅₀ >30 μmol/L).
These results indicate that oveporexton has direct inhibitory effects in vitro on CYP2C8,
CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5 activities.
Clinical Implications: Although oveporexton at high concentrations showed inhibition
potential for CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5, the clinical unbound Cmax
following the proposed dosage regimen (2 mg BID) is low (~0.0015 μmol/L) and Cmax,u/IC50
ratios are well below the regulatory cutoff established in the guidance on M12 Drug
Interaction Studies (0.02). Therefore, the risk of DDIs with oveporexton acting as an inhibitor
is low.
CYP Induction
Study Method: The induction potential of oveporexton (0.1, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, and 30 μmol/L) on
the gene expression of 5 CYP isoforms (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, and 3A4) was examined using
cryopreserved human hepatocytes prepared from three donors.
Results: The mean fold change in gene expression of CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, and 3A4 at an
oveporexton concentration of 30 μmol/L was 2.55, 4.47, 4.42, 2.78, and 36.3, respectively.
There were no significant differences among hepatocytes prepared from the three donors.
The mean maximal induction (Emax) and concentration that exerts half-maximal induction
(EC₅₀) for CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, and 3A4 were 1.89 and 6.21 μmol/L, 4.38 and 2.87 μmol/L,
4.41 and 3.54 μmol/L, 4.62 and 50.1 μmol/L, and 44.1 and 2.75 μmol/L, respectively. These
results indicate that oveporexton has induction potential in vitro for CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9,
and 3A4.
Clinical Implications: Although oveporexton at high concentrations (30 μmol/L) showed
induction potential for CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, and 3A4, the clinical unbound Cmax following
the proposed dosage regimen (2 mg BID) is low (~0.0015 μmol/L) and Cmax,u/EC50 ratios are
well below the regulatory cutoff established in the guidance on M12 Drug Interaction Studies
(0.02). Therefore, the risk of DDIs with oveporexton acting as an inducer is low.
Transporter Interaction
Study Method: Multiple cell-based assays using transfected cell lines: MDCKII cells expressing
P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) were used for evaluation of
oveporexton as a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, and various cell lines (MDCK-MDR1, LLC-PK1,
Caco-2, HEK293) for inhibition studies.
Transporters Tested: Efflux transporters: P-gp, BCRP and bile salt export pump (BSEP)

302
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Uptake transporters: Organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP1B1 and OATP1B3),
organic anion transporter (OAT1 and OAT3), organic cation transporter (OCT1 and OCT2), and
multidrug and toxin extrusion protein (MATE1 and MATE2-K).
Results: Oveporexton did not inhibit P-gp-mediated digoxin transport or BCRP-mediated
prazosin transport at concentrations up to 100 μmol/L. Oveporexton inhibited BSEP-mediated
taurocholate transport with an IC50 value of 7.225 μmol/L. Oveporexton did not inhibit
MATE1- or MATE2K-mediated metformin accumulation at concentrations up to 4.5 μmol/L.
Oveporexton did not inhibit OAT1-mediated tenofovir, OAT3-mediated estrone 3-sulfate,
OCT1-mediated sumatriptan, or OCT2-mediated metformin accumulation at concentrations
up to 10 μmol/L. Oveporexton (0.3 to 100 μmol/L) inhibited the OATP1B1- and OATP1B3mediated transport of typical substrates with IC50 values of 0.910 and 37.7 μmol/L,
respectively.
Clinical Implications: Given that the estimated clinical unbound Cmax following the proposed
dosage regimen (2 mg BID) is low (~0.0015 μmol/L), the Cmax,u/IC50 ratios were well below
thresholds of clinical concern (typically <0.1) for all transporters tested. Therefore,
oveporexton is unlikely to cause clinically meaningful transporter-mediated drug interactions.
The lack of P-gp and BCRP substrate activity suggests minimal risk for drug interactions
involving these major efflux transporters.

Summary of Individual Clinical Pharmacology Studies
Clinical Study Report TAK-861-1001
Title
A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study to Assess the Safety, Tolerability,
Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamics of Single and Multiple Doses of TAK-861 in Healthy
Adult and Elderly Subjects and Subjects with Narcolepsy Type 1.

304
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Primary Objectives:
To investigate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 when single and/or multiple oral doses
are administered to healthy adults, healthy elderly subjects, and subjects with narcolepsy
type 1 (NT1).

•

Secondary Objectives:
•

To investigate the PK of TAK-861 following single and/or multiple oral doses of suspension
and/or tablet formulation in healthy adults.

•

To investigate the PK of TAK-861 following multiple oral doses of tablet formulation in
healthy elderly subjects and subjects with NT1.

•

To assess the effect of food on the PK of the TAK-861 tablet formulation.

Additional/Exploratory Objectives:
•

To assess CYP3A4/5 inductive activity via the 4β-hydroxycholesterol/cholesterol ratio (Parts
B–E).

•

To assess OATP1B1/3 inhibition via coproporphyrin (CPI and CPIII) biomarkers (Parts B and
E).

•

To evaluate TAK-861 CNS penetration through cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) PK (Part F).

•

To assess the PD effects of TAK-861 on NT1 symptoms including MWT, ESS, WCR, CGI, PGI,
nPSG, and other measures (Part D).

•

To evaluate orexin CSF levels following TAK-861 administration (Part F).

Study Design
A first-in-human (FIH), phase 1/1b, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study of
single- and multiple-rising oral doses of TAK-861 conducted across seven independent parts
(Parts A through G; this report covers Parts A through F). The study was conducted at sites in
Japan and the United States. Details of the study cohorts can be found in the table below.

PK Results
Part A – Single Dose (Healthy Japanese Adults, Fasted)
Following single oral administration, TAK-861 was absorbed with median tmax of 0.60 to 2.50
h. Geometric mean Cmax and AUC∞ increased approximately dose proportionally over the
dose range of 1- to 10-mg suspension and 5- to 75-mg tablet. Mean t½z ranged from 17.15 to
23.61 h; CL/F and Vz/F were generally stable across dose levels. Urinary excretion of
unchanged TAK-861 was <1%.
Food effect (high-fat, high-calorie meal): A single 5-mg tablet co-administered with a high-fat
meal increased Cmax by ~46% and AUC∞ by ~37% versus fasted conditions; median tmax was
unchanged (1.50 h).

Clinical Study Report TAK-861-1003
Title
A Phase 1 Study to Characterize the Mass Balance, Pharmacokinetics, Absorption,
Metabolism, and Excretion of [¹⁴C]-TAK-861 in Healthy Adult Male Participants
Study Objectives
Primary Objective
•

To evaluate the mass balance of [¹⁴C]-TAK-861 in urine and feces following a single oral
administration of 25 mg (~1 μCi) [¹⁴C]-TAK-861.

Secondary Objectives
•

Characterize the PK of TAK-861 in plasma and total radioactivity (TRA) concentration
equivalents in plasma and whole blood following a single oral suspension dose of 25 mg
[¹⁴C]-TAK-861.

•

Evaluate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 and [¹⁴C]-TAK-861.

Exploratory Objective
•

Characterize the metabolite profile of TAK-861 in plasma, urine, and feces following a single
oral administration of 25 mg [¹⁴C]-TAK-861.

317
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Study Design
A phase 1, open-label, single-dose study in six healthy adult male participants. On Day 1,
participants received a single oral dose of 25 mg (~1 μCi; 0.037 MBq) [¹⁴C]-TAK-861 as an oral
suspension after an overnight fast (≥10 h). Actual doses administered ranged from 26.1 to
26.2 mg (1.03 to 1.04 μCi). Participants were confined to the clinical research unit (CRU) until
discharge criteria were met (≥90% TRA recovery in urine + feces, or TRA in both matrices ≤
lower limit of quantitation (LLOQ) for ≥2 consecutive 24-h intervals) or up to a maximum of
14 days postdose (Day 15).
Serial blood sampling (plasma and whole blood) was performed for TAK-861 PK, TRA
determination, and metabolite profiling. Complete urine and fecal collections were made for
TRA quantitation and metabolite profiling. A 30-day follow-up contact was performed. The
estimated total radiation dose from ~1 μCi [¹⁴C]-TAK-861 was below U.S. FDA and
International Commission on Radiological Protection (ICRP) limits for radiation exposure in
human subjects.

Pharmacokinetic Results
Plasma TAK-861 and Total Radioactivity (TRA) Concentrations
Plasma TAK-861 concentrations were quantifiable in all participants by 0.25 h post-dose and
in the majority of participants through 168 h post-dose (2 participants through 240 h).
Arithmetic mean peak plasma concentration (148.3 ng/mL) of TAK-861 reached at 2 h postdose.
Whole blood TRA was quantifiable in all participants by 0.25 h and in the majority through
240 h (one participant through 336 h). Peak arithmetic mean was 174.5 ng eq TAK-861/mL at
1 h. Plasma TRA was quantifiable in all participants by 0.25 h and through 240 h in all
participants (two through 336 h). Peak arithmetic mean was 254.5 ng eq TAK-861/mL at 1 h.
Unchanged oveporexton was the most abundant drug-related species in plasma (59.9%). No
major metabolites (>10%) were observed in plasma. The two most abundant metabolites in
plasma were M4 (5.12%) and M30 (4.92%); each of the other circulating metabolites
accounted for <4% of the dose. Oveporexton was primarily eliminated through hepatic
oxidation, approximately 80% of the dose recovered in feces was contributed by metabolites.
The higher TRA versus TAK-861 concentrations, longer TRA t½z, and AUCt Ratio of ~0.50 at 120
h indicate the presence of circulating radioactive metabolites (see metabolites profiling for
additional information) in addition to parent drug. Whole blood: plasma TRA partitioning
ratios ranged from 0.6054 to 0.8989, suggesting minimal extent of partitioning into the
cellular fraction of whole blood.

Mass Balance—Excretion of Total Radioactivity
Complete urine and fecal collections were obtained. Mean cumulative TRA recovery was
91.49% of the administered dose. Fecal excretion was the predominant route (80.47%), with
urinary excretion playing a secondary role (11.02%). In urine, unchanged oveporexton
321
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
accounted for only 0.1% of the total dose. The most abundant metabolites were M8 and M9
(glucuronide conjugates of the oxidative metabolites M4 and M12), and M29 (sulfate
conjugate); each of the other metabolites accounted for <1% of the dose. In feces, unchanged
oveporexton contributed only 0.5% to the total dose. The most abundant metabolites (>4%)
were M4 (oxidative metabolite), M16 (carboxylic acid derivative), M12 (oxidative metabolite),
M19 (oxidative defluorinated derivative), and M17 (carboxylic acid derivative); each of the
other metabolites accounted for <4% of the dose.

(b) (4)

Source: Clinical Study Report TAK-861-1003,Figures 11.d

Metabolite Profiling
Primary Biotransformation Pathways
Aromatic oxidation (~39% of dose) → M4 and M12, subsequently conjugated to M8, M9,
M29, M30. Aliphatic oxidation (~12%) → M16 and M17. Oxidative defluorination (~5%) →
M19. M4 (from aromatic oxidation) was the major metabolite contributing ~28% of dose,
predominantly excreted in feces. A mean 80% of dose in feces consisted of oxidative
metabolites, confirming hepatic oxidation as the major clearance mechanism.

Clinical Study TAK-861-1004
Title
A Phase 1, Open-Label Study to Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics, Safety, and Tolerability of
Oral Dosing of TAK-861 in Participants with or without Hepatic Impairment
Primary Objective
•

To characterize the plasma PK of TAK-861 following oral dosing in participants with mild or
moderate HI compared to participants with normal hepatic function. Primary PK endpoints:
Cmax, AUClast, and AUC∞.

Secondary Objective
•

To evaluate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 following oral dosing in participants with
mild or moderate HI and in participants with normal hepatic function.

Exploratory Objectives
•

Characterize unbound plasma TAK-861 PK (Cmax,u, AUClast,u, AUC∞,u, CLu/F, Vz,u/F) in HI versus
normal hepatic function participants.

•

Characterize additional PK parameters (tmax, t½z, CL/F, Vz/F) in HI versus normal hepatic
function.

•

Assess plasma protein binding (fraction unbound, fu) of TAK-861 across cohorts.

Study Design
A phase 1, open-label, parallel-group study conducted at two sites in the United States
(Orlando, FL and Miami, FL). Participants with chronic, stable HI with features of cirrhosis
were enrolled and classified by Child-Pugh score (mild (Class A): 5 and 6; moderate (Class B): 7
to 9). Each HI cohort was matched to a control group of healthy participants with normal
hepatic function by body mass index (BMI; mean ±10%), age (mean ±10 years), and sex ratio

323
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
(±2 per sex). All participants were confined to the clinical research unit from Day −1 through
Day 10.

Treatment Administered
TAK-861 4-mg total dose (T3 tablet formulation), administered as two 2-mg oral tablets given
at least 3 hours apart on Day 1. The first dose was administered under fasting conditions
(≥10 h overnight fast), followed by a light meal (~400 to 500 cal, ~25% fat) approximately 1
hour after the first dose. The second dose was administered 3 hours after the first dose,
followed by a second light meal approximately 1 hour later.
Pharmacokinetic Sampling
Blood samples for total and unbound plasma TAK-861 PK were collected at: pre-dose (Day 1),
and at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 3.25, 3.5, 4, 4.5, 5, 6, 8, and 12 hours post-first dose on Day 1;
then at 24, 36, 48, 72, 96, 120, 168, and 216 hours post-first dose (Days 2 through 10).
Samples for protein binding were collected at three selected postdose time points.
Results
Subject Disposition and Demographics
All 33 enrolled participants received trial intervention and completed the study. No
participants withdrew or discontinued.

Bioanalytical Methods
The bioanalytical methods used in Study TAK-861-1004 are the same as those described in the
bioanalytical report section (Section 16.4.2) of this Appendix (Report Nos.
(b) (4)
CB201176/CB201177; Study No. CB210057;
). Total and
unbound plasma TAK-861 concentrations were quantified by validated LC-MS/MS methods at
(b) (4)

Pharmacokinetic Results
After oral administration of TAK-861 4 mg (2 mg BID, 3 hours apart), two plasma
concentration peaks were observed reflecting the two doses. In participants with mild HI,
arithmetic mean peak total plasma TAK-861 concentrations after Dose 1 (12.83 ng/mL) and
Dose 2 (22.58 ng/mL) occur at approximately 1.5 hours and 5 hours (2 hours after Dose 2)
post-initial dose, respectively. Concentrations were higher throughout the sampling period in
participants with mild HI compared to matched healthy controls.
In participants with moderate HI, arithmetic mean concentrations after Dose 1 (10.80 ng/mL)
and Dose 2 (15.84 ng/mL) occur at 1.5 hours and 5 hours post-initial dose, respectively.
Concentrations tended to be slightly lower around the peaks but higher during the
elimination phase compared to matched healthy controls, consistent with reduced hepatic
clearance.

Clinical Study TAK-861-1005
Title
A Phase 1, Open-Label Study to Evaluate the Pharmacokinetics, Safety, and Tolerability of
Oral Dosing of TAK-861 in Participants with or without Renal Impairment
Study Objectives
Primary Objective
•

To characterize the plasma PK of TAK-861 following oral dosing in participants with severe
RI (eGFR <30 mL/min, not on hemodialysis) compared to participants with normal renal
function (eGFR ≥90 mL/min). Primary endpoints: Cmax, AUClast, AUC∞.

Secondary Objective
•

To evaluate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 following oral dosing in participants with
severe RI and in participants with normal renal function.

Exploratory Objectives
•

Characterize unbound plasma TAK-861 PK (Cmax,u, AUClast,u, AUC∞,u, CLu/F, Vz,u/F) and
additional PK parameters (tmax, t½z, CL/F, Vz/F) in severe RI versus normal renal function;
assess plasma protein binding (fraction unbound, fu).

Study Design
A phase 1, open-label, parallel-group study. Two cohorts: Cohort 1: participants with severe
RI (eGFR <30 mL/min); Cohort 2: healthy participants with normal renal function (eGFR
≥90 mL/min) matched to Cohort 1 by BMI (±10%), age (±10 years), and sex (±2 per sex). eGFR
was calculated using the Modification of Diet in Renal Disease (MDRD) formula. Participants
were confined to the CRU from Day −1 through Day 7.

328
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Treatments
TAK-861 4 mg (T3 tablet) as two 2-mg tablets given at least 3 hours apart on Day 1. First dose
under fasting conditions (≥10 h), followed by a light meal (~400 to 500 cal, ~25% fat) ~1 h
after each dose.
PK Sampling Schedule
Pre-dose and at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 3.25, 3.5, 4, 4.5, 5, 6, 8, 12 h post-first dose (Day 1),
then at 24, 36, 48, 72, 96, 120, and 144 h post-first dose (Days 2–7). Protein binding samples
collected at two postdose time points.
Subject Disposition and Demographics
All 20 enrolled participants received trial intervention and completed the study (100%
completion). No participants withdrew or were discontinued.

Clinical Study TAK-861-1007
Title
A Phase 1, Open-Label Study to Evaluate the Relative Bioavailability of Immediate Release (IR)
TAK-861 Tablets, and the Effects of CYP3A Inhibitors Fluconazole and Itraconazole, and CYP3A
Inducer Phenytoin on the Single-Dose Pharmacokinetic Profile of TAK-861 in Healthy Adults
Study Objectives
Primary Objectives
•

Group 1 (rBA): Evaluate the oral bioavailability (BA) of the T2 IR tablet relative to the T1 IR
tablet formulation.

•

Group 2 (Fluconazole Drug-Drug Interaction (DDI)): Evaluate the effect of multiple-dose
fluconazole (moderate CYP3A inhibitor) on the PK of a single oral dose of TAK-861.

•

Group 3 (Phenytoin DDI): Evaluate the effect of multiple-dose phenytoin (strong CYP3A
inducer) on the PK of a single oral dose of TAK-861.

•

Group 4 (Itraconazole DDI): Evaluate the effect of multiple-dose itraconazole (strong CYP3A
inhibitor) on the PK of a single oral dose of TAK-861.

Secondary Objective
•

Evaluate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 following single oral dosing, with and
without the interacting perpetrator drugs, in healthy adults.

Primary PK Endpoints
•

Cmax, AUC∞ (AUC∞_obs), and AUClast of plasma TAK-861.
332

Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Study Design
A phase 1, open-label, four-group study in healthy adults aged 18 to 55 years (BMI 18 to
30 kg/m²). Groups 1 to 3 were conducted concurrently; Group 4 initiated after review of
Groups 1 to 3 data. All participants were confined at the clinical research unit for the duration
of each group period.

Pharmacokinetic Results
Group 1—Relative Bioavailability (T2 vs. T1 IR Tablet, 10 mg)
Following a single oral 10 mg dose, the concentration-time profiles of both formulations were
well characterized. Median tmax of T1 and T2 formulations was 1.5 h and 2.0 h, respectively,
with individual values ranging from 1 to 4 h for both. Geometric mean Cmax was higher for T2
(59.88 ng/mL) compared to T1 (50.93 ng/mL); AUC values were comparable. Mean t½z and
CL/F were similar between formulations (~25 h and ~12 L/h).

TAK-861 T2 IR tablets showed a modest (~18%) increase in Cmax vs. T1 IR tablets, with the
upper 90% CI for Cmax slightly exceeding the 125% equivalence limit. AUC∞ and AUClast were 9
to 10% higher for T2 but within equivalence limits, indicating comparable total exposure. The
T2 formulation was selected for subsequent studies.
Group 2—DDI With Fluconazole (Moderate CYP3A Inhibitor)
In 12 participants, a single 10-mg TAK-861 dose was administered alone (Day 1) and in
combination with fluconazole at steady state (Day 9: fluconazole 400 mg loading on Day 6,
then 200 mg QD Days 7 to 13). TAK-861 + Fluconazole resulted in substantially higher
concentrations throughout the sampling period. Median tmax was delayed (3.0 h versus 2.0 h)
and mean t½z increased to ~48 h versus ~27 h.

Co-administration with a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, fluconazole increased the Cmax of TAK861 by ~50% and AUC∞ by ~187%. These results confirm clinically meaningful CYP3Amediated inhibition of TAK-861 metabolism by fluconazole. The review findings suggest that a
dosage reduction (0.5 mg BID) is recommended when TAK-861 is concomitantly used with
moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor.
Group 3—DDI With Phenytoin (Strong CYP3A Inducer)
In 11 evaluable participants (1 withdrew before phenytoin administration), a single 10-mg

steady state (100 mg TID Days 6 to 23, with TAK-861 on Day 19). Mean concentrations were
markedly lower with phenytoin co-administration compared to TAK-861 alone; only 18% of
participants had quantifiable concentrations at 96 h post-dose when TAK-861 was
concomitantly administered with phenytoin. However, all participants who received TAK-861
alone had quantifiable concentrations at 96 h post-dose. Mean t½z shortened to ~14 h vs.
~32 h.

Co-administration with the strong CYP3A inducer phenytoin decreased TAK-861 Cmax by ~56%

Group 4—DDI With Itraconazole (Strong CYP3A Inhibitor)

9). The TAK-861 dose was reduced to 1 mg (from 10 mg) after review of Groups 1 to 3 data
and updated PBPK modeling to ensure adequate safety margins. Concentrations were higher
throughout the sampling period following itraconazole co-administration; all 3 participants
had quantifiable concentrations through 336 h post-dose. Mean t½z increased markedly to
~125 h vs. ~27 h.

Co-administration with the strong CYP3A inhibitor itraconazole increased the Cmax of TAK-861

strong CYP3A inhibitors was studied in only 3 subjects, the magnitude of this interaction is
consistent with itraconazole's strong inhibitory effect on CYP3A4/5 and confirms TAK-861 as a
sensitive CYP3A substrate. The results suggest that concomitant use of TAK-861 with strong
CYP3A4 inhibitor is contraindicated.
Clinical Study TAK-861-1010
Title
A Phase 1, Open-Label, Randomized, Crossover Trial to Evaluate the Effect of Food on the
Pharmacokinetics of TAK-861 in Healthy Adult Participants
Study Objectives
Primary Objective:
•

To evaluate the effect of food on the oral bioavailability of a single dose of 2 mg TAK-861
administered under fed conditions compared to fasted conditions in healthy adult
participants. Primary PK endpoints: Cmax, AUCinf, and AUClast.

Secondary Objective:
•

To evaluate the safety and tolerability of TAK-861 following a single oral administration of
2 mg TAK-861 under fed and fasted conditions.
340

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Exploratory Objectives:
•

Characterize additional plasma PK parameters (tmax, t½z, CL/F, Vz/F) and additional safety
parameters (MAVs for laboratory, vital signs, ECG, and C-SSRS; change from baseline).

Study Design
A phase 1, open-label, randomized, 2-way crossover food effect trial. Twelve healthy adult
male participants were enrolled and randomized 1:1 to one of two treatment sequences (AB
or BA) with a 5-day washout between periods. All participants were confined to the CRU for
each treatment period (Day -1 through Day 6 of each period).
Treatments Administered
Treatment A (Reference): Single oral dose of 2 mg TAK-861 (1 × 2 mg T3 tablet) under fasted
conditions (overnight fast ≥10 h; continued fast for ≥4 h post-dose).
Treatment B (Test): Single oral dose of 2 mg TAK-861 (1 × 2 mg T3 tablet) under fed
conditions (overnight fast ≥10 h; standard FDA high-fat, high-calorie breakfast [800 to 1000
kcal, ≥50% fat] consumed within 30 minutes immediately before dosing).
PK Sampling Schedule
Blood samples for plasma TAK-861 PK collected pre-dose (0 h) and at 0.25, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4,
6, 8, 12, 24, 36, 48, 72, 96, and 120 h post-dose in each period. The 120 h sample from Period
1 also served as the pre-dose sample for Period 2.
Subject Disposition and Demographics
All 12 enrolled participants received both treatments and completed the trial (100%
completion). No participants withdrew or discontinued. There were no protocol deviations.

No clinically meaningful food effect was observed on the PK of T3 tablet formulation of TAK861. Cmax, AUClast, and AUCinf were equivalent under fed and fasted conditions (the GMRs and

Executive Summary
Table 139. Model Risk Assessment for Pharmacometrics Analysis

Source: Pharmacometrics Reviewer’s analysis

Is the dose adjustment needed for the patents with mild to moderate hepatic
impairment or moderate to severe renal impairment?
Population PK and exposure-response analyses for safety were conducted
Moderate. PK data were collected from patients with mild to moderate
hepatic impairment or moderate to severe renal impairment. The studied PK
exposure range was relatively large in pivotal trials (1 mg BID and 2 mg BID).
Moderate. Wrong decision may increase the risk of safety outcomes in

Moderate. By combining the moderate model influence and moderate
consequence of wrong decision, the overall model risk is moderate.

The effect of HI on oveporexton PK was evaluated in a dedicated study (TAK-861-1004) in
patients with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) HI. In participants
343
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
with mild HI, total oveporexton AUC∞ was 28% higher and Cmax was 17% higher compared to
participants with normal hepatic function. In participants with moderate HI, AUC∞ was 23%
higher and Cmax was 15% lower compared to controls. RI was evaluated in a reduced design
study (TAK-861-1005). In participants with severe RI (estimated glomerular filtration rate
[eGFR] ≥15 to <30 mL/min; not requiring dialysis), oveporexton AUC∞ was 45% higher, AUCt
was 35% higher, and Cmax was 16% lower compared to matched participants with normal
renal function (eGFR 90 mL/min or greater). Population PK analysis, based on data from
patients enrolled in the dedicated PK trials as well as from patients with HI or RI in pivotal
trials, confirmed the PK exposures of oveporexton were increased in patients with mild to
moderate HI or moderate to severe RI.
Exposure-response analysis suggests that the probability of insomnia, urinary events, and
micturition urgency increased with increasing exposures of oveporexton. The predicted
probability of insomnia for median Cavg,ss of 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID were 49.2% [95% CI:
42.2%-56.3%] and 56.1% [95% CI: 50.4%-61.7%], respectively. The predicted probability of
urinary events for median Cavg,ss of 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID in North America were 43.6% [95%
CI: 30.6%-57.6%] and 48.4% [95% CI: 35.4%-61.7%], respectively. The predicted probability of
micturition urgency for median Cavg,ss of 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID in North America were 16%
[95% CI: 11.7%-21.5%] and 20.5% [95% CI: 16.2%-25.6%], respectively. However, the
predicted difference between two dosing regimens were relatively close. In addition, the
observed percentage of events as well as the percentage of resolved events for insomnia,
urinary events, and micturition urgency were comparable between 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID
based on pooled data from Study TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002. The event duration for
these three safety events were comparable as well between 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID.
The probabilities to have insomnia, urinary events, and micturition urgency in patients with HI
(mild to moderate) or RI (moderate to severe) were largely within the range of those in
patients with normal hepatic and renal function. Based on the comparable dose-response for
safety as well as the limited increase in the probability of safety events in patients with
hepatic or renal impairment, dose adjustment based on HI (mild and moderate) and RI
(moderate and severe) is not recommended.

In total, the population PK model was developed using 8535 PK observations of 494 subjects
from phase 1 studies TAK-861-1001 and TAK-861-1007, TAK-861-1004, and TAK-861-1005 as
well as from non-placebo arms in studies TAK-861-2001, TAK-861-2002, TAK-861-3001, and
TAK-861-3002. The baseline characteristics stratified by study are summarized in Table 140
and Table 141.

In general, the proposed population PK model is acceptable. Iterative two-stage estimation
was run first to obtain stable starting values for the subsequent stochastic approximation
expectation-maximization (SAEM) step to get final parameter estimates. The importance
sampling was performed to get objective function value. During reproducing the Applicant’s
model, the pharmacometrics reviewer changed SAEM from using one Metropolis-Hastings
chain to using two chains for the stochastic individual-parameter sampling step. Increasing
ISAMPLE from 1 to 2 (default) improved the robustness of the SAEM search. The parameter
estimates of the pharmacometrics reviewer’s reproduced model were reasonably close to
those estimated by Applicant. The predictive capability of reproduced model appeared to be
reasonable (Figure 46). All the subsequent population PK model related evaluations were
performed using the reviewer’s reproduced model.

In the development program of oveporexton, three immediate-release (IR) formulations (T1
[phase 1 tablets], T2 [phase 2 tablets], and T3 [phase 3 tablets]) were used. A comparative
bioavailability study (TAK-861-1007) was conducted to establish the PK bridge between T1
and T2 formulations. However, a comparative bioavailability study for T2 versus T3 was not
performed. Both pivotal safety and efficacy studies (TAK-861-3001 and TAK-861-3002), the
food effect study (TAK-861-1010), and hepatic and renal impairment studies (TAK-861-1004
and TAK-861-1005) were conducted with the T3 formulation, which shares the same core
tablet composition as the registration and commercial tablets. As a result, it is practical to
bridge the T2 and T3 using population PK analysis. The T2 and T3 were not estimated to have
different impact on PK parameters, and the simulation suggested the PK exposures of T2 and
T3 were comparable (Figure 47).

The effect of HI on oveporexton PK was evaluated in a dedicated study (TAK-861-1004) in
patients with mild (Child-Pugh Class A) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class B) HI. RI was
evaluated in a reduced design study (TAK-861-1005). Population PK analysis was performed to
further evaluate the impact of HI and RI on PK. Mild and moderate HI as well as moderate and
severe RI were estimated to decrease the apparent clearance. Population PK analysis included
patients with HI or RI from dedicated PK trials as well as the patients with RI or HI from
pivotal trials. PK simulation using the studied subjects suggested PK exposures at steady state
increased in the patients with mild and moderate HI (Figure 48) as well as in patients with
moderate and severe RI (Figure 49). Substantial overlaps in exposures to oveporexton were
observed in patients with HI or RI compared to those in subjects with normal organ function.

Dose-Response for Efficacy Based on Data From TAK-861-3001
Study TAK-861-3001 was conducted to evaluate the efficacy and safety of oveporexton for
the treatment of narcolepsy with cataplexy (narcolepsy type 1). The primary efficacy endpoint
was change from Baseline to Week 12 in mean sleep latency from the four maintenance of
wakefulness test (MWT) trials. Oveporexton, at the doses of 1 and 2 mg BID, demonstrated
statistically significant improvements compared with placebo for the change from Baseline to
Week 12 in mean sleep latency on the MWT (Figure 54).

359
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
Figure 54. Mean Sleep Latency From the MWT in Study TAK-861-3001

Source: Study TAK-861-3001 report, Figure 3

Both 1 mg BID and 2 mg BID demonstrated effectiveness in pivotal trial TAK-861-3001,
supporting the approval of both dosage regimens. The response of 2 mg BID arm was
numerically better than that of 1 mg BID arm at both Week 8 and Week 12, suggesting
potential dose-response relationship in MWT.

Physiologically-Based Pharmacokinetic Analyses Review
Executive Summary
The objective of this review was to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s Physiologically
based Pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling analyses to predict the following:
•

Interaction effect of a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor on the exposure of oveporexton

•

Interaction effect of moderate and weak CYP3A4 inducers on the exposure of oveporexton

•

Interaction effect of oveporexton on the exposure of a CYP3A substrate

370
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK submission (Reports TAK/25/A
associated modeling files and response to FDA IR dated February 17, 2026) and concludes:
•

A weak interaction effect on oveporexton exposure was predicted with coadministration of
a weak CYP3A inhibitor (AUC increase <2-fold) or weak CYP3A4 inducer (AUC decreased by
<50%).

•

Coadministration of oveporexton with a moderate CYP3A inducer was predicted to
decrease oveporexton AUCtau and Cmax by 72% and 55%, respectively.

•

Co-administration of oveporexton 2 mg BID (3 h apart) with a strong or moderate CYP3A
inducer was predicted to result in approximately 2-fold and 5-fold lower AUC, compared to
administration of oveporexton 1 mg and 2 mg BID (3 h apart), respectively.

•

Co-administration of oveporexton 0.5 mg BID (3 h apart) with a moderate CYP3A inhibitor
was predicted to result in a similar oveporexton AUC compared to oveporexton 1 mg BID (3
h apart) dosing alone.

•

The PBPK analysis, in combination with clinical PK data of oveporexton at steady state,
indicated a low likelihood for a clinically relevant interaction of oveporexton 2 mg BID with
a substrate of CYP3A4.

Methods
Oveporexton PBPK Modeling
The PBPK analyses were performed using the software Simcyp® version 23. The oveporexton
PBPK model consisted of a first-order absorption model and a full PBPK distribution model.
A schematic representation of the PBPK modeling strategy, including the key PBPK modeling
steps and clinical studies used in the model development, verification and application, is
shown in Figure 65. The input parameters for oveporexton PBPK model are listed in Table
148.

Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)

coefficient; MBI: mechanism-based inhibition; tlag: lag time; VSS: steady-state volume of distribution.

Simulations were conducted using the default population model for healthy volunteers. For
model verification, simulations of 10 virtual trials of each clinical study were generated to
assess variability across groups. A fixed-trial design was used in which individual simulated
ages, weights, heights, and sexes were matched to the clinical study demographics.
373
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
For model application, 10 virtual trials of 20 healthy subjects (50% female) aged 18 to 70
years receiving multiple oral doses of oveporexton (administered as formulation T3) in the
absence and presence of CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers administered for 28 days were
generated.
The default library models (Simcyp version 23) of itraconazole, fluconazole, efavirenz,
modafinil, dexamethasone, and midazolam were used without modification. The library
model of phenytoin was modified (CYP2C9 and CYP3A4 Indmax values) to improve model
predictive performance of multiple-dose exposure of phenytoin. The modified phenytoin
model predicted all the CYP3A4 DDIs for model verification within 0.7- to 1.5-fold of the
observed value. The library model of cimetidine was modified (CYP3A Ki value), by the PBPK
reviewer, based on version 25 model. All simulations were performed under steady-state
conditions for the CYP3A4 inhibitor or inducer.
Additional Comments
In vitro studies in human hepatocytes, hepatic microsomes, and recombinant CYP expressing
microsomes indicated oveporexton clearance is primarily mediated by CYP metabolism. In
vitro recombinant CYP data were used to assign the initial value for the relative contribution
of CYP3A4 to the clearance of oveporexton (fmCYP3A =97.3%) (Report DDA-A41-011). The
fmCYP3A value was further optimized to 91% using the clinical DDI effect of itraconazole on
single-dose oveporexton PK (Study TAK-861-1007). The in vitro estimated fmCYP3A4 value
overpredicted the interaction effect of itraconazole on oveporexton AUC by more than 2-fold
in the initial PBPK model of oveporexton. The in vivo optimized fmCYP3A4 value was verified
using the clinical DDI effect of fluconazole or phenytoin on single-dose oveporexton PK (Study
TAK-861-1007). Additionally, the metabolite profiling from human mass balance study (Study
TAK-861-1007) indicated the primary metabolites M16 and M17 accounted for 7% and 4.5%
of total metabolites in urine and feces, for 91.5% of total percentage dose recovered. M16
and M17 are carboxylic acid derivates which metabolism could potentially be mediated by
non-CYP pathways (e.g., carboxylesterases). Thus, in vivo metabolite data support the
percentage decrease of relative CYP3A contribution to parent clearance compared to in vitro
data.
Oveporexton exhibited time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4 in vitro (Report DDA-A41-017),
with estimates of apparent enzyme inhibition constant (Ki) and inactivation rate (kinact)
values of 11.83 μM and 3.047 1/h, respectively (Report TKD-BCS-00565-R1). Oveporexton also
exhibited concentration dependent induction of CYP3A4 mRNA in human hepatocytes, with
estimates of CYP3A4 Indmax of 44.1 and IndC50 of 2.75 μM (from one donor) (Report DDAA41-016). For model input, the in vitro estimated CYP3A4 Indmax was calibrated to 15.01,
using the in vitro rifampin data. The in vitro estimate for competitive inhibition of CYP3A4 IC50
of 18 μM (Report DDA-A41-017) was adjusted to Ki of 9 μM using the Cheng-Prusoff equation.
For modeling purposes, all in vitro estimates for the CYP3A4 interaction constants were
corrected by the unbound fraction. These input parameters were evaluated by comparison of
predicted oveporexton plasma concentration-time profiles (visual check) and PK parameters
to observed data after multiple-dose administration, as autoinduction and autoinhibition
374
Reference ID: 5847402

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 220860
Orzeyful (Oveporexton)
were simulated within oveporexton model. The CYP3A4 kinact value was optimized to 1.17
1/h (reduced by 2.6-fold, as described by Mao et al. 2016) to better describe the observed PK
data following 20 mg QD dosing, including accumulation ratio.
Formulation was identified as a covariate affecting oveporexton exposure in the population
PK analysis (Applicant’s population PK report). The formulation T1 resulted in approximately
29% lower Cmax,ss and Cavg,ss compared to the formulation T3, while oveporexton PK following
formulation T2 were comparable to those of T3 (GMRs Cmax,ss and AUC24,ss T3/T2=1.13 and
1.14, respectively) (See Section 6.3.1 for more information). For PBPK modeling, it was
assumed that formulation-dependency in oveporexton exposure was attributed solely to
changes in the fraction absorbed (fa) among formulations and not changes in the rate of
absorption (ka), lag time (tlag) or first-pass metabolism. The formulation with the highest
dose-normalized exposure, T3, was assumed an fa value of 1. Estimates of fa values for T1
and T2 were subsequently calculated based on their dose-normalized exposure relative to T3,
as follows: the fa of T2 was set to 0.897 (T2 exposure, 10 mg SD, Study -1007 relative to T3
exposure for two 2 mg doses Study -1005). The exposure for T1 was 91.7% of the exposure
with the T2 formulation in the comparative BA study (TAK-861-1007). Thus, the initial
estimate of fa for T1 was calculated to be 0.822 (0.917 * 0.897). The fa for T1 was further
optimized to 60% to capture the Day 1 AUC of oveporexton observed for 20 mg oveporexton
T1 in the clinical study TAK-861-1001. The values of ka (1.15 1/h) and tlag (0.6 h) used in the
PBPK model were comparable to the estimates of ka (2.16 1/h) and tlag (0.38 h) from
population PK analysis.
Membrane permeability and P-gp-mediated transport of oveporexton were investigated using
Caco-2 cell monolayers (Report DDA-041-015). The results indicated that oveporexton has
high apparent permeability and does not appear to be a substrate for P-gp.
Results
Performance of PBPK Model To Predict Oveporexton PK
The predictive performance of the PBPK model of oveporexton was evaluated by comparing
predictions to observed PK following single and multiple oral doses of oveporexton in the
clinical studies TAK-861-1001, TAK-861-1007 (T1 formulation, healthy subjects), TAK-8611004 and TAK-861-1005 (T3 formulation, healthy subjects), and TAK-861-2001 and TAK-8612002 (T2 formulation, patients with narcolepsy).
Overall, the predicted values for AUC and Cmax were ≤ ±25% of the observed values, across the
dose range simulated. Predicted and observed PK values are listed in Table 149. After multiple
doses (5 mg QD or 10 mg BID) of oveporexton, the predicted accumulation ratios for AUC24h
and Cmax were ≤±20% of the observed values. The predicted and observed Tmax values
(median) for two doses of 2 mg oveporexton (administered 3 hours apart, as formulation T3,
to healthy subjects) were 4.20 h and 4.50 h, respectively. A comparison of predicted versus
observed plasma concentration-time profiles following single and multiple doses of
oveporexton are shown in Figure 66 and Figure 67.

Simulations were conducted to predict the effect of strong, moderate, and weak CYP3A
inhibitors or inducers on the PK of oveporexton following multiple doses of 0.5 mg BID (3
hours apart) or 2 mg BID (3 hours apart, for 28 days) in healthy subjects. The predicted GMRs
of steady state AUC24h and Cmax values with coadministration of a CYP3A precipitant are listed
in Table 150.
The analysis predicted that coadministration of oveporexton at steady state with a strong
CYP3A inhibitor would result in a strong interaction effect (AUC GMR ≥5). While
coadministration of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor would result in a moderate interaction effect
(oveporexton AUC GMR around 3). No relevant changes on oveporexton exposure were
predicted with coadministration of a weak CYP3A inhibitor (PK change fell within the no
interaction effect boundaries of 80 to 125%). A moderate interaction effect was predicted
with coadministration of with either the strong CYP3A inducer, phenytoin or the moderate
CYP3A inducer, efavirenz (AUC GMR decreased by ≥50 to <80%). A weak interaction effect
was predicted with coadministration of a weak CYP3A inhibitor (AUC GMR ≥1.25 to <2) or
weak CYP3A inducer (AUC GMR decreased by ≥20 to <50%).
The predicted steady state AUC24h and Cmax of oveporexton (formulation T3) in the presence
of CYP3A4 precipitants were compared to the AUC and Cmax of oveporexton dosing of 1 mg or
2 mg BID (3 h apart) alone (Table 151).

Prediction of Drug-Interaction Risk of Oveporexton With a CYP3A4 Substrate
Oveporexton was determined to be an in vitro inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A4. The potential
for clinical interaction with a CYP3A4 substrate was evaluated using the index substrate
midazolam and the oveporexton CYP3A4 inhibition and induction parameters evaluated to
recover oveporexton PK (base model). Sensitivity analysis was also conducted to evaluate the
CYP3A4 inhibition and induction potential independently and uncertainty associated with in
vitro determined parameters. Table 152 lists the predicted changes on the PK of midazolam
(5 mg SD) with co-administration of oveporexton (2 mg BID 3 h apart, Day 14 of 17 days of
dosing of formulation T3 to healthy subjects).
The predicted GMRs of AUCinf and Cmax for midazolam in the presence of oveporexton were
within the no interaction effect boundaries of 80 to 125%, when considering the base model
and optimized induction and inhibition parameters simultaneously or independently.
Sensitivity analysis simulations predicted weak inhibitory effect on midazolam (GMR AUCinf
<2-fold), when considering only inhibition via a CYP3A4 Ki reduced 10-fold and in vitro
CYP3A4 kinact value. Similarly, SA predicted a weak induction effect on midazolam (GMR
AUCinf =0.7) when considering only induction via uncalibrated in vitro Emax value.

1. Background
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to assess the potential carcinogenicity of TAK-861 in rats
and mice. TAK-861 was administered by oral gavage once daily to Sprague-Dawley rats for 24
months and to CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic mice (Tg rasH2 mice) for up to 26 weeks.
In this review the phrase "dose response relationship" refers to the linear component (trend) of the
effect of treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor
incidence rate as dose increases.
2. Rat Study
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. As indicated
in Table 1, in the experiment there were three treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Two
hundred and fourty Sprague-Dawley rats of each sex were assigned randomly in size of 60 rats
per group. TAK-861 was administered in a vehicle of 0.5% (weight-to-volume ratio [w/v])
methylcellulose (MC) solution at 0, 1, 20, and 100 mg/kg by oral gavage once daily for 24
months. In this review the three treated groups were referred to as the low (Group 2), mid (Group
3), and high (Group 4) dose groups, respectively. The rats in the vehicle control group (Group 1)
were administrated with 0.5% (weight-to-volume ratio [w/v]) methylcellulose (MC) solution,
and handled for the same duration and in the same manner as the treated groups.

The following parameters were evaluated in all animals for the evidence of carcinogenicity and
toxicity: mortality, clinical signs including evidence of palpable masses, body weights, food
consumption, hematology, macroscopic observations, and microscopic findings.
2.1.
2.1.1.

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, the survival function was estimated by Kaplan-Meier’s method (product
limit estimator) and graphed (Kaplan-Meier’s curve). The positive trend of survival rate to dose
level was analyzed by Tarone-type method, and then the difference between the vehicle control
group and each TAK-861 group was compared using the log-rank test (level of significance:
0.05). The above analyses were performed by the integrated statistical package, SAS release
9.1.3 (SAS Institute Inc.).

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 4

Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis (page 29) showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal
necropsy were 38 (63%), 32 (53%), 27 (45%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control, low, mid, and
high dose groups, respectively, for male rats, and 29 (48%), 25 (42%), 22 (37%), and 28 (47%) in
the vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for female rats.
Although a statistically significant decrease in survival rate at the end of the dosing period was
recorded in the mid dose group of male rats, it was judged to be incidental because it was not
dose related. No other statistically significant findings were noted in mortality for either male or
female rats.
2.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

In the sponsor’s analysis, for tumors that occurred with high incidence (incidence of 10/sex or
above), positive trend to dose level and pairwise comparison between the vehicle control group
and each TAK-861 group were conducted using the asymptotic survival-adjusted Peto’s method.
For tumors that occurred with low incidence (incidence of less than 10/sex), positive trend to
dose level and pairwise comparison between the vehicle control group and each test article group
were conducted using the Peto’s exact test. Incidental tumors were analyzed in the following
partitions: Week 1 to Week 52, Week 53 to Week 78, Week 79 to Week 92, Week 93 to Week
104, and terminal necropsy period.
Tumors that can occur anywhere in the body were also analyzed for their total incidence over the
entire body. The total incidence of benign/malignant tumors and the tumors that have the same
origin were also analyzed. The above analyses were performed by the integrated statistical
package, SAS Release 9.4 (SAS Institute Inc.) and EXSUS Version 10.2 (CAC Croit
Corporation).
Adjustment for multiple testing:
In the sponsor’s report, analyses of positive trend and pairwise comparison were both performed
at the significance levels of 0.01 (one-tailed) for common tumors and 0.05 (one tailed) for rare
(b) (4)
tumors. Common tumors were defined as those with a historical incidence in controls at
exceeding 1% (>1%) and rare tumors as 1% or less (≤1%).
Sponsor’s findings:
In the sponsor’s report (pages 31, 64, 256), for female rats, the incidence of follicular cell
adenoma of the thyroid gland was statistically significant in the trend test, but not in the pairwise
comparison test. The combined incidence of follicular cell adenoma and carcinoma was not
statistically significant in either the trend or pairwise comparison tests. The incidence of
endometrial stromal polyp and combined tumors (endometrial stromal polyp and sarcoma) was
statistically significant in the trend test, but not in the pairwise comparison test. The incidences
of follicular cell hyperplasia and endometrial stromal hyperplasia did not differ from those in the

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 5

control group. Therefore, the incidences of follicular cell adenoma of the thyroid gland and
endometrial stromal polyp were not considered TAK-861-related.
2.2.

Reviewer's analyses

To verify the sponsor’s analyses and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing
toxicologist, this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses using
the data provided by the sponsor electronically.
2.2.1.

Survival analysis

In the reviewer’s analysis, the survival distributions of rats in all four groups (Groups 1, 2, 3, and 4)
were estimated using the Kaplan-Meier product limit method. The dose response relationship was
tested across Groups 1, 2, 3, and 4 using the likelihood ratio test, and the homogeneity of survival
distributions was tested using the log-rank test.
Reviewer’s findings:
The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates are given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for all
four groups in male and female rats, respectively. The intercurrent mortality data of all four groups
and the results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals for Groups
1, 2, 3, and 4 are given in Tables 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 38 (63%), 32 (53%), 27 (45%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose
groups, respectively, for male rats, and 29 (48%), 25 (42%), 22 (37%), and 28 (47%) in the
vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for female rats.
A statistically significant increase in mortality (p = 0.0304) was observed in the mid dose group
(Group 3) of male rats when compared to the vehicle control group (Group 1), without a
corresponding statistically significant positive dose response relationship. No statistically
significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons in mortality was observed for
female rats.
2.2.2.

Tumor data analysis

The tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationships across the vehicle control, low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups, and pairwise comparisons of each of the three treated groups
against the vehicle control group, using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer and
Portier (1988) and Bieler and Williams (1993).
In the poly-k method, the adjustment for differences in mortality among treatment groups is
made by modifying the number of animals at risk in the denominators in the calculations of
overall tumor rates in the Cochran-Armitage test to reflect less-than-whole-animal contributions
for animals that die without tumor before the end of the study (Bailer and Portier 1988). The
modification is made by defining a new number of animals at risk for each treatment group. The

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 6
*

*

number of animals at risk for the i-th treatment group R i is defined as R i = ∑ W ij where w ij
is the weight for the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and the sum is over all animals in the
group.
Bailer and Portier (1988) proposed the weight w ij as follows:
wij = 1 to animals dying with the tumor, and
wij = ( tij / tsacr )k to animals dying without the tumor,
where tij is the time of death of the j-th animal in the i-th treatment group, and tsacr is the
planned (or intended) time of terminal sacrifice. The above formulas imply that animals living up
to the end of the planned terminal sacrifice date without developing any tumor will also be
assigned wij =1 since tij = tsacr. Also animals developed the tumor type being tested before the
end of the study will be assigned as wij = 1.
Certain treatment groups of a study or the entire study may be terminated earlier than the planned
(or intended) time of terminal sacrifice due to excessive mortalities. However, based on the
principle of the Intention-to-treat (ITT) analysis in randomized trials, the tsacr should not be
affected by the unplanned early terminations. The tsacr should always be equal to the planned (or
intended) time of terminal sacrifice. For those animals that were sacrificed later than tsacr,
regardless their actual terminal sacrifice time, tsacr was used as their time of terminal sacrifice in
the analysis.
One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the appropriate value of k, which depends on the
tumor incidence pattern with the increased dose. For long term 104 week standard rat and mouse
studies, a value of k=3 is suggested in the literature. Hence, this reviewer used k=3 for the analysis
of this data.
Multiple testing adjustment:
For a submission with one two-year study in one species and one short-term study with another
species, in order to keep the overall false-positive rate at the nominal level of approximately 10%,
as indicated in Table 2, this reviewer used the methodologies suggested in the Expanded
Statistical Decision Rules for Interpretations of Results of Rodent Carcinogenicity Studies of
Pharmaceuticals by Lin and Rahman (2018).
A rare tumor is defined as one in which the published spontaneous tumor rate is less than 1%.
However, if the background information for the common or rare tumor is not available, the number
of animals bearing tumors in the vehicle control group in the present study was used to determine
the common or rare tumor status in the review report. That is, if the number of animals bearing
tumors in the vehicle control group is 0, then this tumor is considered as the rare tumor; otherwise,
if the number of animals bearing tumors in the control group is greater than or equal to 1, then this
tumor is considered as the common tumor.

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 7

Table 2. Recommended decision rules for controlling the overall false positive rates

Reviewer’s findings:
The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 2A and 2B in the
appendix for male and female rats, respectively.
Based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed above, for male rats, as
indicated in Table 3, a statistically significant increase (p-value = 0.0477) was noted in medium
dose group in the incidence of adenoma hepatocellular in liver, without the corresponding
statistically significant dose response relationship if this tumor is considered rare. No other
statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons were observed in the
tumor data for either male or female rats.

3. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. As
indicated in Table 4, the male mouse experiment included four treatment groups, one vehicle
control group, and one positive control group. The female mouse experiment included three
treatment groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive control group. A total of 140 male
and 115 female transgenic CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice were randomly assigned to
groups of 25 mice each, except for the positive control groups, which contained 15 mice each.
TAK-861 was administered by oral gavage once daily as a solution in a vehicle at 0, 6, 20, 60,
and 600 mg/kg for males and 0, 3, 30, and 300 mg/kg for females for up to 26 weeks at a dose
volume of 10 mL/kg. In this review, the four treatment groups in male mice are referred to as the
low-dose (Group 2), mid-dose (Group 3), mid-high-dose (Group 4), and high-dose (Group 5)
groups, respectively. The three treatment groups in female mice are referred to as the low-dose
(Group 2), mid-dose (Group 3), and high-dose (Group 4) groups, respectively. The rats in the
vehicle control group (Group 1) were administrated with 0.5% (weight-to-volume ratio [w/v])
methylcellulose (MC) solution, and handled for the same duration and in the same manner as the
treated groups.
In addition, 15 male and female mice were used for the positive control group (N-MethylNnitrosourea (MNU) in a vehicle of citrate buffer solution, single intraperitoneal dose at 75

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 9

mg/kg at a dose volume of 10 mL/kg) to confirm the carcinogenic susceptibility of the Tg rasH2
mice used in this study.
