The following parameters were evaluated in all main study animals for evidence of
carcinogenicity and toxicity: mortality, clinical signs (including observations of palpable masses,
body weight, food consumption, hematology, blood chemistry, macroscopic observations, and
microscopic evaluation of tissues.
3.1.
3.1.1.

Sponsor's analyses

Survival analysis

In the sponsor’s report, the survival function was estimated by Kaplan-Meier’s method (product
limit estimator) and graphed (Kaplan-Meier’s curve). The positive trend of survival rate to dose
level was analyzed by Tarone-type method, and then the difference between the vehicle control
group and each TAK-861 group was compared using log-rank test (level of significance: 0.05).
The above analyses were performed by the integrated statistical package, SAS release 9.1.3 (SAS
Institute Inc.).
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 25 (100%), 24 (96%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control, low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups, respectively, for male mice, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 23 (92%),
and 23 (96%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for
female mice. In the sponsor’s report (page 29), there were no TAK-861-related effects on
survival rate at any dose. In the positive control group (MNU), 15/15 males and 14/15 females
died or were euthanized as moribund during the study period due to neoplasm as expected.
3.1.2.

Tumor data analysis

In the sponor’s report (page 67), positive trend to dose level and pairwise comparison between
the vehicle control group and each TAK-861 group was conducted by Peto’s exact test. Analysis
of positive trend was conducted at the significance level of 0.01 (one tailed-level) for common

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 10

tumors and 0.05 (one tailed-level) for rare tumors. Tumors that can occur anywhere in the body
were also analyzed for their total incidence over the entire body. The total incidence of benign
and malignant tumors was also analyzed. The above analyses were performed by the integrated
statistical package, SAS Release 9.1.3 (SAS Institute Inc.) and EXSUS Version 7.6 (EPS
Corporation).
Multiple testing adjustment:
In the sponsor’s report, analysis of pairwise comparison was conducted at the significance level
of 0.05 (one tailed-level). Common tumors were defined as those with a historical incidence in
(b) (4)
controls at
exceeding 1% (>1%) and rare tumors as 1% or less (≤
1%).
Sponsor’s findings:
In the sponsor’s report (pages 36, 111), there were no TAK-861-related tumors at any dose. In
the positive control group, malignant lymphoma was observed in 13/15 males and 10/15 females,
and papilloma/carcinoma of the squamous cells in the forestomach was observed in 14/15 males
and 14/15 females. Thus, a high susceptibility to a known carcinogen of the test animals used in
this study was confirmed because both tumors were induced at remarkably high incidences..
3.2.

Reviewer's analyses

Similar to the rat study, this reviewer independently performed survival and tumor data analyses of
mouse data to verify sponsor’s analyses. Data used in this reviewer's analyses were provided by the
sponsor electronically.
For the analysis of both the survival data and the tumor data in mice, this reviewer used similar
methodologies that were used for the analyses of the rat survival and tumor data.
3.2.1.

Survival analysis

Reviewer’s findings:
The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival rates are given in Figures 2A and 2B in the appendix for all
four groups in male and female mice, respectively. The intercurrent mortality data of all groups and
the results of the tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals are given in
Tables 3A and 3B in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 25 (100%), 24 (96%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control, low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups, respectively, for male mice, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 23 (92%),
and 23 (96%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for
female mice. No statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons in
mortality was observed for either male or female mice.

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

3.2.2.

Page 11

Tumor data analysis

Reviewer’s findings:
The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types are listed in Tables 4A and 4B in the
appendix for male and female mice, respectively.
In the reviewer’s analysis, based on the criteria of adjustment for multiple testing discussed
above, no statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons in any
tested tumor was noted in either male or female mice. For both male and female mice, the
pairwise compairons between the positive control group (Group 5) and the vehicle control group
(Group 1) showed p-values less than 0.05 for many tumors, confirming animal expressed
oncogenes and responded to carcinogenic insult as expected.
4. Summary
In this submission the sponsor included reports of two animal carcinogenicity studies, one in rats
and one in mice. These studies were to assess the potential carcinogenicity of TAK-861 in rats
and mice. TAK-861 was administered by oral gavage once daily to Sprague-Dawley rats for 24
months and to CByB6F1-Tg(HRAS)2Jic mice (Tg rasH2 mice) for up to 26 weeks.
Rat Study:
Two separate experiments, one in male rats and one in female rats were conducted. In each of the
experiment there were three treated groups, and one vehicle control group. Two hundred and
fourty Sprague-Dawley rats of each sex were assigned randomly in size of 60 rats per group.
TAK-861 was administered in a vehicle of 0.5% (weight-to-volume ratio [w/v]) methylcellulose
(MC) solution at 0, 1, 20, and 100 mg/kg by oral gavage once daily for 24 months.
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of rats surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 38 (63%), 32 (53%), 27 (45%), and 28 (47%) in the vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose
groups, respectively, for male rats, and 29 (48%), 25 (42%), 22 (37%), and 28 (47%) in the
vehicle control, low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for female rats. A statistically
significant increase in mortality (p = 0.0304) was observed in the mid dose group (Group 3) of
male rats when compared to the vehicle control group (Group 1), without a corresponding
statistically significant positive dose response relationship. No statistically significant dose
response relationship or pairwise comparisons in mortality was observed for female rats.
In the reviewer’s analysis, a statistically significant increase (p-value = 0.0477) was noted in the
medium dose group in the incidence of adenoma hepatocellular in liver of male rats, without the
corresponding statistically significant dose response relationship if this tumor is considered rare.
No other statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons were
observed in the tumor data for either male or female rats.
Mouse Study:

Reference ID: 5824736

NDA 220860 (TAK-861)

Page 12

Two separate experiments, one in male mice and one in female mice were conducted. The male
mouse experiment included four treatment groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive
control group. The female mouse experiment included three treatment groups, one vehicle
control group, and one positive control group. A total of 140 male and 115 female transgenic
CByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice were randomly assigned to groups of 25 mice each,
except for the positive control groups, which contained 15 mice each. TAK-861 was
administered by oral gavage once daily as a solution in a vehicle at 0, 6, 20, 60, and 600 mg/kg
for males and 0, 3, 30, and 300 mg/kg for females for up to 26 weeks at a dose volume of 10
mL/kg..
The reviewer’s analysis showed that the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy
were 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 25 (100%), 24 (96%), and 23 (77%) in the vehicle control, low, mid,
mid-high, and high dose groups, respectively, for male mice, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 23 (92%),
and 23 (96%) in the vehicle control group, the low, mid, and high dose groups, respectively, for
female mice. No statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise comparisons in
mortality was observed for either male or female mice.
In the reviewer’s analysis, no statistically significant dose response relationship or pairwise
comparisons in any tested tumor was noted in either male or female mice. For both male and
female mice, the pairwise compairons between the positive control group (Group 5) and the
vehicle control group (Group 1) showed p-values less than 0.05 for many tumors, confirming
animal expressed oncogenes and responded to carcinogenic insult as expected.

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
The applicant, Takeda Pharmaceuticals USA, submitted data for Study TAK-861-1009 in NDA 220860
for the assessment of abuse potential for Oveporexton (TAK-861) (IND 154232). The Study TAK-8611009 was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, active- and placebo-controlled, 5-way
crossover trial evaluating the abuse potential of Oveporexton in healthy adult nondependent recreational
drug users with CNS stimulant experience. A total of 50 subjects were randomized into the treatment
phase and forty-seven (47) participants (94.0%) completed the treatment phase. A total of 43 subjects
(86%) were included in the Modified Completer Population (MCP).
The primary endpoint of this study was the maximum effect (Emax) for Drug Liking (“at this moment”)
of Oveporexton, as assessed by a bipolar (0–100 point) visual analog scale (VAS). The key secondary
endpoints were VAS Emax of Overall Drug Liking, Take Drug Again, and High. Other secondary
endpoints included VAS Emax of Good Drug Effects and Bad Drug Effects, and the occurrence of at
least 1 TEAE during the study including the follow-up period, as applicable. Data for the endpoints of
Any Drug Effects, and the VAS Emax of Alertness/Drowsiness, and time-averaged area under the
effect-time curve [TA_AUE]) are also available.
The reviewer’s primary analysis was conducted on Modified Completers population Set (n=43).
Considering the overall evidence from the data, this reviewer confirmed the following findings:
1. The validation test for comparing the mean difference of Drug Liking VAS Emax between
Phentermine 75 mg and placebo with a test margin of 15 points was statistically significant at
alpha level of 0.05 (1-sided) and the lower 95% confidence limit (one-sided) of the mean
difference was 20.8, supporting the study validity.
2. For the relative abuse potential of Oveporexton at each dose in this study compared to
Phentermine 75 mg with a margin of 0 point, the data rejected the hypothesis of the mean Emax
score of Drug Liking VAS in subjects receiving Phentermine 75 mg being less than or equal to
those receiving Oveporexton 6 mg at alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), suggesting that the subjects
in this study prefer Phentermine 75 mg to Oveporexton 6 mg. However, the data failed to reject
the null hypothesis of the relative abuse potential of the higher doses of Oveporexton (18 mg, or
30 mg) compared to Phentermine 75 mg with p-value > 0.05 (1-sided), suggesting that the
subjects in this study liked Oveporexton no less than Phentermine 75 mg.
Furthermore, analyses of the key and other secondary endpoints showed consistent patterns as
seen in the relative abuse potential analysis of the primary analysis.
Since only the low dose of Oveporexton demonstrated a significant difference relative to
Phentermine 75 mg while the analysis failed to reject the null hypothesis at the medium and high
doses of the test drug, this is sufficient to draw the conclusion about the potential for abuse of
Oveporexton. Therefore, the analysis of absolute abuse potential of Oveporexton would be
exploratory.
3. Exploratory analysis: At alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), there was no sufficient evidence to
reject the null hypothesis of the mean Emax of Drug Liking VAS to the treatment of each
Oveporexton dose is higher than that of placebo by 11 points, suggesting that the subjects in the
treatment phase more like Oveporexton than placebo.
4

Reference ID: 5806728

In summary, the data suggested that Oveporexton has higher abuse potential than placebo when
assessed on the mean Emax score of Drug Liking VAS and other secondary endpoints. Relative to
the positive control treatment, data from both the primary and secondary endpoints support that
Oveporexton at either 18 mg or 30 mg has no less potential for abuse than Phentermine 75 mg, while
Oveporexton 6 mg has a lower abuse potential than Phentermine 75 mg.

2. INTRODUCTION
2.1

Overview

Oveporexton (TAK-861) is developed for the treatment of narcolepsy with cataplexy (NT1), a rare
neurological disorder characterized by EDS and associated rapid eye movement phenomena including
cataplexy, hypnagogic/hypnopompic hallucinations, sleep paralysis, and disturbed nighttime sleep.
This study had been conducted to assess the relative abuse potential of TAK-861 in healthy adult
nondependent recreational drug users with central nervous system (CNS) stimulant experience.
The primary purpose of this study was to evaluate the abuse potential of oveporexton, in comparison to
placebo and an active control with Phentermine, which is a Schedule IV controlled substance.

3. REVIEW REPORT on STUDY TAK-861-1009
3.1 STUDY OBJECTIVES
Primary Objective:
To evaluate the relative abuse potential of oveporexton as measured by the VAS for Drug Liking (“at
this moment”).
Key Secondary Objectives:
To evaluate the relative abuse potential of oveporexton as measured by the VAS for Overall Drug
Liking, Take Drug Again, and High.
Other Secondary Objectives:
• To evaluate the relative abuse potential of oveporexton as measured by the VAS for Bad Drug Effects
and Good Drug Effects.
• To evaluate the safety/tolerability of oveporexton as measured by TEAE incidence.
3.2 STUDY DESIGN
This was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, active- and placebo-controlled, 5-way
crossover trial evaluating the abuse potential of oveporexton in healthy adult nondependent recreational
drug users with CNS stimulant experience.
An overview of the study design is presented in Figure 1.

6

Reference ID: 5806728

Figure 1 Study Design Schematic

Qualification phase: 2 period, 2 sequence cross-over with 5-day washout before treatment period.
Treatment phase: 5-period, 10 sequence cross-over.
Treatment A: Oveporexton 6 mg.
Treatment B: Oveporexton 18 mg.
Treatment C: Oveporexton 30 mg.
Treatment D: Phentermine (active comparator) 75 mg.
Treatment E: Placebo.

Qualification Phase
Eligible participants underwent screening (within 28 days before dosing) and an inpatient
qualification phase (Day –1 through Day 3) to determine their ability to discriminate the drug
effects of phentermine 75 mg versus placebo and tolerate phentermine.
• Treatment QA: 75 mg of phentermine
• Treatment QP: matching placebo
Participants will be required to meet all of the following qualification criteria to be eligible to enter the
treatment phase:
1. The participant continues to meet all of the inclusion criteria and none of the exclusion criteria.
2. The participant satisfactorily completes the qualification phase, is able to tolerate phentermine, and
general behavior suggests that the participant could successfully complete the trial, as judged by the
investigational site staff.
3. On the assessment of drug liking (“at the moment” Drug Liking VAS):
a) The participant’s Emax in response to phentermine is numerically ≥15 points higher than that of
placebo, and the participant has an Emax ≥65 points with phentermine.
b) The participant has an acceptable placebo response, defined as an Emax between 40 and 60 points
inclusive (that is, a score considered neutral on the scale, meaning neither like nor dislike).
4. The participant has acceptable overall responses to phentermine and placebo on the VAS measures, as
judged by the investigational site staff.
After the qualification phase, eligible participants who meet these qualification criteria will remain
confined until the end of the treatment phase. The last trial intervention administration in the
7

Reference ID: 5806728

qualification phase and the first trial intervention administration in the treatment phase will be separated
by a washout interval of at least 5 days, exceeding 5 half-lives of phentermine (~20 hours).
Treatment Phase
The treatment phase is based on a 5-treatment, 10-sequence, 5-period Williams square crossover design.
Treatments were:
• Treatment A: Oveporexton 6 mg
• Treatment B: Oveporexton 18 mg
• Treatment C: Oveporexton 30 mg
• Treatment D: Phentermine 75 mg (active comparator)
• Treatment E: placebo
Participants received each treatment as a single oral dose in randomized sequence (double-dummy).
Each treatment period was separated by a ≥7-day washout based on the oveporexton half-life (16–28
hours). PD, PK, and safety assessments were performed up to 24 hours postdose.
Approximately 50 qualified participants will be randomized equally to the 10 sequences in the treatment
phase with the intent to ensure that at least 40 participants are in the modified completer analysis set.
For the Day 43 follow-up visit, a minimum of 3 documented telephone calls must be attempted to
contact any participant (or their legally authorized representative) for the last scheduled visit.
Participants will be considered as having completed the trial if they successfully complete the Treatment
phase and are discharged from the site.
The Schedule of assessments is presented in Table 1 and Table 2 for different Phases, respectively.

3.3 Statistical Methodologies of the Sponsor
Primary endpoint:
• Maximum effect (Emax) for Drug Liking (“at this moment”) as assessed by a bipolar VAS (0-100
points).
Key secondary endpoints:
• Overall Drug Liking (Emax), assessed by a bipolar VAS (0-100 points).
• Take Drug Again (Emax), assessed "overall" by a bipolar VAS (0-100 points).
• High (Emax), assessed "at this moment" by a unipolar VAS (0-100 points).
Other secondary endpoints:
• Bad Drug Effects (Emax) assessed "at this moment" by a unipolar VAS (0-100 points).
• Good Drug Effects (Emax) assessed "at this moment" by a unipolar VAS (0-100 points).
• Occurrence of at least 1 TEAE during the study including the follow-up period, as applicable.
9

Reference ID: 5806728

3.3.1 Sample Size Determination
Assuming the true difference in Drug-Liking VAS peak score (maximum effect, Emax) between
phentermine 75 mg and placebo is 25, and the true standard deviation of within participant differences is
21, it was estimated that, in the treatment phase, a sample size of 40 participants in the modified
completers analysis set would provide at least 90% power to test each hypothesis for the primary PD
endpoint.

3.3.2 Analysis population
Randomized Analysis Set
The Randomized Analysis Set consisted of all participants who were randomized in the Treatment Phase.
Safety Set
The Safety Analysis Set consisted of all participants who received at least 1 dose of trial intervention in
the Treatment phase.
Completer Set
The Completer Analysis Set consisted of all participants in the Randomized Analysis Set who completed
all treatment periods of the Treatment Phase and had at least 1 response on the VAS for Drug Liking “at
this moment” within 2 hours postdose for each treatment.
Stat comment: This definition of a treatment completer is problematic. The correct definition is that a
completer should have at least 1 observation within 2 hours of the median Tmax for each active
treatment and within 4-hours post dose for placebo for Drug Liking VAS.
Modified Completer Set
The Modified Completer Analysis Set consisted of all participants in the completer analysis set,
excluding those whose Drug Liking “at this moment” VAS Emax scores meet any one of the
elimination criteria:
•
•
•

Similar Emax scores (within a 5-points difference) for a participant across all trial treatments
(including placebo)
Emax(phentermine) ≤55
Emax (placebo) - Emax (phentermine) ≥ 5

3.3.3 Statistical Analysis
Primary Analysis
Analysis of the primary endpoint will be performed on the modified completer analysis set. A linear
mixed effects model containing treatment, period, sequence, and first-order carryover (if applicable) as
fixed effects, and participant as random effect, is planned to be used to evaluate the hypotheses for the
primary endpoint. The Kenward-Roger method for calculating denominator degrees of freedom will be
used.
Prior to applying the model, homogeneity of variances across treatments will be tested using a restricted
likelihood ratio test. If the test is statistically significant at the 0.05 level, variances will be considered
heterogeneous. Otherwise, variances will be considered homogeneous.
10

Reference ID: 5806728

If the variances are homogeneous, the model will constrain variances to be equal across treatment
groups. If variances are heterogeneous, the model will allow unequal variances across treatment groups.
After determining whether the treatment variance is homogeneous or heterogeneous, the residuals from
the model will be investigated for normality using the Shapiro-Wilk W-test. If the test has p-value
<0.05, the residuals will be considered not normally distributed. Otherwise, the residuals will be
considered normally distributed.
If the residuals are normally distributed, it will be determined whether the carryover effects should be
included in the model. Carryover effects are defined as the effect of the treatment administered in the
previous treatment period. If the carryover effect is found to be significant at a level of 0.25, then it will
be included in the mixed-effects model as a fixed effect. Otherwise, carryover effect will be dropped
from the model.
If the residuals are not normally distributed, the distribution of the paired difference for each contrast
will be examined in terms of normality and skewness. Each paired difference will be investigated for
normality using the Shapiro-Wilk W-test. If the test has p-value ≥0.05, the paired difference will be
considered normally distributed, and a paired t-test will be used for analysis. If the paired difference is
not normally distributed, that is, the W-test has p-value <0.05, the following criteria on skewness will be
used to determine whether the paired t-test or sign test is used for testing:
a) If the alternative hypothesis is upper-tailed, and skewness is [0, 0.5], the paired t-test will be used.
b) If the alternative hypothesis is upper-tailed, and skewness <0 or skewness >0.5, the sign test will
be used.
c) If the alternative hypothesis is lower-tailed, and skewness is [-0.5, 0], the paired t-test will be
used.
d) If the alternative hypothesis is lower-tailed, and skewness <-0.5 or skewness >0, the sign test will
be used.
e) If the alternative hypothesis is two-tailed, and skewness is [-0.5, 0.5], the paired t-test will be
used.
f) If the alternative hypothesis is two-tailed, and skewness <-0.5 or skewness >0.5, the sign test will
be used.
If the sign test is used for analysis, the median of the paired difference, first quartile (Q1), third quartile
(Q3), 1-sided 95% confidence interval (CI), and p-value from the sign test will be displayed (Hollander
and Wolfe 1999). Note that the p-value of the sign test excludes paired differences in scores that are
equal to zero after subtracting the margin from the differences. The calculation of the CI does not
exclude any paired differences (Hollander and Wolfe 1999). The conclusions will be based on p-values.
If the paired t-test is used for analysis, the mean difference, SE, 1-sided 95% CI, and p-value from the ttest will be displayed.
If a mixed effects model is used for analysis, the least squares mean difference, SE, 1-sided 95% CI, and
p-value from the contrast test will be displayed.

3.3.4 Hypothesis testing
Primary Endpoint Analysis
11

Reference ID: 5806728

• Positive control phentermine (C) vs placebo (P)
Does phentermine (C) produce mean responses that show a greater abuse potential compared
to placebo (P)?
Ho: μC – μP ≤ 15 vs. Ha: μC – μP > 15, δ1 =15

(1)

where μC and μP are the mean Drug Liking VAS Emax for phentermine and placebo, respectively. The
margin of 15 aligns with previous studies of this type (Braeckman et al. 2019; Shram et al. 2022).
• Test drug TAK-861 (T) vs positive control phentermine (C)
Does TAK-861 produce mean responses that show less abuse potential compared to positive
control, phentermine (C)?
Ho: μC – μT ≤ δ2 vs. Ha: μC – μT > δ2, δ2 =0

(2)

where μC and μT are the mean Drug Liking VAS Emax for phentermine and TAK-861, respectively.
This hypothesis will be tested separately for each TAK-861 dose. Rejection of each null hypothesis
would suggest lower relative abuse potential of TAK-861 compared to phentermine.
The evaluation of absolute abuse potential of OVEPOREXTON will be the comparison of
OVEPOREXTON versus placebo. The following hypothesis will be tested for each dose level of
OVEPOREXTON compared to placebo:
1. A: OVEPOREXTON dose 1 vs. D: placebo
2. B: OVEPOREXTON dose 2 vs. D: placebo
• Test drug TAK-861 (T) vs placebo (P)
Does TAK-861 produce mean responses that show similar abuse potential compared to placebo (P)?
Ho: μT – μP ≥ δ3 vs. Ha: μT – μP < δ3 , δ3 =11

(3)

where μT and μP are the mean Drug Liking VAS Emax for placebo and TAK-861, respectively. This
hypothesis will be tested separately for each TAK-861 dose. Rejection of each null hypothesis would
suggest that TAK-861 does not produce an abuse-related signal. The margin of 11 was selected based
on Chen and Boson (Chen and Bonson 2013). Assuming the true difference in Drug-Liking VAS peak
score (maximum effect, Emax) between phentermine 75 mg and placebo is 25, and the true standard
deviation of within-participant differences is 21, a total of 40 participants in the modified completers
analysis set is needed to ensure at least 90% power with a margin of 15 and an upper-tailed test with
significance level of 0.05, using a paired t-test.
The above hypotheses will be tested sequentially, and no adjustments will be made for multiplicity.
Secondary Endpoints
The following hypotheses will be evaluated for key and other secondary endpoints. Comparisons among
treatments will be evaluated at 1-sided significance level 0.05 for hypothesis 1 and 2, and 2-sided
significance level 0.10 for hypothesis 3.
Hypothesis 1, Positive control (phentermine) (C) versus placebo (P):
12

Reference ID: 5806728

H0: μC - μP ≤ 0 vs. Ha: μC - μP > 0
Hypothesis 2, Positive control (phentermine) (C) versus each dose of TAK-861 (T):
H0: μC − μT ≤ 0 vs. Ha: μC − μT > 0
Hypothesis 3, Each dose of TAK-861 (T) versus placebo (P):
H0: μT − μP = 0 vs. Ha: μT − μP ≠ 0
No adjustments to p-values will be made. All hypotheses will be evaluated using nominal pvalues.
Key and other secondary PD endpoints will be analyzed with the same linear mixed effects model or
nonparametric approaches (as appropriate) as the primary endpoint analysis, using the modified
completer analysis set and following the same strategy for determining the analysis method. If a PD
endpoint is based on a VAS that has a baseline (pre-dose) measurement, the mixed effects model for that
endpoint will include baseline as a covariate.
Changes in Planned Analysis
No changes were made to the planned analyses described in SAP Version 1.
3.5 Disposition of Subjects
In the qualification phase, a total of 125 participants were randomized and all received at least 1
dose of trial intervention. Fifty (50) participants (40.0%) were eligible for the treatment phase
upon completing the qualification phase, while 75 (60%) participants did not meet the criteria to
continue into the treatment phase. The most common reason for discontinuation was failure to
meet qualification criteria (38.4%), followed by protocol-specified withdrawal criteria (15.2%).
Other reasons included withdrawal of consent (5 participants, 4.0%), physician decision (2
participants, 1.6%), and failure to meet continuation criteria (1 participant, 0.8%) (Table 3).
The 50 participants who completed the qualification phase were randomized to treatment
sequences in the Treatment Phase, and all (100%) received at least 1 dose of trial intervention.
Forty-seven (47) participants (94.0%) completed the treatment phase, and 3 participants (6.0%)
discontinued. Reasons for discontinuation were withdrawal of consent (2 participants, 4.0%) and
other reasons (1 participant, 2.0%). No discontinuations occurred due to AEs. No participants were
randomized but not treated (Table 4).

The randomized analysis set included 50 participants, most of whom were male (88.0%) and Black or
African American (60.0%). The mean (SD) age was 35.0 (6.66) years, and the mean (SD) BMI was

3.6 Sponsor’s Pharmacodynamic Summary and Conclusions
• Primary Endpoint (Drug Liking Emax):
– The positive control phentermine showed greater drug liking compared to placebo, thereby
confirming trial validity.
– At the 6 mg dose of oveporexton, Emax for Drug Liking “at this moment” was significantly lower
than phentermine, supporting lower drug liking relative to active control. Supratherapeutic doses
(18 and 30 mg) did not demonstrate abuse potential less than that of phentermine. None of the
oveporexton doses showed abuse potential similar to placebo.
•

Secondary Endpoints (Overall Drug Liking, Take Drug Again, “High” VAS):
– Phentermine demonstrated significantly greater effects than placebo on key secondary endpoints,
further demonstrating the sensitivity of the endpoints and trial.
– At 6 mg, oveporexton consistently demonstrated lower effects than phentermine for all 3 key
secondary endpoints (Overall Drug Liking, Take Drug Again and High VAS).
– At higher doses (18 and 30 mg), oveporexton were similar to those of phentermine, with no
statistically significant differences.

•

Additional Subjective Measures (Good/Bad Drug Effects, Any Drug Effects, Alertness/Drowsiness):
– Results for Good Drug Effects were similar to those of High VAS.
– Bad Drug Effects were minimal with phentermine and oveporexton doses and comparable to
placebo.
– Dose-related increases were observed for Any Drug Effects and Alertness/Drowsiness, with higher
oveporexton doses showing effects comparable to phentermine.

•

Drug Similarity VAS: Phentermine and oveporexton were both rated as having similarity to
stimulants.

Stat comment: The statement regarding the validity of the study is valid only for the analysis based on
the primary endpoint, but for the secondary endpoints, the validity of this statement may be uncertain
due to the unadjusted margins used in the analyses.
3.7 Reviewer’s Assessment
The statistical analysis by this reviewer used the modified completer population (MCP).
There was one subject receiving oveporexton 6 mg with missing visit at hour 1 in the Treatment Phase.
This missing outcome occurred within two hours of Tmax for determining the Emax score of drug liking
VAS (the primary endpoint), therefore, they could affect the primary analysis.

Overall Drug Liking VAS (Figure 3) and Take Drug Again VSA (Figure 4), High VAS (Figure 5), Good
Effects VAS (Figure 6), Bad Effects VAS (Figure 7), Any Effects VAS (Figure 8), Alertness/Drowsiness
VAS (Figure 9), show that the mean responses to different measurements peaked around 2 to 3 hours after
treatment administration, and the peak time for Oveporexton came earlier than for Phentermine 75 mg.

3.7.2 Statistical Testing
This reviewer’s analysis was first carried out using the mixed-effects model proposed by the sponsor,
including treatment, sequence, period and first-order carryover as fixed effects, and subject as a random
effect. The assumption of normality for the mixed-effects model was met in either the primary endpoint or
the key secondary endpoint Feeling High, but not in the Overall Drug liking and Take Drug Again, as
listed in Table 7 at a significant level of 0.05 using Shapiro-Wilk W-test. The null and alternative
hypotheses for the normality analysis are:
H0: Distribution of residuals is normal
Ha: Distribution of residuals is not normal
Table 7 also shows the skewness and p-values of the Shapiro-Wilk W-test for the residual distribution in
the mixed-effects model on other secondary endpoints. The endpoints with p-value >0.05 from the
normality test were analyzed using the mixed-effects model. The analysis of the mixed-effects model on
the primary endpoint showed no concern regarding the carryover effects (p-value=0.1487).

For the endpoints whose p-values of the Shapiro-Wilk W-test for the residual distribution in the mixedeffects model <0.05, the distribution of the paired difference for each contrast were examined in terms of
normality and skewness using the Shapiro-Wilk W-test. Provided N=43 for MCP and based on the
normality tests, this reviewer used t-test analysis (one-sample t-test) on paired differences for each
contrast, either with a p-value of the normality test >0.05 or those with a p-value <0.05 but satisfying a
skewness within [-0.5, 0.5] (ref. Linsen Liu, 2024). Otherwise, the non-parametric sign test was used on
those paired differences.
The results of the primary analysis are summarized in Table 8 at a significance level of 0.05 (1-sided)
using the sponsor preselected margins for these tests. The null hypothesis of the validation test was
rejected with a highly significant level of p-value<.0001. The lower 95% confidence limit of the median
difference is 20.8 (see Table 8), suggesting that the median difference between Phentermine 75 mg and
placebo is more than 15 points. This analysis supports the study validity.
For testing whether the abuse potential of the positive control Phentermine 75 mg is no higher than the
test drug Oveporexton at each planned dose at least 0 point, the data failed to reject the null hypothesis
of the relative abuse potential for each Oveporexton dose, except for Oveporexton 6 mg, compared to
27

Reference ID: 5806728

Phentermine 75 mg with p-value > 0.05 (1-sided). As seen in Table 8 for Drug Liking VAS Emax, the
lower 95% confidence limit (1-sided) of Phentermine 75 mg – OVEPOREXTON 6 mg was 5.1,
suggesting the difference greater than the pre-specified margin of 0 point by the subjects in the treatment
phase; while that for Phentermine 75 mg – OVEPOREXTON 18 mg was -1.1, and for Phentermine 75
mg – OVEPOREXTON 30 mg was -5.8, respectively, suggesting the difference less than the prespecified margin of 0 point by the subjects in the treatment phase.
Since only the low dose of Oveporexton demonstrated a significant difference relative to Phentermine
75 mg while the analysis failed to reject the null hypothesis at the medium and high doses of the test
drug, this is sufficient to draw the conclusion about the potential for abuse of Oveporexton. Therefore,
the analysis of absolute abuse potential of Oveporexton would be exploratory.
At a level of 0.05 (one-sided), there was no sufficient evidence to reject the null hypothesis of the mean
Emax of Drug Liking VAS to the treatment of each Oveporexton dose is higher than that of placebo by
11 points, suggesting that the subjects in the treatment phase more like Oveporexton than placebo.

Table 9 summarized the results of the key secondary endpoints analysis. The results of other secondary
endpoints analysis are summarized in Table 10. The analyses were carried out using a margin of 0 for all
these analyses, either 1-sided or 2-sided at the significance level of 0.05. Since the margins used for
testing the validation and absolute abuse potential by the sponsor were not adjusted to align with the
corresponding margins of the primary analysis, the validity of these results on the secondary endpoints
remains uncertain as to whether they support the findings of the primary analysis.
For the hypothesis test of the relative abuse potential, the Drug Liking VAS TEmax analyses of all
secondary endpoints showed consistent patterns for relative abuse potential between the positive control
Phentermine 75 mg and each corresponding dose of Oveporexton:

28

Reference ID: 5806728

•

the data rejected the null hypothesis of the relative abuse potential of Oveporexton 6 mg
compared to Phentermine 75 mg with a p-value < 0.05 (one-tailed), suggesting that the subjects
in this study prefer Phentermine 75 mg to Oveporexton 6 mg;

•

the data failed to reject the null hypothesis of the relative abuse potential of Oveporexton (18 mg,
or 30 mg) compared to Phentermine 75 mg with p-value > 0.05 (1-sided), suggesting that the
subjects’ preference for Oveporexton (18 mg, or 30 mg) is not less than their preference for
Phentermine 75 mg.

The Drug Liking Emax analysis for the completers population was consistent with that of the

4. SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
4.1 Statistical Issues
There was one subject receiving oveporexton 6 mg with missing visit at hour 1 in the Treatment
Phase. This missing outcome in responses to drug liking VAS occurred within two hours of Tmax to
determine the corresponding Emax, that might affect the primary analysis of this treatment. However, it
was not sufficient to change the conclusion.
4.2 Conclusions and Recommendations
The reviewer’s primary analysis was conducted on Modified Completers population Set (n=43).
Considering the overall evidence from the data, this reviewer confirmed the following findings:
1. The validation test for comparing the mean difference of Drug Liking VAS Emax between
Phentermine 75 mg and placebo with a test margin of 15 points was statistically significant at
alpha level of 0.05 (1-sided) and the lower 95% confidence limit (one-sided) of the mean
difference was 20.8, supporting the study validity.
33

Reference ID: 5806728

2. For the relative abuse potential of Oveporexton at each dose in this study compared to
Phentermine 75 mg with a margin of 0 point, the data rejected the hypothesis of the mean Emax
score of Drug Liking VAS in subjects receiving Phentermine 75 mg being less than or equal to
those receiving Oveporexton 6 mg at alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), suggesting that the subjects
in this study prefer Phentermine 75 mg to Oveporexton 6 mg. However, the data failed to reject
the null hypothesis of the relative abuse potential of the higher doses of Oveporexton (18 mg, or
30 mg) compared to Phentermine 75 mg with p-value > 0.05 (1-sided), suggesting that the
subjects in this study liked Oveporexton no less than Phentermine 75 mg.

Furthermore, analyses of the key and other secondary endpoints showed consistent patterns as
seen in the relative abuse potential analysis of the primary analysis.
Since only the low dose of Oveporexton demonstrated a significant difference relative to
Phentermine 75 mg while the analysis failed to reject the null hypothesis at the medium and high
doses of the test drug, this is sufficient to draw the conclusion about the potential for abuse of
Oveporexton. Therefore, the analysis of absolute abuse potential of Oveporexton would be
exploratory.
3. Exploratory analysis: At alpha level of 0.05 (one-sided), there was no sufficient evidence to
reject the null hypothesis of the mean Emax of Drug Liking VAS to the treatment of each
Oveporexton dose is higher than that of placebo by 11 points, suggesting that the subjects in the
treatment phase more like Oveporexton than placebo.
In summary, the data suggested that Oveporexton has higher abuse potential than placebo when
assessed on the mean Emax score of Drug Liking VAS and other secondary endpoints. Relative to
the positive control treatment, data from both the primary and secondary endpoints support that
Oveporexton at either 18 mg or 30 mg has no less potential for abuse than Phentermine 75 mg, while
Oveporexton 6 mg has a lower abuse potential than Phentermine 75 mg.
